Chapter 1 
: Introduction and Overview 
1.1 Introduction 
This chapter explores the research overview of the historical development of the study, the 
background and research real-world problems leading to what the research is all about, a brief 
discussion on study concepts like the systems reinforcing model (SRM), energy culture (EC) 
and sustainable energy access (SEA) framework’s that is contextual to the development of the 
new Systems Energy Technology Innovation Living Lab (SETILL) theoretical model focused 
on gender design for the research study. The research aims, objectives, and research questions 
were also presented for suitable guidance on what the research study intended to accomplish. 
A brief overview of the study's relevance and justification, as well as its methodology, is 
included to help guide the research design. Thereafter, the chosen technology to be used for 
the testing or implementation of the SETILL is discussed briefly. Generally, this chapter 
explores this study for contextual applicability to the topic to develop a new Systems Energy 
Technology Innovation Living Lab (SETILL) focused on gender design and to identify the 
processes that can guide the exploratory theory.  
 
1.2 The overview of the historical development of the research study 
Energy poverty conditions are mostly prevalent in poor rural and urban African informal 
settlement households, most informal settlement households make use of localised energy 
sources but still lack access to clean energy sources and modern energy services (Okoye, 2020). 
Therefore, ‘energy insecurity’ which can also be called ‘energy poverty’ in the context of this 
research study, is challenging and the issues have been generally supported in literature and 
policies in the public domain (Balmer, 2007; McCollum et al, 2017; Sadath and Acharya, 
2017). The highest rate of energy insecurity is in Africa with more than 80% of fuel 
consumption being paraffin (high carbon emitting fuel), biomass, and municipal industrial and 
animal wastes (UNEP, 2017). In developing countries, billions of individuals are unable to 
access clean cooking sources and electricity (Sokona, Mulugetta and Gujba, 2012), and the 
United Nations (2014) indicated that by 2030, over 2.5 million individuals could be unable to 
access clean modern sources of energy for cooking. Therefore, this research study looked in 
the direction of developing a modern energy source for cooking with women as the end-users 
with the Living Lab through a bottom-up approach which will be validated and tested. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 2 
 
Generally, energy poverty challenges are socio-economical, and usually found in poor 
households in urban or rural environments mostly in developing countries. Simply put, energy 
poverty could be referred to as the unavailability of clean energy and energy services in 
households and beyond. Okoye (2020:1) stated that “one of the factors of promoting energy 
poverty mostly comes from socio-cultural environments, which are likely to shape energy use 
patterns and behaviours in households”. However, energy poverty is not only the deficiency of 
access to energy and modern energy services. Thus, there should be no need to attempt to 
reduce energy poverty by means of mere technical concepts and numerical metrics, socially 
and culturally, but of rights and justice, of transformation as a capacity and for gender as an 
economics. This is because the complexities of addressing energy insecurity within the 
contested geography of the informal settlements in Africa remains both gendered and 
persistently understudied and underdeveloped (Musango et al., 2020; Anditi et al., 2022). In 
addition, the following is an argument that decreasing energy insecurity can decrease the 
poverty rate through economic development, sustainability, and human involvement in the 
informal settlement (Kohler, Rhodes & Vermaak, 2014). First of all, concerning the 
enhancement of modern energy services with an emphasis on cooking, the decisions of the 
designs and promotion of environmentally friendly energy technology are off policy making 
and should be linked to the energy users or end-users in order to increase the systems and 
mechanisms regarding energy access and the consumption/uses of energy resources. For a fact, 
and as much as the South African government has attempted to introduce energy pro-poor 
policies since 1998 into the households of the informal settlements, the nation still lack clean 
energy. Nakhooda (2012) states that fifty percent of the nation has no access to clean energy 
sources (SEA, 2014). 
In one capacity or the other, research studies have either individually or in groups considered 
policies on energy and have made great signs of progress and or proffer recommendations 
(Mohlakoana, 2014; Kelly and Geyer, 2018; Okoye, 2020). Other studies have also provided 
an overview of energy services achievements concerning gender mainstreaming and creating 
living labs towards energy security (Musango et al., 2020; Mukama et al., 2022). However, to 
our knowledge, no research study in South Africa has been identified to have promoted energy 
security in the Living Lab by developing energy technology innovation to a pilot scale to 
improve energy access and clean cooking energy services with a gender design using the 
systems approach. Therefore, this research study would study policies, conceptualise the 
Living Lab, gender inequality, energy technology, energy security, how human factors affect 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 3 
 
technology, and develop and test energy technology to a pilot scale through the Living Lab 
with a systems approach to improve access to energy services in South Africa.  
In addition, in the decisions within the households, a study on the factors regarding payments 
models on clean cooking in the Kenyan informal settlement revealed that although 83% of 
women occupying the informal settlement affirm they cook for their households and 59% of 
the women participate in the process when decisions are being made within the households. 
Yet another probable study done in Ethiopia revealed that in so many households at the 
informal settlements, where women are the head of or leading, men have the least control of 
the expenses of the households (Mengistu et al., 2016). This at times creates cross gender inter 
connection and affiliations. Other proximate household energy studies in the similar region of 
South Africa concerning the acceptance of solar water heaters, liquid petroleum gas studies in 
Ghana and Cameroon and a study in Rwanda concerning an upgraded cookstove, observed that 
increases in acceptance of innovations were a result of women’s leadership or participation and 
female education (Grant et al., 2021). Consequently, this study will enhance the involvement 
of women in the co-design, co-creation, and co-testing of the energy technology innovation to 
be developed in the informal settlement through the systems living lab, on enhancing the 
adoption level of energy innovations and getting more inputs from end-users in as far as 
addressing the security of the energy services in Africa is concerned. In addition to this, it is 
central to the growth of the adoption of energy innovation to empower women wholly (Anditi 
et al., 2022). 
Through the 1994 electrification program (DoE, 2015), most households in the informal 
settlement have access to electricity or energy sources. While they have access to electricity, it 
does not stop the households in the informal settlement from making use of other energy 
sources like charcoal and paraffin to achieve energy needs such as cooking or lighting (Okoye, 
2020). This indicates that the households in the informal settlement practice or use several 
energy sources such as electricity for heating, paraffin and others at the same time (Mdluli and 
Vogel, 2010). This may not be due to financial implications, but related to sociocultural 
tendencies. According to Okoye (2020:2), the World Health Organisation (WHO) “considers 
energy access as a percentage of people using electricity whilst integrating other energy 
sources”. This research study does not take into consideration the particular energy sources 
used in the informal settlements used as the case study or the costs involved but simply 
proposes a technology or energy source that is accepted by the end-users to test the theoretical 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 4 
 
model to be developed. Thus, the study will use the SRM-EC and SEA research approaches to 
determine the energy use patterns, behaviour, and norms for future prevention of energy 
insecurity and potentially apply the interventions to co-design the energy technology 
innovations with the selected poor informal settlements, whereby understanding the 
mechanisms that affect the input of knowledge and participation of women in decision-making 
processes for contributing to the adoption of energy innovations. In regards to this study, the 
identified informal settlements were the primary and nearly carbon copy equivalent studies of 
energy-starved South Africa communities that could also stand for other communities in Africa 
that are plagued with the same predicaments. 
Therefore, if there will be an upgrade to energy access in the households of the informal 
settlements in 20 years, the Sustainable Energy Access (SEA) (2014:10) stated that “a 
deepened engagement with every society and communities in energy-poverty matters and 
decision-making is crucial and urgently needed at the local level, and it is important to develop 
an integrated energy poverty policy/framework for the country”. Furthermore, the SEA (2014) 
made a proposal or recommendations to develop a ‘management framework’ to reduce energy 
poverty by the use of a systems approach. 
Hence, the systems approach concepts which Biahmou (2015), Buede, D. M. and Miller, (2016) 
identified are used to study energy poverty reduction by Sullivan and Howden-chapman, 
(2017) and Karásek and Pojar, (2018) to formulate the theoretical model of this research study. 
For this reason the research theoretical model is dubbed the Systems Energy Technology 
Innovation Living Lab (SETILL) with an ascribe to gender for energy security. Furthermore, 
within this study, the systems approach was applied to identify the drivers for women’s 
engagement and the decision-making processes for co-design and testing of energy technology 
innovations and how the mechanisms can be optimised with regard to the level of adoption and 
acceptance of energy innovations and end-users through the development of a systems 
dynamics model as part of the Living Lab process. Therefore, a design process in energy 
poverty and access to clean cooking energy sources through systems approach like the systems 
engineering and systems thinking may contribute to minimization of energy poverty and 
improvement of Energy Assess Sustainability (Okoye, 2020). This is because the SE and SsT 
concepts were termed useful in the reduction of energy poverty. 
Various research studies have been done to apply the SsT concepts in the sector of public 
health, sustainability of wind energy (Homer and Hirsch, 2006; Mutale et al., 2015; Mph et al., 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 5 
 
2018), processes of technology transfer (Tejeda and Ferreira, 2014), renewable technologies 
(Brent and Kruger, 2009), the teaching of climate change (Roychoudhury et al., 2017), 
understanding of disaster risks reduction in informal settlements as cited by Onyeagoziri, Shaw 
and Ryan, 2021 and assessment of gendered energy transition (Musango and Bassi, 2021). 
Some studies were also close to using the SE and SsT approaches to reducing energy poverty 
(Shrubsole, Hamilton and Zimmermann, 2019; Okoye, 2020). However, no study to our 
knowledge has considered reducing energy poverty through the Living Lab concepts while 
using the SE or SsT approaches concurrently. 
Therefore, this study introduces SE and SsT concepts in engineering clean cooking energy 
access or energy access sustainability by developing a new SETILL to contribute to the ‘state 
of the art’ to: ‘serve as a suitable theoretical framework or concept to support energy 
innovations’, ‘improve adoption of gender in energy technology’, 'reduce energy poverty’, 
‘improve access to energy services in the poor informal settlements and the energy sectors’, 
‘apply the new SETILL in the poor informal settlement selected’, and ‘build and test the energy 
technology innovation in the informal settlement selected’. 
As of this moment, a study developed a systems-reinforcing model, using the concepts of SE 
and SsT approaches, to tackle energy poverty in informal settlements (Okoye, 2020). However, 
this study was not applied to gendered energy, but to only solve some areas of energy poverty 
challenges. Also, studies have reviewed the effectiveness of policies that could enhance energy 
access in households (Mohlakoana, 2014; Azimoh et al., 2015; Masekameni et al., 2018; Ye, 
Koch and Zhang, 2018). There have also been studies to analyse the challenges of energy 
poverty globally (Maxim, Apostoaie and Popescu, 2016; Team and Baffert, 2015), and also 
studies that developed a framework for sustainable access to energy (Shrestha, R.M. & 
Acharya, 2015). However, all these studies were conducted to tackle some areas of energy 
poverty challenges while ignoring gender. 
The studies that came closest to this research study were those that developed a gender analysis 
framework to familiarize energy policy and encourage the adoption of energy innovations in a 
South African informal settlement (Anditi et al., 2022), developed a living lab to demonstrate 
its concepts and abilities to achieve energy service security (Mukama et al., 2022), and 
concentrated on the understanding and conceptualization of mainstreaming gender, suggesting 
that gender should be taken into account in continuous energy practices (Musango et al., 2020). 
Despite the fact that the majority of these studies focused on gender issues and energy security, 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 6 
 
none of them used the Living Lab and the systems approach to address energy poverty in South 
Africa's informal settlements. 
The mixed method research approach was used for this research study to answer the research 
questions using primary and secondary case studies, and the systems thinking approaches in 
developing a Living Lab for gendered energy technology innovation which will lead to the 
building and testing of an energy technology innovation to a pilot scale. Theoretically, the 
research study developed a new SETILL model and tested the utility of the model in the case 
study selected by developing an energy technology innovation on a pilot scale. A Living Lab 
of the system at this preliminary step in the research study is considered as a physical or virtual 
design methodology space that co-creates and tests innovations through collaboration of end-
users (women) with active involvement in a realistic manner for mitigating energy poverty for 
informal settlement in collaboration with multiple stakeholders using systems thinking 
approaches. 
Furthermore, this research study analysed energy culture, energy patterns, energy assessment 
and sustainable energy access in the households in the case study, which brief introduction is 
found in the next section. This was done to understand energy matters and consumption before 
answering the research questions. The analysis done was to give a better understanding and 
credibility to the barriers to the participation of women as end-users of energy technology 
innovations. Significantly, the outcomes of the research study and investigations were 
represented and modelled as the systems Living Lab to support gendered energy technology 
innovation. 
1.2.1 Defining SRM for EAS, EC, energy behaviour and SEA for this study 
As discussed above, for the purpose of achieving the objectives of this research study, it was 
important to adopt the framework of systems reinforcing model for energy access sustainability 
as proposed by Okoye (2020), which focused on the behavioural, normative and practice 
change processes relating to the adoption of energy innovations as well as gender designs in an 
informal settlement of South Africa. The SRM which was developed after mobilizing the 
energy culture (EC) and sustainability energy access (SEA) frameworks (Stephenson et al., 
2010; Shrestha, R.M. & Acharya, 2015) has four primary system connections recognized in 
the processes and access of energy poverty as presented in the figure 1.1 below: 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 7 
 
 
Figure 1.1: The four major systems interconnections in the systems reinforcing model (SRM) 
for energy access sustainability showing its systems and subsystems. Source: (Okoye, 
2020:75). 
The first interconnections include the material cultures, the cognitive norms or energy practices 
constituting the systems, which is ‘design decisions: energy poverty ’system. They help in 
assessing the status of energy poverty’, energy and energy behaviours in the households of the 
informal settlements. This is because the following ‘design decisions’ or systems of energy 
poverty which were introduced in the development of the energy technology innovation in this 
research study were used to test the model. The second of these interconnections also involves 
many variables as already illustrated in figure 1.1 These are the systems that complement 
energy access that in turn uses the ‘design decisions’ systems for assessment. Such variables 
or systems residing in the third system interconnections may refer to the aspects of options for 
enabling access to energy. Last, System IV interconnections involve factors that indicate the 
impact of adding all the interrelated systems simultaneously on the sustainability of energy 
access improvement (Okoye, 2020). 
The third and fourth systems interconnection are referred to as systems designs because the 
first and second systems interconnections regard them as adequate to expand the network 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 8 
 
reaching of energy in a given region (Gevelt et al., 2018; Gupta, 2018). These systems, when 
treated or addressed, can be detached; but this should be done at an order of a systems approach 
(Okoye, 2020). Hence, in this research study, it was important to treat each system singly in a 
bid to determine which of the interconnections in the system were relevant to the 
accomplishment of the research objective. Row 1 and 2 were deemed feasible system 
interconnections for analysis of this study, while row 3 may be implemented at the end of the 
study for future analysis of how to draw attention to the benefits of options for energy access. 
The fourth row was not considered applicable for this study as this study is not geared towards 
the advocacy for EAS through policies and other forms of intervention as has already been 
done in the literature (Okoye 2020). Nevertheless, if needed later, an addition can always be 
made from the row 4. Therefore, the SRM represented “acts as a management decision 
framework” (Okoye, 2020:163). This constant rationale stands tall at which is parallel to the 
findings of the framework established by Stephenson et al., (2010) and Shrestha, R.M. & 
Acharya (2015). Details on the various ‘design decisions made in the process are explained in 
chapter six of this research. 
Several systems and subsystems have characterised processes or methods enclosed by the EC 
and SEA frameworks, but the EC framework includes numerous systems and components 
which impacts the tenacity of energy poverty in poor informal settlement, while the SEA 
framework also includes numerous systems and subsystems enclosed by the procedures for 
energy access improvement. However, they are characterised and contribute to a common goal 
(Okoye, 2020). Therefore, due to the collaboration of the system interactions, it can be said 
that an adjustment or change to one system can lead to a change in another system (Jamshidi, 
2008). Important processes and components related to energy consumption and the possibility 
of behavioral modifications are described by the EC framework (figure 1.2). Comparable 
components, structured to the prospective performance (Ishak, 2016), have been used in 
comparable research to analyze behaviors (Lawson, R. and Williams, 2012; Okoye, 2020). 
Therefore, a number of logical yet well-known elements that enhance energy usage and 
efficiency should be included in order to comprehend "consumption behavior" (Shrubsole, 
Hamilton, and Zimmermann, 2019). 
Figure 1.2 explains that the systems of energy poverty in the EC recognize the 
interrelationships between them and the factors influencing energy use behaviour. Therefore, 
the objective of the assessment of energy poverty is to enhance the efficiency of energy use in 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 9 
 
energy-poor informal settlements or households (Okoye, 2020). The energy use behaviour 
found in the EC is pivotal to this research study in developing energy technology innovation 
which focuses on gender design in the informal settlement. 
 
Figure 1.2: The main concept of the Energy Cultures framework explaining energy use 
patterns and behaviours. Source: (Stephenson et al., 2010:6124) 
Additionally, the ability to categorize the system's elements explains why it is difficult for 
"consumer behaviors" to expand in a way that is acceptable and boost energy efficiency in 
families or informal settlements with low levels of energy (Pelenur, 2018). Accordingly, the 
"energy poverty assessment" concurs that the interplay among "material culture," "cognitive 
norms," and "energy practices" can influence energy use behavior, resulting in the adoption of 
gender-specific design and energy technology innovation in low-income households in 
informal settlements via the Living Lab. 
Additionally, Shrestha, R.M. & Acharya (2015) proposed the framework known as sustainable 
energy access (SEA) to enhance energy access and evaluate energy poverty in households. 
They emphasized the importance of improving energy access through "access options," 
"assessment of energy poverty," "resource availability," "sustainable technologies," 
"affordability," and "cost and benefits" (Okoye, 2020). Respectively, increased and better 
energy requests, alternative energy supplies and systems in poor households are determinants 
of the ‘assessment of energy poverty’ (Gevelt et al., 2018; Gupta, 2018). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 10 
 
Therefore, according to Okoye (2020:8), the SEA framework (Figure 1.3) examines the least 
possible fundamental energy demands such as “environmental-friendly energy technologies, 
the financial weight of energy supply, affordability of cleaner energy options, benefits of 
energy access options, and optional sustainability, and while considering improved energy 
access concerning social economic and environmental benefits, the SEA framework also 
assesses energy technologies in terms of economic, environmental, social, institutional, and 
technical sustainability pillars”. Therefore, to assist ‘sustainable energy access’ in a poor 
informal settlement, these individual elements that are respectively interrelated are considered. 
 
Figure 1.3: Sustainable Energy Access framework including the elements of energy access 
and energy poverty. Source: (Shrestha & Acharya, 2015:5). 
In order to lessen "energy poverty" and enhance "energy access," this research study has 
merged the core ideas or hypotheses of the EC and SEA frameworks that were discovered and 
integrated into the SRM, which employed SE and SsT to construct the relationships between 
systems and elements. While the SEA insists that the sustainability of the "social," "economic," 
"technical," and "environmental" systems be taken into consideration in order to lessen the 
effects of energy poverty, the EC recognizes the energy consumption behaviours.  
In order to support energy innovation at a pilot scale by involving end users (women) and other 
stakeholders in real-life scenarios to achieve the security of energy services, it is crucial to 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 11 
 
comprehend and incorporate these concepts into the development of the new SETILL model 
with gender design. Chapter 5 of my thesis will go into further detail about these ideas. 
 
1.3 The background and problem statement to the context of the 
research study 
The energy sector has produced substantial attention in both research and industries due to the 
observed striking technological advancements (Mastelic, 2014), like renewable energy 
technology, solar energy technology and clean cooking technology. However, the promising 
technological innovations seen are not guaranteed to be accepted by customers and prospective 
end users (Mastelic, 2014). This might not be because of the willingness of the energy sector 
to implement these innovations or if the energy innovations truly exist as applicable to 
technology, but because some of these glaring innovations lack the direct involvement of end-
users in the development processes of the energy products and services (Pełka, 2013), and 
because they are mostly weakened by potential user’s fear for change, inability to comprehend 
the technologies, and by legislative or political hindrances (Mastelic, 2014). This means that 
there should be a collaboration between the stakeholders and end-users in co-researching, co-
designing, co-creating, prototyping, and testing energy innovation technology. This will help 
to meet the expectations and needs of the end-users (Pełka, 2013). 
Gender and energy have grown to be one of the analytical pathways for connecting the 
intervention of energy (Mazzeo, Lazer and Zieky, 2006), to the Sustainable Development 
Goals, and an important achievement of the SDGs by 2030 is the reduction of energy poverty 
as presented by the United Nations (2014). Regardless of many attempts, gender inequality still 
exists at all stages of energy, power, management, and all economic sectors, and energy poverty 
is still prevalent in society. There is no doubt that energy insecurity is a huge challenge in the 
households of the informal settlements leading to about 50% of the dwellers in South Africa 
(Anditi et al., 2022). The issue of gender affects every part of society, especially in households. 
Therefore, when it comes to energy management, distribution of energy resources, and 
deciding who uses energy products, there is quite a gender imbalance, especially at the 
household level in informal settlements, although the industries and workplaces could be 
different (Köhlin et al., 2011). 
As stated above, the issue of energy insecurity in the informal settlements in South Africa is 
gendered (Musango et al., 2020). Literature has investigated and primarily articulated the links 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 12 
 
between gender and energy (Oparaocha and Dutta, 2011). Women perform a more significant 
number of labour-rigorous tasks in households. Therefore, the advancements in the energy 
market or innovations around this sector need to benefit women uniquely. The recommendation 
is to support the gender equality by including women in the processes of decision making of 
the design of innovation of energy (Sohna, 2018). This is the same way as prevention of 
comprehensive market opportunities for financial services due to gender discrimination 
(George et al., 2016). 
Energy technology needs sustainable development goal 5, gender equality, poverty, and 
inequality as identified in the United Nations’ Agenda 2030, (United Nations, 2016). 
According to Elena Bou, (2019), gender equality is individually relevant to the energy industry 
for three key reasons: 
1. Gender diversity enables employees to deliver more enhanced performances than any 
other gender secured teams. 
2. The energy industry has remained as an innovative industry that inspires creativity; 
hence, for organizational creativity, the energy industry requires diversity. 
3. In line with a human approach, energy designs and solutions can therefore be viewed 
from the user perspective. This is due to the fact that in energy efficiency sector and 
some other sectors such as smart building, the African businesses and domestic 
markets in rural and urban settings (Temu, 1999; Mwaura et al, 2012), women are a 
meager market that needs to be understood qualitatively as the end-users and 
consumers. 
More women live in the poor informal settlements that constitute more than half of the 
population in an informal settlement, implying that gender defines the poor informal 
settlements (Kovacic et al., 2019). Therefore, gender equality matters in policy formulation 
and design of energy technology or the energy sector at all levels. But it is mandatory to find 
out the issues and make women involved in the initial stages to discuss and search for the 
options that might be defined as possible. 
Anditi et al. (2022) have indicated that women are the most vulnerable and also hugely affected 
by the issue of energy insecurity, and although plenty of women (poor or wealthy) suffer greatly 
in poor informal settlements. (Barsoum, 2006; European Institute of Gender Equality, 2020), 
indicates that there is a need to investigate and understand the importance of gendered energy 
in the informal settlements in the process of developing energy innovations. Thus, gender 
equality issues are essential at all levels of energy sectors, management, distribution of energy 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 13 
 
resources, policymaking, and designs in energy technology. Also, the argument for greater 
gender representativity in the design of energy systems is not that it will change the system's 
performance, but that it can reduce the issue of injustice. This is because the argument should 
be one of rights and justice, transformation, and agency for women as economic actors. Hence, 
it is crucial to include more women in gendered energy technology design because more 
women are necessary in energy technology innovation and decision-making. With regard to 
this research, it was revealed that energy technology innovation can be promoted under the 
banner of ‘Living Lab,’ to enhance gender in the energy industry. 
Living Lab, throughout the last years, has been even more developed and defined; its 
subsequent concern is based on the creative engagement of the end-users as co-designers or 
co-producers of technologies and innovations (as Higgins, 2011 highlighted). The Living Lab 
can therefore be defined as the private/public domain where people; specifically the 
stakeholders and users bring the new innovations or technology introductions that are required 
to be designed in the Innovation Design stage and provide input in the process (Evans, et al, 
2017). For example, the phones are either touch screen phones or button screen phones; the 
manufactures of the mobile phones set a forum where the end users can participate to establish 
which between the touch screen and the button is more favorable. This is important in that the 
end users are involved in manufacturing decisions thus enabling the manufacturers save costs 
and results into social acceptance of the mobile phone. This process is called a “User-Centred 
Design (UCD)”. 
The user-centred design (UCD) is also a factor within this research study as the design is also 
a process considered within this study to obtain the intended objectives. The Living Lab at the 
UCD shows us why the focus of this study is on the end-users, in this case women only to 
accomplish the goals of this type of research. Therefore, according to the report of Interaction 
Design Foundation (2022:1), the UCD is “an iterative design process in which designers focus 
on the users and their needs in each phase of the design processes”. It is also stated that user-
centred research was used to “support new product/system design and development to promote 
more rapid adoption and carbon-saving use of energy efficient and renewable technologies in 
homes” (Caird and Roy, 2008:1). The diagram below, shows the process of user-centric design: 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 14 
 
 
Figure 1.4: User-Centric Design (UCD) Processes. Source: (Interaction Design Foundation, 
2022:1) 
The diagram above explains the approach of the UCD which is an optimistic process to 
innovate new solutions. It begins with human beings and ends with the solutions to their 
personal needs. This has a strong link to the research goal and the Living Lab concept to be 
used in this study. Hence, the Living Lab engages the end-users to co-generate the open, 
innovative or developed environments.  
The Living Lab idea is still relatively recent with regard to the gendered energy research field 
(Mukama et al., 2022; Petrulaityte et al., 2022). However, the Living Lab could dare to 
support the innovation processes for all the parties concerned, ranging from the stakeholders, 
the consumers and or users, to the prospective consumers and or users, especially the 
feminine fraternity within the real-world context as proposed by Ståhlbröst & Holst 2012. 
Living Lab concepts have been applied successfully in the ICT sector (Guldemond & 
Geenhuizen, 2012), geographical areas: urban and rural, ICT for Energy efficiency and Food 
security (Thematic perspective) (Geerts, 2011) and in Education (Callaghan & Herselman, 
2015). 
Thus, the objective of this research is the same as well, to enhance energy access, or in other 
words eliminate energy poverty. This research will advance theoretical and empirical 
knowledge of processes of developing energy technology innovations within Systems thinking 
Living Lab that is gendered design or the roles of women in co-designing, co-creating, co-
researching and testing energy innovation expectations of the end-users. According to Jere, 
Kauhonina and Gamundani (2014) it is important to look at the rural and urban setting in a bid 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 15 
 
to formulate or integrate the Living Lab. But it centred the research on the poor rural informal 
settlements and the case learnings manifest from the Living Lab environment. 
This study provides the understanding of how women supply their insights in the decision-
making process of co-design of technologies when having to do with energy insecurity 
challenges in rural informal settlements, leading to understanding of how management of 
energy sector in Africa is relevant to phenomena under investigation to make it possible to 
generate new theories (George et al., 2016). Clearly, the existing solutions to gendered 
energy technology needs have not been able to meet the energy poverty challenge and 
although some are already targeting the urban informal settlements as the approach here, 
none were now targeting the informal systems settlements as observed in the Living Lab 
using the systems approaches. Thus, this comparison regarding the informal settlements 
expands on the objectives of this study. 
Additionally, the majority of analyses on energy poverty and the Living Labs focus on rural 
areas because the majority of Africa remains rural (UNDP, 2011; Azimoh et al., 2015; Eder, 
Mutsaerts and Sriwannawit, 2015; Pedersen, 2016; and Zavratnik, Superina et al., 2019). This 
research will also engage in analysis and developlement of gender design in SETILL for the 
rural areas because there are more poor people in the rural areas than in the urban areas. The 
correlation between gender and energy is that gender balance should be attained in energy 
technology construction so that both those who fund the technology and its users will be 
pleased during the testing stages as pointed by Pełka (2013). For instance, based on industries 
or households or the majority of poorest informal settlements, more women is involved in the 
use of energy products yet more men are the ones who make design related decisions. Hence, 
according to the research study, women should also contribute from the design stage right to 
the testing stage of the products (that is let the innovations be brought by more women). 
In light of this, the study suggests that women should always be included in the design process 
if we want to address the problems of energy insecurity, market transitions and investment in 
the energy sector in Africa, poverty and inequality, rural and urban livelihood, and the 
development of sustainable energy technology to meet the energy products. Therefore, the 
problem statement is: ‘the big challenge and hindrance towards stipulating the needs and 
contribution of women in the processes of energy innovation or the lack of greater gender 
representativity in the design of energy innovations/systems which could reduce the issue of 
justice, reduce one of the agencies for women as economic actors, thereby leading to lack of 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 16 
 
security of energy services in informal settlements in Africa’. As a result, this study will add 
to the body of knowledge on the predicted behaviors of stakeholders impacting the innovation 
of energy technology in the Living Lab and the extent to which women make decisions in the 
energy sector in relation to rural informal settlements.  
 
1.4 Research aims and objectives 
In response, the South African government has devoted its attention to addressing Energy 
poverty endeavours in poor informal settlements or households. Unfortunately, much of this 
gendered energy is missing in the poverty research analysis and poverty assessments by most 
researchers (Mazzeo, Lazer and Zieky, 2006). They have culminated into the argument that 
energy poverty within the Africa continent is gendered. The issue of using gender as an 
insignificant factor in the energy innovation processes of the informal settlement is that it 
hampers the recognition of energy innovations (Anditi et al., 2022). The informal settlements 
in Africa are gendered (Kovacic et al., 2019) and thus require gender equality as provided in 
SDG5 by the year 2030 and to encourage gender mainstreaming to realize energy security in 
the context of informal settlements. 
Hence, this study seeks to ‘advance theoretical and empirical knowledge on the processes of 
developing energy technology innovations within a systems thinking Living Lab that at least 
addresses positioning of gender or women at the core of designing, creating, researching, 
prototyping, and testing of energy innovations to ensure the end-users’ utility requirements can 
be met in ways that would eventually help address energy poverty. To attain the research aim, 
the following research objectives will be addressed: 
1. To conceptualise and understand the Living Lab concepts, gender inequality, energy 
security, energy technology innovations and informal settlements. Determining the 
present functions and impact of women in energy technology innovation. Evaluating 
the consequences of gender inequality on results of energy innovation. Suggesting 
strategies and frameworks for mainstreaming gender disparities in energy technology 
innovation. 
2. To understand the cultural, norms, practices or technological mechanisms influencing 
the level of adoption or acceptance of energy innovations, gender design or end-users 
within energy innovation processes in the informal settlements. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 17 
 
3. To investigate the influence of end-user’s contributions on energy technology 
innovation in a systems Living Lab context and how human factors affect 
technologies. 
4. To develop a Systems Living Lab that focuses on gender designs for the development 
of energy technology innovation up to a pilot scale through the involvement of end-
users (women) and other stakeholders in real-life scenarios to achieve the security of 
energy services. In the typical case study, the new SETILL model incorporates the 
crucial elements of objectives 1-3 to advance energy security. 
 
1.5 Research questions 
To address the research objectives, the following research questions related to each of the 
research objectives were answered: 
1. What is the conceptualization and understanding of the Systems Living Lab concept, 
gender inequality, energy security, energy technology innovations, material cultures, 
cognitive norms and energy practices of the informal settlements and how do human 
factors affect technologies in literature? a) What positions do women hold in energy 
technology innovation in Sub-Saharan Africa today? b) To what extent do women's 
and men's life experiences of energy inequality affect outcomes for energy 
innovation, such as access, sustainability, and technological progress? c) Which 
strategies and frameworks can be designed or put into action to address the gender 
imbalance and promote fair participation in energy technology innovations? 
2. Which are the possible factors determining the involvement of knowledge and 
participation of women in the decision-making process of co-designing, co-creating, 
co-researching, prototyping and testing the energy technology innovations in the 
informal settlement to satisfy the intended recipients? 
3. How can the mechanism(s) be improved to enhance the level of adoption or 
acceptance of energy innovations and end-user (women) contributions and 
participation to address the security of energy services in poor informal settlements? 
4. Is it possible that the proposed Systems Living Lab specifically on gender design can 
act as a theoretical model or frame work for energy technology innovation, to enhance 
the take up of gender, fight energy poverty and enhance access to energy services in 
the poor informal settlements and the energy sectors? 
 
1.6 Significance and rationale of the research study 
This reinforces the previous remarks on gender structure of energy production and distribution 
and gender definition of the customer of energy products. Ordinarily, firms develop objects 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 18 
 
indifferent to who uses them in their homes (Kohlin et al., 2011). Women suffer most from the 
impacts of restricted use of modern energy forms, and they should contribute towards the 
development of energy technologies (Cecelski, 2012). 
Women and men are not society and or energy sector equal and women are in different ‘spaces’ 
from those designed by men (Gillard and Okonjo-Iweala, 2020) hinder equal distribution of 
energy services or secure energy services in Africa. That is because the structure which has 
already been framed and constructed as male; the structure cannot mold itself in that framework 
and yet it has to do justice to women. In addition, it is as reported that out of the world’s total 
population, 68 percent of population in Africa has no access to energy services where the use 
of electricity is considered. (United Nations, 2014). Also, it is recorded that 2.6 million people 
carry out household cooking relying on biomass which has several effects like economic and 
social problems in the informal settlement (United Nations, 2014).  
The South African government has paid more attention in addressing energy poverty 
challenges in the poor informal settlements, or parts of the community. However, the improved 
own-constructed indicator of the government shows limited progress in the provision of 
electricity and gender inequality in the poor informal settlements (Musango et al., 2020). By 
the same token, most researchers and poverty assessments overlook or even dismiss gendered 
energy (Mazzeo et al. 2006). In other words, failure to consider gender in the analysis of energy 
innovation processes in the informal settlement means that the issue of when to accept energy 
innovations will never be addressed (Anditi et al., 2022). This is because the energy innovation 
processes have been seen, to consist of the generation of knowledge, then conversion of the 
knowledge into the energy innovation products and the consumption of the energy innovation 
products (Fri and Savitz, 2014).  
Nonetheless, there is a low uptake of adoption of energy innovations in the informal settlements 
(Soman 2014; Nasra et al. 2021), because the end-users within the informal settlements do not 
participate in the design processes, because the designers pay little attention to the users and 
their requirements throughout the design processes. However, given that the energy policies 
and energy decision-making are not bottom up policies and decision-making, but rather top up 
policies and decision-making Ambole et al., 2021), this means that there will be a low rate of 
uptake or acceptance of the innovation products by the targeted end-users. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 19 
 
In addition to being gender neutral, current approaches to energy security and systems thinking 
overlook informal settlements and take little into account the provision of energy services 
(Musango et al., 2020; Musango and Bassi, 2021; Anditi et al., 2022). The well-being of people 
living in informal settlements and practically every element of daily life is impacted by 
achieving energy services for all, as outlined in SDG 7 (Oparaocha and Dutta, 2011). As 
previously mentioned, women should have equal access to the Living Lab for the design 
process if we hope to develop a sustainable energy system that can keep up with energy 
breakthroughs. Living Labs offers fresh approaches to the phenomena of managing innovation 
processes. 
In order to enable and empower end-users (women) to actively participate and co-create open 
innovation through the systems Living Lab, the study's rationales included bringing about 
gender balance by taking into account and involving women in decision-making in energy 
design processes, as well as contributing to a body of knowledge on energy poverty, Living 
Lab concepts, gender equality, energy studies, science, technology, and innovation, 
developmental policy, planning, systems thinking, and energy use patterns and behaviors in 
order to develop energy innovations in Africa. 
In terms of practical adequacy let us outline where the study could be of more benefit to a lot 
of stakeholders and informal settlements particularly in Development policy and planning, 
Living Lab studies and contributions to methodology. The research could also compel 
researchers to use Africa as a research context and appreciate the possibility of developing new 
theory in energy, engineering, and management research out of the phenomena under study 
(George et al., 2016). Nevertheless, most of papers covering energy poverty and Living Labs 
are conducted in rural area setting since the majority of Africa comprises of rural areas (Azimoh 
et al., 2015; Eder, Mutsaerts & Sriwannawit, 2015; Pedersen, 2016; UNDP, 2011; Zavratnik, 
Superina & Duh, 2019). This work will approach, evaluate and advance Systems Living Lab 
scenarios in rural areas with the aim of combating energy poverty. This is due to the reason 
that there are previous works that have implemented research on reducing energy poverty 
within the context of cities. Equally important, these have informed the increase in the 
provision of human capital within the manufacturing industry within the last decade as noted 
by authors such as Okoye (2020), as well as Mukama et al., 2022). 
Furthermore, most of the typologies of the Living Labs identified as part of the studies for this 
thesis do not have the gender or are gender blind which means that although they may engage 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 20 
 
users, a gender perspective may not be considered. Therefore, this research study would 
supplement by developing the SETI Living Lab typology in which the gender aspect is included 
by the users. This study will also be useful to the gender equality alongside combating poverty, 
livelihoods in urban territories noted in the well-being section of United Nations, Agenda 2030 
as a sustainable development goal. Therefore, the study will be able to gain an appreciation and 
operationalisation of energy security, the Living Lab and in order to appreciate the prospects 
of the management of the energy technology in Africa for the phenomena that may be providing 
potential to co-create new theories.  
The contribution of the study will lie in the formation of a novel theoretical approach, which is 
to be utilised to explain tendencies of end-user involvement or integration of energy 
technologies in energy processes, as well as human factors affecting technologies, creation of 
the proposed new Systems Living Lab and establishment of innovation in Biogas technology, 
which can be trialled in the selected Case study utilizing systems approaches towards 
supporting the gendered energy technology advancements. However, health literacies general 
importance to a diverse population and expected methodological, theoretical and practical 
implications for the field are discussed below. Moreover, it would extend the first results on 
gendered innovation concept and how it is produced in the context of the Living Lab 
environment from the theoretical and empirical perspectives. 
With this backdrop, this PhD study indicates that, if we intend to address the issue of energy 
insecurity, energy sector investment and market transitions in Africa, poverty and inequality, 
rural and urban livelihood, and attain a sustainable energy technology to meet the energy 
products, women and girls should be featured in the design process always. 
This chapter has shown the relevance and purpose of the research stating that the development 
of SETILL could be essential to reducing energy poverty and gender inequality in South Africa. 
Therefore, the next sections will continue with the literature review, which further explores the 
definitions or conceptualization of the term Living Lab, gender and energy for more 
understanding. Thereafter, the problem statements, proposed research questions and, 
contributions of the research study.
