Chapter 2 
Literature Review 
2.1 Introduction 
The literature review is used to conceptualise the Living Lab, gendered energy security, energy 
poverty, biogas technology, the informal settlement and give a critical analysis of the terms 
defined and how the proposed study may conceptualise it for the context-specific application 
in the case studies. 
This literature review seeks to discuss the role of gender in energy technology innovations in 
the context of Living Labs. Living Labs are pre-competitive, open innovation environments 
that engage real-life communities in the development and testing process (Clermont & Fionda, 
2016). By structuring Living Labs based on gender, new energy solutions can be created that 
are equally useful for all community members with particular focus on women. To address this 
challenge, it is therefore very important to apply new strategies which should take into account 
the Social, Economic and Cultural aspects of these communities. In addition, this review 
underlines the need to embrace gender empowerment as captured in the SDG 5, which seeks 
to address inequalities for gender at all levels. Energy poverty and the gendered nature of it 
further is a compelling social justice issue, but also a determinant in the viability of 
effectiveness and practices of energy solutions at a worldwide scale (Callaghan and Herselman, 
2015). Thus, raising awareness of gender aspects in energy technology innovations as well as 
including girls and women into the enumerated list of beneficiaries will help to contribute 
toward sustainable and fair development on the basis of improved energy access for South 
African communities. 
The literature approach utilised in this study is what the researcher calls a stand-alone 
qualitative systematic literature review (realist review) (Finfgeld-Connett and Johnson, 2013), 
conducted using a rigorous quality (Okoli and Schabram, 2010). This type of literature review 
focuses on limiting random and scientific misconceptions and requires rigorous use of 
scientific plans (Paré, 2017), with close adherence to clear scientific concepts (Liberati et al., 
2009). The realist review as a new method of a systematic review “does not provide simple 
answers to complex questions, might not tell policy-makers or managers whether something 
works or not, but will provide the policy and practice to the community with the kind of rich, 
detailed and highly practical understanding of complex social interventions which is likely to 
be of much more use to them when planning and implementing programs at a national, regional 
or local level” (Pawson et al. 2005:21). This type of review method is theory-driven which can 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 22 
 
be used to improve the systematic review by applying qualitative strategies of complex 
interventions that could be used to inform policy decision-making (Paré, 2017). 
This approach of literature review used in this proposal could be similar to the narrative review 
(Torraco, 2016), which is focused on the qualitative interpretation of existing knowledge 
(Sylvester, Tate and Johnstone, 2013), and does not require a generalisation or collective 
knowledge from the review (Davies 2000; Green, Johnson, and Adams 2006). The reason for 
a literature review in a theory study is to enable ideas on the topic to emanate from the data and 
to avoid the influence of the existing literature on the research and its results (Glaser & Strauss, 
1967; Glaser, 1992; Ramalho et al. 2015; Strauss & Corbin, 1990). Similarly, this study’s 
approach to the literature review is based on the following objectives: to develop the 
phenomenon of gendered energy technology to energy security, as well as to provide a relevant 
and informed understanding of gendered energy technology and its core theories. 
Furthermore, the approach used for the literature review was to consider the topic at three levels 
of recursion, which is a three-level review framework based on the three systems thinking 
questions (Onyeagoziri, 2020). This three-level review framework or systems questions can be 
regarded as the WHY (level 1), which is the parent discipline concept analysis of underlying 
key concepts, which gives a broad context of the research concern on how it functions (that is, 
what role do the concepts play in the bigger system of which they are a part). 2), HOW (level 
2), which gives attention to the concern of the research study (that is, how the behaviours of 
the core variables can be changed). The third level WHAT, which is the key concepts in the 
empirical research results, that is the core concepts formulated as variables (this process could 
be completed after the data collection and analysis). 
2.1.1 Literature review framework 
The outline for the three-level literature review process for this study based on the three systems 
thinking question (Onyeagoziri, 2020), which was used to build responsiveness into the study, 
is presented in figure 2.1 below: 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 23 
 
Figure 2.1: Three-level literature review framework based on systems energy technology 
innovation Living Lab with gendered perspectives. 
2.2   Level 1: parent discipline 
Level 1 supplies information about the contextual condition functions of the research focus; it 
describes the framework upon which the fundamental research themes fit. On level 1, the 
context in which the focus of the research finds itself is explained-with insight into and 
identification of how the SETI Living Lab fits within it, thus influencing the same. The core 
question underlying this paper rests on, 'Why may we want to develop this theory in relation 
to achieving the question of gendered energy security?' In creating attempts that are aimed at 
bringing either a total or certain aspects of insecurity about energy or energy poverty to 
improved states of gender equality, hence driving development in the energy fields within 
settlements. 
Therefore, the overall parent discipline key concept of this research is 'gendered energy 
technology innovation and energy security' as seen in 2.1. This is the discipline that questions 
why the research result needs to be developed, or why the research problem has to be solved. 
2.2.1 Gendered energy technology innovations and energy security development 
Indeed, there is a rather feeble materialised proof on gendered diffusion of energy 
development of informal settlement in African about addressing energy insecurity. Energy 
insecurity which could be referred to as ‘energy poverty’ based on this study is said by 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 24 
 
Reddy, Annecke, Blok, Bloom, Boardman, Eberhard, Ramakrishna, and Wodon (2000) as 
any situation where access to adequate number of options to purchase sufficient, affordable, 
reliable, clean, and secure energy services that can support human and economic 
development is not available. Energy insecurity or energy poverty, the nature of energy 
provision to the poor informal settlement in South Africa is Gendered (Kovacic et al., 2019) 
and literature has examined and mainly discussed the relationship between gender and energy 
(Oparaocha and Dutta, 2011). A failure to concentrate or even attend to gender equality is 
indeed one of the processes that deny achievement of energy access in Africa (Ulsrud, 2020). 
Many papers in Energy Research and Social Science (Ockwell et al., 2018) and most of the 
energy research relevant to the Energy for Sustainable Development has focused on a range of 
gendered issues including sustainable energy development, gender analysis, and women’s and 
gender studies (McDade and Clancy, 2003). Additionally, the SGDs have incorporated an 
energy to gender to program to recognize their partnership in addressing a specific SDG 
objective, (McDade and Clancy, 2003). Consequently, the surveys have presented considerable 
evidence on gendered implications stemming from energy poverty or energy insecurity such as 
Clancy, Maduka, 2008; Clancy, 2009. On the other hand, the strategies and methods applied to 
address energy access have been fairly slow when connected to gender and insufficiently 
advocating for the necessity for the poor informal set up (Ambole et al., 2019; Kovacic et al., 
2019). 
Consequently, gender inequality adds to the energy deficiency problem; women in the low-
income sectors bear the brunt of the energy poverty (Botreau and Cohen, 2020). They oversee 
the energy utilisation within homes and are most affected by both physical health and economic 
repercussions of limited access to energy (Botreau and Cohen, 2020). However, gender issues 
are often neglected in energy innovation activities, and therefore, energy solutions that are 
derived to meet the needs of everyone in the community are not well addressed. Gender-centric 
energy technology innovation is an approach, which focuses on the integration of females 
within technology to contribute to the development and deployment of energy solutions. This 
approach enhances the chances of adoption and utilisation of energy technologies such as the 
Biogas Technology and the solar powered radio since they meet the needs of the men and 
women within the community.  
Energy security is a complex challenge in informal settlements that encompasses the 
accessibility and cost of energy, their renewability and consistency. Energy security is, 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 25 
 
therefore, dependent on improvements in the factors that lead to energy poverty such as 
infrastructure provision, -economic difficulties and social disparities (Pysar et al., 2018). It is 
within this context that a systems living lab provides a holistic approach to addressing these 
challenges, because the methodology of living labs is coupled with a system thinking 
perspective. It makes it possible to get a systemic view of the energy situation to provide for 
the growth of solutions that can be adapted and implemented on a larger scale.  
Poor informal settlements in the Global South suffer disproportionately from a lack of 
reliable and affordable energy (Nightingale, 2006 and Fathallah et al., 2020). This scenario 
depicts an inability to avail adequate and reliable energy for the normal household use 
without disruptions and affordable prices (Parag, 2014 and Jain, 2010). More emphasis is 
placed on how one or the other type of social exclusion: gender included impacts energy 
poverty in these areas (Mosango et al., 2020 and Ballesteros-Arjona et al., 2022). Lack of 
energy resources hampers the drive to promote gender equality and women’s rights (Pascale 
et al., 2016). For instance, women have health problems due to exposure to indoor 
combustive emissions obtained from using raw materials such as paraffin and biomass 
(Kovacic et al., 2019); and collecting the fuel is usually a time-consuming exercise which 
confines women to the house thus limiting their education and income earning opportunities 
(Winther et al., 2017 and Choumert-Nkolo et al., 2019).  
Thus, it is crucial to consider gender aspects when performing the energy research to avoid 
biases towards gender relations concerning resources, pay differentials, and gender 
expectations (roles and tasks) (Ihalainen et al., 2020). Hence,gender mainstreaming an 
approach that entails integration of gender perspectives in policies, processes, structures, and 
culture of systemic synthesis (Mengistu et al., 2016 ) is crucial to enhance common and equality 
in terms of energy access for both male and female (Dutta et al., 2017). Yet, despite the growth 
of interest in the link between gender and energy, relatively limited studies focus on how gender 
inequalities in the informal settlements deepen energy vulnerability (Nightingale et al., 2006; 
Musango et al., 2020; Chicombo and Musango, 2022), with more focus on rural informal 
contexts (Pascale et al., 2016; Johnson et al., 2019 and Hirmer et al This has an implication 
that informal settlement in the rural areas, especially the poorest ones are highly likely to be 
more negatively impacted in the aspects of energy insecurity and development. 
Hence, an approach may be in the provision of know-how as well as information regarding 
gender prospects that could be used by the energy sectors as well as the communities to 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 26 
 
address such problems. In this regard, many support designs have been developed to help 
those who want to introduce gender perspective to energy solutions for urban setting 
(Mukama et al., 2022 and Petrulaityte et al., 2022). Yet, these sources tend to lack systems 
thinking, do not sufficiently accommodate co-design that blends gender concentrated 
initiatives with energy and vernacular rural areas; and although such sources present 
considerable information, they appear increasingly unmanageable because of their length and 
extensive preliminaries. This lack is further evidenced from our field work interviews where 
no stake holder interviewed, whether independently or collectively, reported having any 
systems thinking or co-design strategies they apply that address the issue of gender. This 
underscores a significant deficiency that must be addressed: there are currently no readily 
usable and efficient solutions methods to facilitate the co-design of effective gender-sensitive 
energy solutions to serve the populations living in informal rural settlements. 
This research fills this gap by proposing and validating the first Systems Energy Technology 
Innovation Living Lab (SETILL) co-design framework. This framework is intended to build 
proactive readiness of both private and public actors toward developing efficient energy 
solutions that put into consideration impoverished informal settlements’ gender concerns. 
This study applies this framework in an introduction, to establish it as the study’s prime body 
of knowledge, as well as a co-design tool in the discussion of its empirical findings with the 
intended users. 
Therefore, this research study posits that a user-centred approach could be employed to make 
sense of and re-imagine gender equality, the Living Lab and energy security. Musango et al. 
(2020) research study has considered the re- conceptualisation of Gender Mainstreaming, 
Energy Security and Poor urban Environment Without Living lab. Hence, this research study 
will also seek to incorporate the process of re-conceptualising the re concepts of Living Lab 
technology into the re-conceptualisation of gender equality; energy security and poor rural 
informal settlements in order to understand the idea of gender equality and energy security, 
based on systems thinking approach. This is because, for this research study the subjects for 
‘designing sustainable solutions to deal with the energy insecurity challenge’ (Musango et al., 
2020, p.4) would be gender equality, energy security, poor informal settlement, and Living Lab 
technology. 
Scholars, such as Cravioto et al. (2014), advocate for the integration of energy services into 
energy policy agendas, with a particular focus on ensuring accessibility for disadvantaged 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 27 
 
communities. Energy innovation aimed at providing energy services for poor informal 
settlements has thus far overlooked gender considerations, and the lack of attention to gender-
specific energy needs hinders efforts to address energy insecurity and promote the adoption of 
energy innovations, particularly in regions of the Global South (Anditi et al., 2022). Energy 
insecurity is prevalent in poor informal settlements, which are home to approximately 50% of 
Sub-Saharan Africa's population, and in these areas, Women are usually affected by this issue, 
being both the most vulnerable and the hardest hit (Anditi et al., 2022). Understanding the 
gender-energy relationship in poor informal settlements is crucial for integrating gender 
perspectives into the energy innovation process. Most current gender analysis frameworks fail 
to adequately address gendered energy innovations, especially within the unique context of 
poor informal settlements. Therefore, this research study aims to fill this gap by examining the 
commonalities in the formation of informal settlements across the continent. 
Therefore, against the backdrops, this research study seeks to cover the gendered energy gap 
in the poor informal settlement and investigate how the theory or conceptualization of gender 
equality is linked to the theory or concept of the Living Lab and poor informal settlement when 
tackling energy insecurity. This study will thus be greatly embracing the idea of the systems 
Living Lab in developing a theory for gendered energy technology innovation in the informal 
settlement. The subsequent section briefly contextualizes and gives the status of gender 
inequality and energy access in South Africa and Africa. 
2.2.2 Historical context and current status of gender inequality in energy access 
Prominent gender inequality in energy access has historical political and social causes that can 
be traced to South Africa and many other African countries. The culture of male dominance 
that has been evident in many African societies tends to influence gender equality or the lack 
of it in many sectors like energy (Johnson, Gerber and Muhoza, 2019). Traditionally, women 
in African societies have been confined to the home, socially and economically powerless, and 
have had little scope or opportunity for education, business, and other ventures. This division 
of labour based on gender has greatly influenced energy access since women are responsible 
for supplies of energy for home use, which include cooking, water heating, and space heating, 
services that mainly depend on traditional sources of energy such as firewood and charcoal 
(Jagoe et al., 2020). 
In South Africa, the apartheid policy system aggravates gender and racial disparities and 
provides a background to the country’s current socio-economic system that gives differentiated 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 28 
 
access to the mineral-energy complex. There have been challenges in the South African energy 
landscape since the definite post-apartheid progressive upturns; today, millions of people in 
the country, including those who are rurally and informally housed, remain inadequately 
electrified (Mario, Brincat and Moe, 2024). Women suffer grave consequences in these 
communities due to their social and economic status and roles based on gender (Heise et al., 
2019). 
The currently available data shows that there has not been much progress in addressing the 
gender gap in energy access. Estimates by the International Energy Agency (IEA) show that in 
sub-Saharan Africa alone, more than 600 million people have no access to electricity, and 
women endure the worst impacts of energy poverty (Ferdous, 2021). Although there are 
improvements in the electrification rates in South Africa, this does not necessarily mean that 
everyone can access modern energy services or have reliable energy for several activities, 
especially for rural women. 
Patriarchal norms shape access, use, and control of energy resources in African societies to a 
large extent. This research study also examines how the energy sector has remained a male-
dominated industry in the past, where very few women were employed, let alone given 
decision-making authority. It is also examining how human factors affect energy security. It is 
also established that men dominate the formal employment sector, whereby they are the major 
participants in generating, distributing, and vending energy (Oyiana, 2021). At the same time, 
women are either ceremonially involved in the same processes or are part-time employees 
involved in the informal vending of energy in their homes. The next section explains gendered 
energy technology innovation leading to energy access. 
2.2.3 Energy technology innovation for achieving gender equality in energy services 
As explained above, ensuring reliable energy services continues to pose a significant challenge 
in impoverished rural areas across Africa. A considerable number of households face a deficit 
in appropriate energy technologies to meet their modern energy demands. Women bear the 
brunt of this issue due to entrenched gender roles within households. Despite the evident gender 
disparities in energy services, the incorporation of gender perspectives in the innovation of 
energy products remains largely unaddressed. This research study delved into the intersection 
of gendered innovation and energy innovation, investigating how this combination can 
alleviate issues surrounding the security and accessibility of energy services in households 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 29 
 
within impoverished rural settings and went ahead to develop a biogas technology to a pilot 
scale with gendered perspective. 
Innovation has long been a cornerstone of human progress, driving pivotal shifts in agriculture, 
science, and industry throughout history. Today, in our modern, technology-driven societies 
powered by digital advancements, energy innovation plays a pivotal role in facilitating access 
to a wide array of services (batinge et al., 2022). Energy services encompass a spectrum of 
benefits facilitated by energy carriers, fuel sources, and technologies, (Sovacool, 2011). These 
benefits extend to various outcomes of energy utilisation (Modi et al., 2005), ranging from 
preserved food and cooked meals to illuminated spaces, chilled beverages, rapid internet 
connectivity, and cosy sleeping arrangements (Sovacool, 2011).  
The conventional centralised approach to innovation is swiftly losing relevance 
(MacCormack et al., 2007). Top-down innovation typically follows a sequence of idea 
generation, screening, market research, product development, commercialization, and 
implementation (Gaynor, 2013). However, the limitations of these traditional top-down 
models have been evident in academic discourse for approximately four decades, these 
limitations encompass neglect of alternative actors (Sabatier, 1986), limited user ownership, 
prolonged innovation readiness periods, and potential market evolution by the time of 
product availability. Furthermore, even in cases where the market remains static, there's a 
considerable risk that the innovation may not align with market needs due to flaws in market 
research or misperceptions regarding innovation diffusion (Batinge 2022). The process by 
which innovations are adopted within a society (innovation diffusion) is a dynamic and 
evolving phenomenon (Bale et al., 2013). The concept of how innovations spread is well-
established in the study of household energy usage (Wilson & Dowlatabadi, 2007). Studies 
across various disciplines have examined how technologies and ideas spread through 
networks (Valente, 2005). An infection paradigm may also be used here to indicate the 
process of network diffusion (Akbarpour & Jackson, 2018; Pastor-Satorras et al., 2015; Raj et 
al., 2012), as well as an epidemic with the very beginning and the potential development of 
the spread of a disease and its further consequences. This analysis would cannot be complete 
without the mention of the Bass model which was proposed in 1969 and is a clear example of 
a diffusion model. 
The Bass model developed by Frank Bass defines the process of new products adoption via the 
communication between current and potential users. Although several models of diffusion 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 30 
 
focus on social aspects, which in the model of Bass are in the form of word-of mouth influence 
(Bale et al., 2013), the decision making process of the adoption of a new product depends on 
this. Such elements can include the likelihood of its purchase, compatibility of the new product 
with other products, appropriateness of the technology in view of the individual characteristics, 
and the social-cultural factors. Those decisions are usually taken based on such relative factors 
as ‘intrinsic advantages for the intended adoption’ and the technology ‘compatibility with the 
existing technology environment’, as well as ‘simplicity in the usage of a given technology’, 
the possibility of ‘trial run’, and the ‘clearliness of results’ (Vagnani & Volpe, 2017). Perceived 
benefits refer to the extent to which an innovation is believed to be more beneficial than the 
concept which it replaces. Adoption by others is the degree to which an innovation matches up 
to current practices, past experiences and needs of other users. IPO means how difficult or easy 
it is considered to understand and implement an innovation. Trial option can be defined as 
potential in which an innovation can be first tried on a limited level. Observability of its impact 
is the level of measurability in terms of how others are aware of the presence of an innovation. 
On this note, it is in most models explaining the diffusion of innovations that an innovation's 
ability to fulfill users' needs is critical towards the adoption decision. For these reasons, this 
study suggested that the innovation process takes into consideration the context in which the 
user will eventually apply the innovation while developing the innovation based on the specific 
attributes identified in Vagnani and Volpe, (2017) for the innovation to serve exactly according 
to the needs of end consumers. In low-income households, where females are the majority, 
their energy use is significantly higher—about 80% more overall, with a notable 54% increase 
in electricity consumption compared to males (Elnakat et al., 2016). These communities, as 
highlighted by Elnakat et al. (2016), have a higher energy consumption per unit of living area, 
attributed to the prevalence of female-led households and the inefficiency of their homes. The 
role of women is pivotal in achieving satisfaction with household energy services in 
economically disadvantaged areas, given their dominance in such households (Kovacic et al., 
2019) and their higher usage of energy services. 
Concerning energy access, there was a reveal that gender disparities were apparent in many 
African countries, including South Africa (Ngarava et al., 2022). Women, and especially those 
living in the irregular structures have limited access to the energy resources. They experience 
these barriers in their day to day life and in their opportunities to work, to earn an income and 
thus be included in society. Mukumba, Makaka, and Mamphweli (2016) further note that 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 31 
 
women in these communities are most affected by energy poverty because they are the ones 
who are usually charged with the responsibility of fetching traditional biomass energy sources 
like wood and charcoal for cooking and heating. Besides, it requires a huge amount of time and 
energy and imposes threats to women’ health such as respiratory diseases caused by indoor 
emissions. 
These negative effects are not only health wise but also economically as limited energy access 
is more often than not devastating. As highlighted by Anditi et al. (2022), due to lack of access 
to affordable and reliable energy, women cannot efficiently market their products, pursue 
employment ventures like operating small businesses, or attend vocational training sessions. 
This economic subjugation creates poverty and gender inequality cycles because the women 
are helpless in converting the energy resources to better standards in their own life. 
Additionally, the absence or rather scarcity of electricity for learning and operations at night 
limits educational achievement and livelihood opportunities for women and girls, thereby 
perpetuating gender inequalities (Botreau & Cohen, 2020). 
Energy exclusion deepens this inequality through exclusion from planning and decisions that 
shape the provision of this essential commodity. In their study, Botha et al., 2012 have focused 
on the idea that traditional energy governance structures are still patriarchal and hence women 
have very little role in the decision making that impacts their lives through policies and projects. 
As a consequence, energy solutions fail to meet the female demand and choice and therefore, 
have low usage and sometimes undesirable impacts. For example, energy projects may target 
technologies that are in some way detrimental to the usability of the female population, thus 
reducing the utility of those projects. 
To address gender inequality in the access to energy we need to effectively implement gender-
aiding policies, programs, and technologies in the provision of energy. Musango et al., (2020) 
emphasise that women should be included in the energy planning processes and the decision-
making process in practices and activities in order that their specific needs and perspectives are 
recognized. To ensure better solutions of energy in various aspects, it is time we involve women 
as active stakeholders in that process. For instance, engaging women in the design and 
implementation of solar energy solutions can promote the creation of unique solutions 
according to women’s usual schedules and dangers involved with electricity systems. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 32 
 
In the context of the Living Labs, active pro-gender equality approaches can be used to assess 
gender inequality in energy accessibility. Leal Filho et al. (2022) note that Living Labs, which 
involve end-users and promote their active engagement, can help replicate this process, thus 
guaranteeing that energy innovations are developed in parallel with those recipients of energy 
they are intended for. This approach involves identifying women as some of the major 
stakeholders to consult and participate in the co-design process towards meeting their energy 
requirements and emerging issues. For example, the GENS Living Lab (Sphokazi Phelokazi, 
2022) has applied the Living Lab approach in engaging women in the iteration and testing of 
sustainable energy, which point to the Living Labs’ having the capacity to enable gender 
sensitive energy solutions. 
However, communicating supported policies and participatory approaches can lead to reducing 
the obstacles that women encounter when engaging in energy. According to Sphokazi 
Phelokazi (2022) the above-mentioned measures include offering specific grants in guide for 
clean baptistery and specialty, investing in gender-sensitive facilities, and offering capability 
development programs that enable women to be effective members of the energy sector. Hence, 
training and capacity building schemes that assist women with aptitude in renewable energy 
increase employment chances and economic development for the female gender and thus, 
improving gender equality. On integrating gender perspectives within the context of energy 
innovations, this can help mainstream the sustainable development goals (SDGs). According 
to Anditi et al. (2022), gender equality is one of the indicators of the fifth SDG which focuses 
on the speedy attainment of gender equality and the empowering of women. Therefore, there 
is considerable potential for such a target of eliminating gender differences in access to energy 
by countries to help them advance this goal.  
The imbalance between genders in terms of household energy use does not mirror their 
involvement in the innovation of energy services, even though there's evidence that women 
play a crucial role in delivering community-level energy solutions and that initiatives led by 
women in the emerging energy sector often yield positive outcomes (UN Women, 2013). 
Typically, the realms of energy and technology are viewed as male territories, resulting in a 
lesser degree of female contribution to energy service provision and innovation compared to 
their participation in various other sectors of the economy (Pachauri & Rao, 2013). Involving 
users in the design phase can significantly enhance technology development, ensuring it meets 
the unaddressed needs of users. Approaches to energy intervention at the domestic level that 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 33 
 
acknowledge and leverage the significant role women play in the energy landscape, utilising 
their knowledge and impact in both households and wider communities, have the potential to 
facilitate better access to energy solutions (Karlsson, 2007). 
Despite the significance of gender roles in the energy sector, there's a noticeable lack of 
understanding regarding how gender-focused innovation impacts the provision of reliable 
energy services in economically disadvantaged rural settings. This research aims to examine 
the potential of gender-specific innovation in the energy sector to enhance the reliability of 
energy services in such areas using systems thinking approaches. The study suggests that 
involving users in a cooperative and gender-sensitive manner in the design of energy solutions 
can significantly improve the satisfaction with the services provided. The next section explains 
gender equality as a way to codesigning innovations leading to energy security. 
2.2.4 Gender equality as an approach to codesign innovation 
Resulting in the limited empirical means to measure gender equality in the world which is 
scarce (Musango et al., 2020). Different works have focused on the disconnection of gender 
which however there seems to be a general acceptance of gender in energy applications, 
policies and research (Ryan, 2014; Bradshaw, 2018) to foster the power access innovation. 
This created stereotypical influences on gender difference and does not support any concept 
of gender equality (Bradshaw, 2018). The concept of gender equality receives inadequate 
emphasis as a strategy toward ensuring security of energy services in a poor informal 
settlement in Africa (Musango et al., 2020). At present, there is focus on SDG 5 in the 
promotion or support of gender equality and this happened prior through the Millennium 
Development Goals. However, these efforts by the SDG to achieve the results and progress to 
meet their interest in gender equality still encounter challenges within Africa towards 
realizing the appropriate results (Jahan, 1996; Moyo and Dhliwayo, 2019; United Nations, 
2019). 
Moreover, a small invest in African feminine population can only get only 87% of the male 
isolation development level (UNDP, 2016) and this is according to the Gender Development 
Index (Council of Europe, 2004). Out of all the African nations, only six of African countries 
have experienced increased attention to gender inequalities, and this has been considered a loss 
of human development due to inequality between males and females in Africa (UNDP, 2016) 
and this can be explained through the Gender Inequality Index (United Nations, 2019). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 34 
 
For the sake of further explanation, unbiased data on women in the leadership roles, energy 
business, finance, politics, and gender wage differential show that people still have a long 
way to go to embrace gender parity, particularly for women of color (Gillard and Okonjo-
Iweala, 2020). This gender biases research concern does not only encompass identifying the 
gender biases concerning women but also considerable structural and technological hurdles 
that keep women from participating in the design phase of the energy technology 
advancements. They do so because these barriers amplify the main problems and retarding 
the advancement of women (Gillard and Okonjo-Iweala, 2020). Further, this qualifies this 
research study as being about energy security and gender which defines the social 
manufactured attributes that is accorded to men and women while informing that women 
should be empowered to make equal use of energy technology advancements. 
The research study is arguing that the poor informal settlement requires more champions who 
will speak for women in the energy sector who are rarely heard. Thus, every person’s life 
becomes better when women start using their gift to express themselves, because women’s 
rights mean societies’ successes It is said that when one largely included a certain group at first 
that had not been included, every person in that community becomes a chance at getting 
something since women and girls make up half the population of every community (Gates, 
2019). According to Gates (2019) it has been claimed that whenever women thrive, people rise 
up for this is the reason that working for women’s rights, women’s ability and societies health 
and wealth are all in harmony, and nobody can work for society health and its wealth without 
comprehending the link between communities, Women Empowerment and health as those who 
want to lift up humanity should empower women. 
The issue of gender equality was also conceptualized by Booth and Bennett (2002, p.435) with 
the use of a metaphor of a three-sided stool which they called the "equality stool" (see Figure 
2.2) which emanated from the three waves of the push of feminism related to the campaign of 
gender equality. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 35 
 
Figure 2.2: The ‘equality stool’. Source: Booth and Bennett (2002). 
One major trend of the first generation of feminism from as early as 1918 was to campaign 
for equal status, treatment and opportunity in legislation and the second-generation feminism 
that begun from as early as the 1960 was to campaign for the differentiation of women with 
affirmative action. The third wave starting from 1990 emphasizes on the gender concept of 
the campaign as well as equal differences. The third wave in the equality stool recognizes the 
diverse needs of the male and female and concentrates on the necessity to extend the gender 
out in the coverage of services and the direction of the male in the accomplishment of all 
essential outcomes necessary (Booth and Bennett, 2002). 
Thus, the research study supports the ‘equality stool’ and endeavours to argue that gender 
equality and its application, together with its implementations, can aid in engaging with, and 
solving, the issues that surround the provision of, ‘security of energy supplies’. This is because 
gender equality has been given a tangible form in the research and development activities of a 
number of international organisations such as International Labour Organisation, 2019; 
Organisations for Economic Co-operation and Development, 2017; World Bank, 2019 and 
most notably the United Nations United Nations Development Programme, 2018; UN Women, 
2019). Therefore, this research study recognises that there is a need to enhance the “equality 
stool” for further research through the involvement of gendered energy and the Living Lab to 
develop the “Equality Stool”, which might provide the fourth wave of gender equality 
advocacy. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 36 
 
Moreover, the alternative routes to gender equity concerning energy security in impoverished 
informal settlements are based on a cognition that neither gender inequality nor energy poverty 
can be considered a distinct factor, but each factor is interlinked with making the decision 
(Clancy and Skutsch, 2003; O’Dell and Peters, 2014) problems and security of the energy 
services. There are efforts toward the implementation of gender equality in the energy sectors 
in sub-Saharan Africa (Musango et al., 2020). However, very little understanding of the 
relationship between the 'security of energy services', 'gender', 'poverty', and the 'Living Lab' 
exists (Dutta and Kooijman, 2017). This research study has proposed to cover this gap by 
investigating the relationship between those variables. 
2.2.5 Energy (in)security challenges and its gender-specific impacts 
Energy security concept evolved during the production of oil to the military in the 
unfortunate 20th century (Yergin et al., 2006). Energy security was also considered in the 
2000s during producing oil circulations from Asia to Europe (Yergin et al., 2006; Hughes, 
2016). Energy security in the earlyyears and recent years are not the same because of climate 
change mitigation and inability to access supply of energy services. As a result, continued 
effort is crucial in thoroughly re-considering the concept of energy security (Cherp & Jewell, 
2014). 
Writing in 2011 explaining the current state of the art, Sovacool has found about 45 definitions 
of energy security in the literature. Nonetheless, Parag (2014, p.99) operationalized energy 
security as “the adequate, reliable and competitive supply of energy”, including adequacy 
which is the “availability of sufficient energy to support economic and social activity”; 
reliability which is “the availability of energy with little interruption in supply”; and 
competitiveness which is “the availability of energy at a reasonable price that does not harm 
the competitiveness of the economy while supporting investment in energy sector. Some 
definitions also consist in the procedures defining the parameters and areas of the 
“environmental” or “equity” (International Energy Agency, 2011) and “public acceptability” 
(Jansen, 2009; B. Sovacool, 2011). According to Parag (2014) p.99, “Public acceptability is 
linked to social, psychological and cultural factors which make the supply of generation 
technology unpopular”. In the contrast, none of the definitions have included the notion of 
gender and the Living Lab that would encompass the issues of the energy security challenges. 
Similarly, at the level of the household where women are present, there is still conceptualisation 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 37 
 
and investigation of what energy security definition(s) should be and what energy security 
means (Musango et al., 2020). 
Barrett et al., (2010, p.4) argued for the necessity to broaden the extent of the analysis of energy 
security: “The level of security is not determined by supplies alone, but by the immediate 
balance between supply and demand and the longer-term trade-off between more energy 
security and environmental considerations (e.g., more wind farms vs. open spaces or more 
nuclear power vs. global security and nuclear proliferation)”. They indicated that there should 
be socio-technical and interdisciplinary methods to the thorough understanding of energy 
security, and these methods should consider the relationships between those engineering for 
the demand of energy and various energy security components (Parag, 2014). Therefore, this 
research study suggests that a thorough understanding of energy security in the poor urban 
informal settlement should require the situation of a socio-technical and user-oriented (women 
and girls) method that focuses on energy services, the security of energy services, and gender. 
This is because Pasqualetti and Sovacool (2012) establish that energy security and energy 
policy decisions’ importance must be viewed and advocated from the micro level to the 
household level. Energy security in the household means a steady and cheap supply of largely 
ubiquitous ‘clean energy fuel’ needed in performing activities in the households. However, up 
to date, there are few number of studies for defining the energy (in) security particularly at the 
level of the household (Musango et al., 2020). Hence, Hernández (2016, p.2) defined energy 
insecurity as the inefficiency which is experienced in the provision of adequate household 
energy necessities. Unauthorized sources also define energy insecurity of the household as “the 
status quo that resulted from the interplay of inadequate and insufficient energy consumption 
which does not meet the basic energy needs of a household”. They also defined energy security 
in the household as the ‘degree of energy required to support the fundamentals of daily life of 
the individual and the household in areas such as cooking, lighting, cleaning/washing and 
warming and cooling the house. These definitions focus mainly on the basic demand of society, 
that’s energy services consumption. 
Therefore, Shrestha et al. (2020) generated a framework for the research of the differences in 
gender for decision-making in the household. The framework underlined categories which are 
“subjective variables, objective variables, differences in reasons, differences in decision-
making, use, purchase, and action needed” (Musango et al., 2020, p.9). Therefore, this research 
study indicates this framework by Shrestha et al. (2020), and the variables could be used to 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 38 
 
develop a theory for gendered energy technology innovation in poor informa; settlements. 
Also, for the data collection purposes, Smit et al. (2019) developed a tool called the 
MuSIASEM tool that is used in the research study for generating data relating to gender 
equality indicators towards achieving security of energy services in the poor informal 
settlement. 
Furthermore, access to energy in informal settlements is a concern across Africa and this is due 
to high energy tropical, low accessibility of energy infrastructure and instability in supply 
(Yvonne Chivanga, 2023). All these constraints not only stifle growth and development of the 
economy but also have a more negative impact on members of the society’s vulnerable group, 
including women who are always charged with the responsibility of managing the compound’s 
energy (Pereira et al., 2020). Problems of energy security differ in informal urban settlements 
as they occupy peripheral status and lack proper physical infrastructure. Internal electricity 
constraint, as well as inadequate access to modern energy services puts the residents to rely on 
traditional and often unsafe technologies like kerosene lamps, and biomass fuels (Anditi et al., 
2022). These energy sources not only cause impersonal domestic air pollution but also are 
dangerous to health, particularly for women and children who most of the time are confined to 
the house (Yvonne Chivanga, 2023). 
With energy costs as an added expense, the challenges faced by the households in informal 
settlement are further compounded by the fact they have to pay for the energy they consume. 
Expensive energy services compared to household income limits the quantity of energy which 
the residents are able to afford leading to a large chunk of their income going into meeting their 
energy needs bare minimum (Choi and Kwon, 2021). This economic burden prevents families 
from obtaining resources and improving their educational systems, healthcare and other crucial 
services, thus causing more suffering by continuing poverty and impartment cycles. However, 
instead of availability, if the energy supply is unreliable then it creates another challenge to 
energy security in the informal settlements. Recurrent power blackouts and interruptions 
interfere with productivity and also pose a risk to safety; these interruptions affect users reliant 
on electricity for domestic chores like cooking, heating, and lighting (Følstad, 2008). Such 
disruption affects the daily tasks which are most often carried out by women and can include 
childcare and house chores hence, it has negative consequences on their wellbeing. 
Managing these energy security challenges therefore calls for a comprehensive approach, 
where the issues of gender are duly considered, and policies put in place to support this cause. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 39 
 
Musango et al., (2020) further emphasise the need for integration of gender issues for energy 
planning and policies in order to improve understanding and address specific energy demand 
and access challenges of the female gender. This includes offering the people the best 
renewable energy options that are cheap, available, and efficient, so that they are not 
constrained to using conventional fuels but have a sustainable way of generating power. 
Despite the lack of tailor-made methodologies for researching and implementing innovations, 
Living Labs holds a potential to provide suitable ground for testing and development of the 
energy solutions that address challenges faced in the informal settlements. These collaborative 
platforms support community, research and design thinking gathering, co-design processes and 
the engagement of community, researchers and policymakers (Grové, 2022). Women 
themselves can get empowered to engage in the innovation processes where Living Labs sit 
aptly with an ability to contextualise the attention and care required by organisations to enhance 
the creation of solutions and services that respond to these needs. South Africa summed up the 
possibilities of Living Labs for engaging in the issue of energy security complemented with 
participative processes. Herselman et al. (2015) reflect on and bring insights regarding Living 
Labs as Futures Centre in South Africa and reflect on the role of communities and localization 
of futures. Programs like the GENS Living Lab demonstrate the involvement of stakeholders 
in the advancement of empowering and gender-sensitive energy solutions (Sphokazi Phelokazi, 
2022). 
Besides the physical and economic barriers, social and cultural factors tend to worsen the 
energy security challenges experienced by women in the informal settlements. With this, 
customary patriarchal values and unfair gender standards deny women equal participation in 
the decision-making process or avenues of economic enfranchisement (Musango et al., 2020). 
This exclusion not only narrowly constrains their capabilities as standard bearers for the 
development of energy policies but also bears direct responsibility for perpetuating poverty 
and reliance on unsound energy systems in these nations. Remedial steps alongside these 
challenges can be correlated with steps that might facilitate Women Education, Women 
Empowerment and Women in Leadership within universally driven energy diplomacy and 
sustainable development projects (McDonald, 2015). Empowering women and achieving the 
related gender equality presents thousands of communities with a new chance on fostering 
further inventive efforts towards innovation in sustainable energy for worldwide society. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 40 
 
Furthermore, relating to the gender-specific impacts, several issues about energy security in 
the informal settlements are awaiting the women depending on their gender issues. Out of these 
challenges, the utilisation of traditional biomass for cooking and heating is one of the most 
significant sources of risks and likely to cause serious illness to women such as indoor air 
pollution Anditi et al., (2022). This is due to poor methods in cooking and lack of proper room 
airing whenever the population relies on biomass fuels which has contributed to respiratory 
illnesses and other ailments such as a result of inadequate ventilation especially affecting 
women who spend much of their time inside the house (Musango et al., 2020). Likewise, the 
female gender is more exercise-enhanced and vulnerable to health complications during 
biomass collection and preparation processes (von Geibler et al., 2014). 
Aside from the health concern, the utilisation of energy by women in informal settlements is 
hindered by time and mobility constraints. Electricity and other modern energy services are not 
available to many women; they spend much of their time in search of fuelwood or else waiting 
in a queue to purchase cooking fuel, which from a time perspective reduces the amount of time 
women can devote to income-generating activities or to acquire education (Hossain et al., 
2019). Also, insufficient lighting to common areas and other paths leads to numerous risks 
concerning personal security at night as well as restricting women’s access and exposes them 
to the increased likelihood of domestic abuse (Sphokazi Phelokazi, 2022). These mobility 
restrictions not only limit measures that can be taken to advance women’s economic status but 
also break the cycle that many women find themselves in where poverty and marginalisation 
become the norm. 
Mitigating these gender-specific impacts requires the promotion of women-centred approaches 
in energy innovation and policy-making processes. Campos and Marín-González (2023) posit 
that it is crucial to foster women’s engagement and inclusion in policies and planning initiatives 
that affect informal settlements, with consideration given to the gendered nature of encounters 
and resource necessities. This encompasses reaching out to women as partners to take charge 
in decision-making within the energy sector and devise solutions on how the challenges faced 
by women and their preferred solutions can be addressed (Herselman et al., 2015). 
Furthermore, the interventions must include focusing the market-based interventions on access 
to clean and affordable energy services; decreasing the reliance on traditional biomass, 
especially among women; and reducing the perceived health risks related to its use (Callaghan 
& Herselman, 2015). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 41 
 
Moreover, literacy or professional training programs can help women become capable of 
benefiting from enhanced technologies in the field of efficient energy usage. This would enable 
women to obtain the required training in energy-efficient ways of cooking, renewable energy 
systems and different business prospects in the renewable energy sector which can help women 
to refrain from using dirty fuels (Motloung, 2022). Furthermore, trying to install better lighting 
in the infrastructure, and to bring improved safety and security to public places may help to 
make women more mobile, to free them from the indoors and to include them into the society 
as active members (Følstad, 2008). 
Therefore, there are different energy security scales related to energy security issues, and these 
are workplace energy scale, national energy scale, household energy scale, and global energy 
scale (Pasqualetti and Sovacool, 2012). However, this research study is focused on the 
household energy scales which have emerging issues relating to gender (Pasqualetti and 
Sovacool, 2012), and the poor informal settlement (Kovacic et al., 2019a). Therefore, the focus 
should be on the security of energy services from energy security (Parag, 2014), which means 
that the focus should not only be on the end-users decision making in the supply or production 
of energy services but also the energy consumption rate of the end-users (Musango et al., 2020). 
The security of energy services which is focused on the end-users decision making was 
described by (Jansen, 2009) as “the extent to which the population in a defined area (country 
or region) can have access to affordable and competitively priced, environmentally acceptable 
energy services of adequate quality”. 
Moreover, this research study is suggesting that strategies for addressing energy security in the 
poor informal settlement towards increased effectiveness could be facilitated by the ‘product-
service systems innovations’ growth (Musango et al., 2020, p.7) and energy generation 
administration (Emili, Ceschin and Harrison, 2016). Thus, as the production and utilisation of 
the security of energy services could occur in various degrees, it would be imperative to engage 
everyone, form the end-users, stakeholders and other sections by using both the bottom-up and 
the top-down approaches (Parag, 2014). In this regard, the Living Lab technology would be 
employed to convene the stakeholders, the institutions as well as the end-users comprising of 
women and girls for the co-design and testing activities. They do so because both the end-users 
and the stakeholders have the aims to provide energy services (Parag, 2014). In their study, 
Musango et al. (2020, p.7) claim that “security of energy supplies is only one way of attaining 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 42 
 
security of energy services.” Hence this research concurs with the above presented statement 
and calls for consideration of energy services to the poor informal settlement. 
2.2.6 Gender-focused case studies of energy technology innovation 
This section describes the energy technologies relating to gender that could be investigated 
during this research study. Renewable energy technologies are one of the main examples of 
energy product distributions and for the provision of distinctive chances for the supply of 
indispensable energy services (Musango et al., 2020). However, to achieve the necessity of 
energy services at the level of the household, it is vital to focus on more than one energy 
technology. 
Therefore, to address energy insecurity, research studies have had the propensity to focus on 
one energy technology. These research studies include Pascale et al. (2016) for the 
investigation of ‘solar photovoltaic technology’ which was led by women in Myanmar rural 
areas to achieve electricity, (Ockwell et al., 2018b) for the focus on ‘solar photovoltaic 
technology’ to achieve energy services in evolving places, and Islam et al. (2005) for the 
development of a diminutive regionalized ‘renewable energy technology’ in evolving places. 
Other energy technologies are empirical research on the investigation of ‘cooking technologies’ 
(Goswami, Bandyopadhyay and Kumar, 2017), underpinning the supply and acceptance of 
optional ‘clean cooking technologies’ (Manjula and Gopi, 2017; Karanja and Gasparatos, 
2019), and (Castán Broto, Arthur and Guibrunet, 2020) for attention on traditional biomass 
(charcoal). Nevertheless, charcoal is well known and seen as the major fuel utilised in the urban 
informal settlements in Africa (Musango et al., 2020). These technologies in the literature 
above can impact gendered energy innovations in the urban informal settlements, and they are 
given attention to politics, economy, social-cultural applications, and technical features. 
Furthermore, the energy sector of Africa has witnessed rather profound changes in recent 
decades – the continent has turned to using renewable energy sources massively. This 
transformation is informed by the fact that Africa holds, for instance, solar, wind, and 
hydroelectric power and biomass very well; in addition to this, though, there is a concern about 
energy insecurity in the continent (Adulugba, 2021). Solar energy is, therefore, one of the 
brightest stars of sustainable power generation, and Africa, in particular, receives some of the 
highest irradiation levels in the world (Abdelrazik et al., 2022). This makes it suitable for solar 
electricity production, and many solar investment areas, including large-scale utility solar and 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 43 
 
distributed solar for off-grid villages, are observed in the country and many other African 
nations, including South Africa, Kenya, Morocco, etc (Agoundedemba, Kim and Kim, 2023). 
Wind energy is another strategic renewable energy source that holds the prospect for 
development in the African continent, especially in the areas bordering the coastal regions' 
highland climate areas. Hydropower has traditionally been used in Africa to provide renewable 
electricity with large, relatively tapped resources (Ugwu, Ozor and Mbohwa, 2022). The hydro 
potential of the African continent is considerable. So many big and small hydropower 
companies can invest in them, especially in the Congo, Nile, and Zambezi rivers. Other 
laudable projects, such as the Grand Ethiopian Renaissance Dam (GERD) and Inga Dam in the 
DRC, seek to increase power generation, making energy a strong pillar that underpins 
integration (Tesfa, 2013). 
In developing strategies to enhance gender equality in energy distribution, one needs to focus 
on methods that will capture the energy patterns and disparities faced by women across the 
globe. Some of the gender-focused energy technologies and programs that have been put in 
place to support and empower women in the energy sector include. The invention of the solar 
home system and the solar lantern, which eliminates the use of the kerosene lamp in the 
provision of artificial light and charging of mobile phones and other small electrical appliances 
(Aghaei et al., 2020). These systems greatly help women who live in rural settings where they 
often use kerosene lamps and candles for illumination. These are the positive impacts of Solar 
Home Systems on women where they can save a lot of their time, be safer in collecting fuel, 
and, thus, make them more productive (Beyene et al., 2024). These strategies include solar 
programs, which involve training women to act as solar sales ladies, which enhances both the 
provision of solar-aided technologies and empowers women economically. 
Clean cookstoves are another smart improvement aimed at enhancing the effectiveness of 
cooking processes and lessening the emission of dangerous substances. Based on statistics, 
barbecuing or boiling foods on open fires or using simple stoves hurts the air quality within 
homes; these mostly affect women and children (Rybak and Pieśniewska, 2024). Clean 
cookstoves require less fuel, release fewer pollutants, and take less time to cook compared to 
the traditional three-stone stove; therefore, the survey showed improved women's health was 
achieved through clean stoves since they had more time on their hands (Phillip et al., 2023). 
Biogas availability has an added advantage to females since it conserves the time used in 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 44 
 
sourcing fuel and cooking (Tekle and Sime, 2022). They also enhance efforts that empower 
women to engage actively in biogas production and sale as a source of income. 
Therefore, mini-grids and off-grid solar solutions remain relevant in supplying electricity to 
rural and un-electrified populations (Asuamah, Gyamfi and Dagoumas, 2021). These small-
scale energy systems provide a steady power supply for lighting, communication, and 
productive functions. Thus, mini-grids facilitate electricity access that helps women to go to 
school, start income-generating activities, and engage in social activities, among others (Duran 
and Sahinyazan, 2020). Mini-grid enterprises related to women are being designed under 
gender mainstreaming, which has been encouraged by institutions like the African 
Development Bank (AfDB), and the formulation of mini-grid designs targets women 
(Antonanzas-Torres et al., 2021). 
Biogas and biomass initiatives remain critical to delivering clean cooking initiatives and 
producing electricity from bio-wastes, especially in rural and peri-urban Kenya, where access 
to modern energy is still limited (Tolessa et al., 2020). People in most rural areas use biomass 
fuel for cooking, leading to deforestation and indoor pollution. Improved biogas technology 
involves using animal dung, agricultural waste, and other organic matter to produce biogas for 
cooking, thus reducing the use of biomass fuel, which is the main cause of deforestation (Omer, 
2021). Despite Africa being endowed with this natural resource, geothermal energy is still in 
its early stages of exploitation and exploration. Still, its prospect is great in the East African 
Rift Valley. Some examples of countries utilising geothermal energy are Kenya and Ethiopia, 
which use it for electrical power production and direct uses (Pasqua, Chiozzi and Verdoya, 
2023). In the category of renewable energy, Kenya is one of the most progressive countries in 
the region, with projects such as the Olkaria Geothermal Power Station that help ensure a 
constant and viable energy supply (Kipsielei, 2020). 
Alternatively, looking at case studies of successful implementations; for the South African 
context – a well-documented example is Solar Sister. Solar Sister is a constructive business 
model that targets women and utilises them as clean energy vendors (Cañas, Ceron and 
Solodka, 2020). In South Africa, Solar Sister identifies, empowers, enriches, and engages 
female climate heroes who market solar products and clean cookstoves in their regions (Raimi, 
Dodo and Sule, 2022). It is also important to note that the initiative is geared towards helping 
women build new business ventures besides supplying clean energy. The Solar Sister works as 
an entrepreneur who gets income from selling solar products, meaning they can be accorded 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 45 
 
better income and social standing. This program has been able to help so many households 
with clean and reliable energy, and this has been achieved. 
Another successful implementation is using biogas technology in rural South African 
communities to address energy poverty and consequently support the communities’ sustainable 
development. The biogas digester technology is implemented through the Integrated Rural 
Development and Nature Conservation (IRDNC) project, which was developed to tackle 
environmental issues in the Eastern Cape province of South Africa (Thilivhali Eugene 
Rasimphi et al., 2022). This process involves animal manure and organic wastes being 
converted into special biogas that ease cooking, reducing the use of extremely harmful 
firewood and improving household air quality (Obaideen et al., 2022). Overall, women claim 
improved time use and health effects, alongside enhancements in roles and responsibilities 
engaged in income-generating activities. 
Therefore, it is important to investigate the dynamics of gender around the supply chain of 
energy technology and energy access. It is indicated that women like to be involved in the 
traditional biomass (charcoal), thereby becoming oppositions in the other energy supply chain 
and becoming part of the energy sector as the non-attendance alternative income opportunists 
(Ihalainen, Schure and Sola, 2020). Furthermore, Musango et al. (2020, p.9) indicated that 
“gender differences and inequalities influence participation and outcomes in areas such as 
resources and income, gender roles and responsibilities, and social and political capital”. 
Therefore, there is a need for the inclusivity of gender as an important aspect of the energy 
supply chain (Ihalainen, Schure, and Sola, 2020). 
Furthermore, most research studies have investigated the gender approach for the acceptance 
of clean energy (Aryal et al., 2019). This study by Aryal et al observed that “the use of clean 
energy in female-headed households tended to be lower than in male-headed households, and 
such observations have implications for gendered innovations” (Musango et al., 2020, p.9). 
Also, there is a framework that examines the effect of electricity access on women's 
empowerment (Winther et al., 2017). They indicated that there is a lesser understanding of the 
effect of electricity access related to ‘gender’ even though both men and women benefit from 
electricity. There is an observation by Rosenthal et al. (2018) in Musango et al. (2020) that 
cooking technology happens to be the main and often energy challenges for women, however, 
this assumption is unintentional and unproved. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 46 
 
Therefore, the “implication is the need to consider all household energy service requirements 
as a bundle that can be satisfied by various energy technologies and that requires an 
understanding of the roles of both women and men as well as their knowledge contributions,” 
(Musango et al., 2020, p.10). Although focusing on one energy technology could hamper the 
benefit of the ‘socio-economic’ position of women (Musango et al., 2020), this research study 
focuses on all household's energy services requirements, also paying huge attention to the clean 
cooking technologies through biogas technology. Although, the biogas technology has been 
adopted for this study, the focus of the research was not on the biogas as a new innovation, but 
the biogas digester was developed in this research study as a technology to further validate or 
test the new SETILL theoretical model focused on gender design. Therefore, the research study 
would endeavour to design the biogas technology to a pilot scale using the bottom-up energy 
innovation decision-making approach with a focus more on women in the selected case study.  
2.2.7 Indicators of energy poverty 
Various definitions of energy poverty have been investigated by different researchers 
throughout the world (Team and Baffert, 2015; Day, Walker and Simcock, 2016). However, 
universally, there is no accepted definition of energy poverty recently (Sadath and Acharya, 
2017). According to Kohler, Rhodes, and Vermaak (2014), energy poverty was defined as the 
absence of access to any kind of present-day energy services which could include cooking 
fuel/heating and electricity. Therefore, this research study would adopt the definition of energy 
poverty by the International Energy Association (IEA) which described energy poverty as the 
absence of present-day, clean and economical energy services that could mainly involve clean 
provisions in households and electricity (United Nations, 2014b). 
The phrase “fuel poverty” is referred to as an absence of heating fuels and the phrase “energy 
poverty” is referred to as the absence of electricity in developing countries, however, both fuel 
poverty and energy poverty tend to describe energy impoverishment in the household and 
industries. The energy poverty in research and policies champions several results and vital 
consequences. These include health problems arising from the use of biomass, air pollution in 
an enclosed place, time wastage, average incomes and productivity, lack of education for the 
women and girls because they are always available fetching firewood and above, and gender 
inequality (Day, Walker and Simcock, 2016). There is energy poverty in rural informal 
settlements and globally. This is evident due to the level of energy in the rural areas, where 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 47 
 
billions of the poor informal settlers do not have access to dependable and cleaner energy 
services. 
Therefore, to control energy poverty, there should be dependable access to present-day cleaner 
and economical energy services in the informal settlements which are vital in achieving the 
Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) (WHO, 2010). However, for alleviating energy 
poverty, there is a need to understand the alliance of energy services with gender equality, 
which has been discussed above and is geared towards what this research study is advocating 
for in the informal settlement. The major and common present-day energy services required at 
household level include cooking technologies, lighting, and heating. These are further detailed 
in Table 2.1, and, at household level, these energy services can contribute to increased well-
being and quality of life for the poor settlers and most especially to the women and girls.

Furthermore, several methods and indicators have been used to quantify energy poverty 
(AGECC, 2010; Nussbaumer et al., 2013), where some consider the opportunity of access to 
present-day energy services (Day, Walker and Simcock, 2016), and some of them assessed the 
overall usage of energy by describing the least bearable level against the real usage of energy 
on the same level with the goods and services (Practical Action, 2010). There are various 
adopted ways to daily quantify the rate of energy use at the household level (Mirza and Szirmai, 
2010; Bazilian et al., 2012). Table 2.2 illustrates the guideline approaches for the rate of energy 
usage that could be employed at the household level to describe the lease energy level usage. 
The United Nations advised to target 1200kWh per person for a year (AGECC, 2010), and 
approximately 100 kilograms of oil (Kgoe) equal to present-day fuels, and 100 kWh of 
electricity, South Africa government also advised approximately 600 kWh for each household 
annually, while the Indian government advised for 365 kWh for each household annually 
(Pachauri, 2011).
