Furthermore, there are more approaches like the empirical evaluation of the standard energy 
usage of the households who consumes less than the earnings poverty rate established from the 
obtained data that could be used to derive the least energy patterns (Barnes, Khandker and 
Samad, 2010), and the Multidimensional Energy Poverty Index (MEPI) by Nussbaumer et al 
(2013) which is used to quantify the lack of energy in the division of 5 parameters (e.g., 
telecommunications, lighting, education, cooking and services of appliances). This MEPI 
indicator mentioned has been adopted to achieve quantification of energy consumptions by 
Alkire and Santos (2014); Sher, Abbas, and Awan (2014); Nussbaumer et al (2013); Alkire, 
Adriana Conconi and Seth (2014); Sadath and Acharya (2017). Other approaches or indicators 
are the Energy Indicators for Sustainable Development (EISD) (Streimikiene, Ciegis and 
Grundey, 2007; Vera and Langlois, 2007) and Energy Development Index (EDI) (Iddrisu and 
Bhattacharyya, 2015), which have demonstrated applicability. 
Therefore, this section was used to briefly explain some energy poverty indicators and their 
influence on energy security services. Although most of the indicators have failed to quantify 
performances, inform policy designs (McGillivray, 1991; Ravallion, 2012) and include gender, 
it is necessary to explain these indicators and regulate the least fundamental energy services 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 51 
 
while relating them to the socio-ecological characteristics, cultural and the aspects of gender 
in a particular context. 
2.2.8 Supporting energy access sustainability 
Theoretically, the energy steps illustrate the interrelationship between the kind of energy 
consumptions, energy income, and the transitional switch (Rahut, Behera and Ali, 2017). It is 
hypothesised that the increase in the household income leads to an increase in the household 
energy consumption shifts from the less traditional energy shape to additional present-day 
energy security services like electricity and liquid fuels. This does not stop the access to the 
traditional biomass and cleaner alternative forms of energy by the poor informal settlement 
even with the increase in income. 
In consultation with the energy steps concept, availability, energy standard and cost come from 
the consumption of traditional solid fuels, down to cleaner liquid fuels (gas and kerosene), then 
lastly to electricity consumption. However, the electricity consumption is common in 
advantageous households, while the poor households are common with the fuelwood and dung 
consumption rate. According to Day, Walker and Simcock (2016), energy services access is 
defined in a descriptive step in figure 2.3, which shows the ‘energy-arising ladder from scarcity 
to the development levels and this was developed by the IEA. As the wealth of the household 
levels increases, the rate of clean energy increases. Therefore, this illustrates the idea of the 
‘energy ladder’ showing that the present-day access to energy services provides a better level 
of energy services with reduced negative effects. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 52 
 
Figure 2.3: Incremental levels of access to present-day energy services. Source: AGECC 
(2010) in Bazilian et al. (2010, p.5). 
Although Gross Domestic Product (GDP) has been used as a standard to quantify energy access 
globally, it has disregarded the efficacy of the capital assets’ devaluation, satisfaction, and 
gender. South Africa as a nation has the highest Gross Domestic Product and energy access, 
however, there are still visible gender inequalities in the poor urban and rural areas, including 
among the rich and poor because of the huge impact of the laws of apartheid. It is required to 
involve both the quantitative and qualitative approaches to the energy access metrics. However, 
with constant examinations, and connection with the Gross Domestic Product (DGP), energy 
access breaches a lot of quantitative illustrations and concepts related to energy poverty 
(Khennas, 2012). 
It is of no doubt that the significance of having access to sustainable and present-day energy 
services is vital for bringing solutions to the challenges of energy poverty, climate change, and 
gender inequalities in the poor urban informal settlements. Most oil-producing countries in 
Africa (e.g., Nigeria), have energy prospects but lack full access to present-day energy forms 
like Liquid gas, natural gas, and most importantly, electricity (Khennas, 2012). This shows that 
energy prospects are not mostly the main factor for energy access, even though it is vital in 
increasing energy access, just like gender equality is also vital in increasing energy access. 
Energy prospects, just as the development of infrastructure are vital for better governance and 
sustainable development. Therefore, there should be energy access for all which is connected 
to gender equality leading to sustainable development in poor informal settlements. Therefore, 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 53 
 
providing equal energy access in the urban and rural areas has a link with the political economy 
and gender equality, because the lack of electricity supply in these areas mostly have to do with 
inequality between the poor and the rich, and between the women and the men. Therefore, 
policies should be merged with frameworks and models to support the poor, bring about 
gendered technology innovation in the informal settlements, and proffer subsidy reformation. 
This has a goal of attaining the United Nations idea of having global energy access by 2030 
(Rehman et al., 2012). Therefore, gender equality and the aspect of political economy are the 
factors to quantify the development of energy access according to this study. 
Furthermore, actions to advance and improve access to energy service and reduce energy 
poverty are declining with time and with this backdrop, the potential of developing access to 
energy services for all in Africa could be unfeasible by 2030. However, Rehman et al (2012). 
has indicated that the key drivers of energy access at the household level are in the shape of 
political economy and pointed out concerns about the way which it could be used to advance 
energy access. However, these concerns lack the aspects of gender, and this research study 
aims to cover this gap. 
2.2.9 Women empowerment: participation of women in energy technology innovation 
There is a saying by Melinda Gates (2019, p.28) that “if you search for poverty, you will find 
women who don’t have power, and if you explore prosperity, you will find women who have 
power and use it”. Some energy research studies classify gender equality and gender challenges 
with women (Fathallah and Pyakurel, 2020), and SDG 5 also directs the issue of gender to 
women and girls. However, Musango et al. (2020, p.8) indicated that “gender does not refer to 
women or differences between men and women”, but that gender is an avenue for the 
clarification and conceptualisation of the supposed biological gendered dissimilarities in a 
certain circumstance (Nightingale, 2006). 
Therefore, empowering women in the energy industry and household is inevitable. This is as 
explained earlier that the impartial statistics on women in the positions of leadership, energy 
business, finance, politics, and the gender pay gap indicates that there is a long way to go on 
the concept of gender equality, and it is more difficult when it involves women of colour 
(Gillard and Okonjo-Iweala, 2020). Socialisation and stereotyping are some of the factors that 
form women and men including their participation in co-designing of energy technologies. 
However, this research study aims to do more than checking on the stereotyping of women and 
men and focus more on the technological, political, and structural barriers that limit women 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 54 
 
from being part of the decision-making during the design and testing phases of the energy 
technology innovations. These barriers which are going to be investigated in this research study 
are seen in this study as a challenge, as it prevents women from progressing in the energy 
sectors and households for the achievement of energy security. 
When women are actively involved in energy innovation processes, they improve the chances 
of creating positive change that is effective and diversified to suit the interests of most people. 
Studies have shown that incorporating female voices means occupying a seat that comes with 
fresh ideas, and uncovered knowledge and vantage points that improve the energy sector’s 
behind-the-scenes work (Habiyaremye, 2019). Thus, adopting gender mainstreaming could 
ensure more extensive engagement of women in Living Labs projects, apply their perspectives 
and expertise for co-creating energy innovations not only for the benefit of the participants but 
also for the improvement of the energy framework (Fortiş & Petcu, 2022). 
In most uses of energy, the role of women is either hidden, neglected or not given its deserved 
attention. The literature has established that women have a distinct understanding and 
information concerning energy consumption, control, and requirement as pertains to their 
homes and societies (Anditi et al., 2022). Through engaging in the energy’s innovation 
processes, such actors can assist in the discovery of other solutions that are more likely to be 
effective, affordable or suitable in the light of the target customers, especially those in the 
developing rural settlements whose access to energy is limited. 
The implementation of gender-mainstreaming in energy innovation requests conscious 
attempts to remove system barriers to women’s engagement and their contributions recognized 
and valued. According to Callaghan and Herselman (2015), it is also critical for Women’s 
Voice to ensure that the female gender feels empowered to express herself in Living Labs. It 
may consist of empowering women to take up innovation by offering training and capacity 
enhancement projects meant for women, and challenging gendered norms that decrease 
women’s engagement in innovation and a call to female engagement in leadership standings of 
advancement innovations. 
According to Choi and Kwon (2021), women must participate in energy innovation not only 
as a right or a fight for women but due to the need for development goals. Focusing women’s 
creativity and expertise, the energy sector can offer the solutions that would be more sensitive 
to the needs and priorities of the communities in terms of the access to energy and its positive 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 55 
 
impact on the environment and society. Moreover, gender diversifying has been found to 
increase organisational creativity and improve performance in tackling problems; therefore, 
imposing gender equity in energy innovation solutions (Følstad, 2008). 
Using the case of South Africa, this study identifies key factors that may inform the future 
positive impacts of women’s involvement in energy innovation. More recently, Grové (2022) 
establishes the power that Living Labs offer in identifying and supporting equitable female 
contributions in new disruptive technologies, as such sharing ways through which women can 
enhance the usability of digital environments for all users. In the same way, Herselman et al. 
(2015) reflect on scientific cooperations with Living Labs in South Africa and Tanzania and 
stress the role of diversification and involvement of more people within society by 
implementing positive change. 
Furthermore, a research study has shown that a man is appointed to the board of directors for 
balance, but when there is lower performance, a woman would be appointed (Gillard and 
Okonjo-Iweala, 2020). This means that having women participate in any energy technology 
innovation is inevitable. This research study is about energy security and gender equality. 
Therefore, using that word means describing the socially established attributes that are assigned 
to women and men and their interrelationships and potentials. There is more in common with 
gender and energy even though they vary in regions and culture, hence, all types of exclusions 
in the energy industries and households should be studied, considered and solutions should be 
provided. This research study does not intend to cover this type of problem, however, gender 
issues and women empowerment in the energy sectors and technology innovations should be 
considered. 
Empowering women can be a vital factor for ending extreme poverty and extreme poverty 
could mean to be left behind by the stakeholders and those who are supposed to uplift us (Gates, 
2019). Therefore, if we want to tackle poverty in the energy sector and the informal settlement, 
we can do that by empowering women. 
Research studies have indicated that women could benefit immensely when there is 
improvement or growth in access to electricity (Rosenberg et al., 2020). Therefore, the issue 
of women being included in the design phases and the testing phases of energy technology 
innovation should be of paramount interest if we intend to achieve energy security in the poor 
informal settlement. Furthermore, in energy studies, a research study by (Winther et al., 2017; 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 56 
 
Ihalainen, Schure and Sola, 2020) has identified gender equality with women/girls which is 
grounded to their research study. Another research study also gave attention to women with a 
disarticulated investigation of gender (Kim and Standal, 2019; Clancy and Mohlakoana, 2020). 
Another research has also indicated that there is a need for generating a new idea about gender 
equality in energy applications and research (Ryan, 2014; Bradshaw, 2018). Most research 
studies have also focused on the proper arrangement of institutional bodies for better decision-
making and participation at the household level (Permana, Aziz and Siong, 2015; Shrestha et 
al., 2020). 
Furthermore, the theory of Gender and Development, which emerged as a post-modern 
framework during the late 20th century (1980s-1990s), follows the earlier Women in 
Development approach. Distinguishing itself from previous models such as Women in 
Development (1960s-1970s), Women and Development (1970s-1980s), and Women, Culture, 
and Development (1990s-2000), the Gender and Development perspective uniquely 
encompasses the requirements and perspectives of both genders, acknowledging the 
interdependent relationships across genders (March et al., 1999; Cornwall, 2000 and Jaquette, 
2017). This aligns with the current study's objective to shift the perception of gender advocacy 
from a solely pro-women stance towards fostering gender equality within the context of energy 
innovation initiatives. 
The theory of Gender and Development acknowledges the importance of empowering women 
during periods of cultural change (Singh, 2007), understanding that the range of needs 
encompasses both strategic and practical aspects (UNDP, 2001). The concept of empowerment 
is complex and multifaceted, requiring recognition of the interplay between different genders. 
Johnson (2015) posits that gender empowerment encompasses areas such as education, 
equality, self-assurance, and the maintenance of cultural heritage. True empowerment should 
not lead to the erosion of cultural identity; rather, it should be woven into the existing norms 
and values of the community being uplifted. 
Gender and Development provides a framework to comprehend the systemic constitution of 
socio-cultural norms, facilitating suitable change. Consequently, diversity is seen as a boon 
rather than a barrier to progress (Aditi, 2021). The energy landscape within informal 
settlements is expected to be varied, especially given the swift urban growth observed in many 
African cities, including examples from Kenya and South Africa (Falkingham et al., 2012 and 
Njambe et al., 2019). Nonetheless, commonalities persist across different energy cultures, such 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 57 
 
as the central role of women in managing household energy needs (Petrova and Simcock, 
2019), despite the evolving nature of roles and the questioning of traditional practices. The 
study of Gender and Development requires a deep dive into various situations, primarily using 
qualitative research methodologies which is crucial for recognizing and effectively tackling 
technology adoptions related to gender, as well as for assessing the advancements of initiatives 
(Jaquette, 2017). It's essential to carefully approach the mitigation of energy poverty, with a 
focus on achieving gender equality. Such efforts are key to dismantling stereotypes that hinder 
efforts towards women empowerment  (Jaquette, 2017 and Pearson, 2000). 
Therefore, this research study argues for the inclusion of a body of knowledge in the energy 
sectors, instead of focusing on fixing ‘institutions’ and ‘numbers’ as some of the studies 
mentioned above and aim to bring about a standard method for the participation of the various 
genders in co-designing and testing of the energy technology innovation in the poor informal 
settlement. This is because literature has shown that there are limited methods for the support 
of important measures for incorporating gender concepts (Musango et al., 2020).  
Resilience in energy services 
The study of resilience is very important to this research study as it has been studied as an 
important aspect in the energy security literature (McPherson, Wood, and Robinson, 2005; 
Pascual, 2010; Skea, Ekins and Winskel, 2012; Sovacool, 2011b). The concept of resilience 
has been studied in different disciplines like disaster risk studies and disaster risk reduction, 
where the mechanisms influencing the level of resilience of the communities vulnerable to 
disasters were investigated (Onyeagoziri, 2020).  
The ability to overcome, survive, and proffer solutions to energy insecurity and bring about 
gender equality in the energy technology innovations is what is referred to in this research 
study as the ‘poor rural resilience’. A research study has examined ways to make cities resilient 
and efficient in energy services and indicated that historic cities could be a huge laboratory for 
studying resilience (Salat and Bourdic, 2011). Therefore, the rural informal settlement could 
be part of a huge Living Lab for the investigation of end-users (women), gendered energy 
technology innovations, and energy security.  
Furthermore, a research study in the context of energy defined resilience of energy systems as 
the “ability to tolerate disturbances and to continue to deliver affordable energy services to 
consumers” (Chaudry et al., 2009, p.4). Resilience in the energy study is often referred to as 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 58 
 
the attribute of a system (Parag, 2014). Therefore, “a resilient energy system can speedily 
recover from shocks” (e.g. short-term interruption in electricity supply) and can “provide 
alternative means of satisfying energy service needs in the event of changed external 
circumstances” (Chaudry et al., 2009, p.4). Poor rural complexity impacts are not only due to 
the rural informal settlement energy productivity. Although rural informal settlement resilience 
is a difficult concept to come by, it is seen as one of the vital factors in the future concerning 
climate change and it is understood to be the ability to overcome shocks and catastrophe, 
therefore, it is vital and worth to investigate how the issues of the resilience of the informal 
settlement are related to security of energy services (Salat and Bourdic, 2011). 
Therefore, relating to the conceptualisation of energy security, resilience is seen as a ‘societal’ 
attribute that includes both the energy suppliers (stakeholders) and the energy consumers (end-
users). This has a stronghold to this research study as it shows that the research study is also 
linked to building resilience in the energy systems that will involve the concept of gender 
equality, systems Living Labs and participation of women as end-users for co-designing and 
testing of the energy technology (Parag, 2014). This means that for energy services to be 
secured in the rural informal settlement, there should be an identification of the various actors 
who will play various roles leading to a proper response to interruptions or shocks in the energy 
services. The next section represents the research focus which explains the energy Living Lab, 
leading to the Systems Living Lab. 
 
2.3 
Level 2: Research focus 
In level 2, the question to ask is: what is the researcher looking for? The answer to this question 
lies in identifying the key underlying concepts of the research focus. The research focus is to 
understand the underlying mechanisms influencing the level of energy technology innovation, 
adoption of gender and access to energy services using the Living Lab. The key underlying 
concept of the research focus is gendered energy technology innovation Living Lab. The 
overarching concept is ‘Energy Living Lab’ with gender design, using systems approach.  
The Living Lab idea came in the 1990s to advance ICT innovation, which creates an 
environment where users and stakeholders can scrutinise and co-design innovation in an 
interdisciplinary manner and towards a specific interest, suggesting that the central part of the 
Living Labs are open innovation, co-designing, end-user driven and multidisciplinary 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 59 
 
(Callaghan and Herselman, 2015). The research focus (SETILL) identified is further discussed 
and developed in the chapter 3 of this study. 
2.3.1 The 
Living 
Lab 
definitions 
The Living Lab started to come to the limelight in early 2000 by (Markopoulos, 2000), whose 
purpose then was to try new technologies in the household erected environments. The concept 
or ideas of Living Labs has since advanced, and currently, Living Lab is found in a real-world 
context (Bergvall-Kåreborn, Holst and Ståhlbröst, 2009; Mukama et al., 2022). There is no 
standardised definition of a Living Lab concept. Thus, many definitions of the Living Lab exist, 
but for this research study, some of the identified selected definitions provided an initial 
understanding of the concept, thus, throughout the study, many definitions of the Living Lab 
will be identified. Relating to this study, the Living Lab has been defined in chapter 3 of this 
study. 
Bergvall-Kåreborn et al. (2009:1) defined the Living Lab as “an environment in which people 
and technology are gathered and in which the everyday context and user need stimulate and 
challenge both research and developments since authorities and citizens take active part in the 
innovation process.” Bergvall-Kåreborn et al. (2009) definition seems to highlight aspects 
similar to the focus of this proposed research, although the definition was not specific to the 
end-users. For this research study, the end-users will include women. 
Furthermore, one of the purposes of the Living Lab is to generate a shared sector in which 
energy designs, innovations, services, and new processes are developed and tested with the 
involvement of the end-users (women) and researchers. Therefore, the Living Lab, as defined 
by Santoro and Conte (2016:39),  is “open innovation environments in real-life settings, in 
which user-driven innovation is fully integrated within the co-creation process of new services, 
products, and societal infrastructures.” Similarly, Santoro and Conte (2016) definition have 
elements similar to the research focus. 
Herselman et al. (2009:6) gave an extensive definition of the Living Lab, which they consider 
the terms are fundamental as the “Living Labs are environments, an approach, user-driven, 
open innovation, collaborative and engagement of various stakeholders.” The part of ‘user-
driven’ and ‘open innovation’ is an essential aspect of this proposed research focus. However, 
the part of being an ‘approach’ is out of the context of this research study which was why 
further studies were done and realised that CoreLabs (2007) defined the Living Lab as a system, 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 60 
 
Eriksson (2006) defined the Living Lab as a methodology or approach, while Ballon and 
Pierson, (2005); Schaffers et al. (2007) defined the living Lab as an environment. The 
researcher does not see these three definitions as oppositions but regards them to be compatible 
with the researcher's viewpoints. This is because the viewpoints of the definitions depend 
totally on the focus and context of the study. Therefore, the Living Lab as a system brings about 
the concept of the Living Lab used in this research study. This concept is the use of systems 
approach for the development of energy Living Lab focused on gender designs. Therefore, for 
the purpose of this study, that Living Lab is called ‘systems energy technology innovation 
Living Lab (SETILL). This SETILL was developed in chapter 3 of this study. 
Bergvall-Kåreborn et al. (2009) explained that the ‘environment viewpoint’ is made up of items 
like the technological programs and end-user societies, while the ‘methodology viewpoint’ is 
made up of the movement of data and the techniques for involving end-users. On the other 
hand, the ‘system viewpoint’ is made up of the connection or interaction between the Living 
Lab and specific different parts. In this research study, the focus is on the systems viewpoint. 
However, the environmental viewpoint and the methodology viewpoint are also very essential 
in this research study as the phenomenon being studied also relates to these viewpoints. 
2.3.2 The 
Living 
Lab 
concepts 
Living Lab is an active and effective concept of research and development where the actual 
user of the product is involved in the process of development (Choi and Kwon, 2021). Being 
open innovation platforms focused on the users, Living Labs offers a systematic approach of 
co-creation that involves the combination of research and innovation activities in the real 
contexts of utilisation (Fortiş and Petcu, 2022). This methodology ensures that not only are the 
solutions that are being developed workable because they serve a developmental function, but 
they are also realistic and feasible, given their organisational applicability. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 61 
 
 
Figure 2.4: The Living Lab process. Source: Vicini et al. (2012:203) 
Figure 2.4 depicts the process of Living lab according to Vicini et al. (2012) outlining the 
necessary stages and interactions for creating the innovative technology in the Living Lab 
context: which further explains that the core principle underlying a Living Lab is to obtain 
direct and unmediated insights from users ideas, knowledge and experiences which is an 
approach that focuses on their everyday needs and aspirations for support through various 
products, services, or applications. In the frame of reference of Living Labs provided by Huang 
and Thomas (2021), it is worth mentioning that Living Labs emphasise the importance of 
participation and are inclusive of various forms of actors like the users, researchers, developers, 
policy makers, and business actors. This is critical due to the fact that creation, prototyping, 
validation, and iteration of innovative solutions for a wide range of challenges are best achieved 
through cooperation. Living Labs brings things a step further and directly engages end-users 
in the innovation process to ensure that the products that are being developed meet the users’ 
requirements (Herselman et al., 2015). 
Living Labs are designed on the understanding that the environment of actual life is the most 
suitable for evaluating new technologies and particular solutions (Hossain, Leminen and 
Westerlund, 2019). This approach is hence different from traditional research and development 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 62 
 
strategies conducted in conventional research laboratories. For example, in a specified area of 
a Living Lab, the community becomes the place where the new solutions will be tested, 
providing a better understanding of the use and functioning of the innovation. It is necessary 
for this task to be conducted in real-life scenarios for purposes of realising available problem 
areas and fine-tuning of the solutions with respect to improved efficacy and uptake among its 
users. Living Labs initiated from the idea that innovation is a social process which involves all 
stakeholders. According to Ahmed, McGough and Mateo-Garcia (2017), Living Labs establish 
a place where stakeholders can collaborate: using their knowledge and experiences. 
User co-creation is also an important aspect when it comes to defining and implementing a 
Living Lab. This includes seeking the involvement of end-users right from the identification 
of the technology idea up to the development of the final technology product. In this way, 
Living Labs can improve upon the existing solutions, by designing and adapting solutions 
according to the user’s experiences and concerns shared by the target group. Precisely this 
focus within users not only makes the innovations more relevant and easier to use but also 
increases the probability of the adoption of these innovations. 
Also, as a result of their philosophy, Living Labs encourages an iterative development approach 
(Huang and Thomas, 2021). Solutions are gradually iterated when users give feedback or when 
it is determined that solutions do not perform as expected in real life (Callaghan and Herselman, 
2015). This cyclical process makes it possible to develop solutions at a very fast rate while they 
are tested and improved as the process continues to the end and the final solutions have to stand 
as the best. It is most effective in situations where there are many subtle factors involved such 
as a complex problem that needs many contemporary and varied solutions to resolve it. Living 
Labs also play the role of a connector between various sectors and disciplines and therefore 
enable interdisciplinary cooperation (Leal Filho et al., 2022). Since Living Labs involve people 
from different disciplines like technology, social sciences and policy, problem solving can be 
viewed and tackled from various perspectives at the same time as opposed to traditional 
perspectives where a problem is solved strictly from a single angle (Tercanli and Jongbloed, 
2022). This is a relevant approach since issues like energy poverty are social problems, which 
also have a technical and economic component. 
Referring to the areas of energy technology innovation, Living Labs creates the possibility to 
build and install solutions directly into contexts (Mbatha and Musango, 2022). Living Labs can 
increase accountability by involving community stakeholders, especially women, during the 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 63 
 
innovative process to guarantee energy solutions that look into the needs and limitations of the 
informal settlements (Sphokazi Phelokazi, 2022). This approach ensures that the solutions that 
are provided are easy to use and will help the said communities achieve proper energy access 
and security (Fortiş and Petcu, 2022). Furthermore, gender equality may be a key priority for 
Living Labs when it comes to energy innovation (Botreau & Cohen, 2020). When women are 
included in the innovation process that Living Labs brings, as users and actors, access to them 
in the innovation process can guarantee that women’s voices and needs are being heard. This 
is in conformity with the principles of the reviewed United Nations’ Sustainable Development 
Goal 5 (SDG5), which envisions the promotion of gender equality and women’s fair treatment 
(Giannetti et al., 2020). 
The next section highlights basic Living Labs concepts as applied in a different context, which 
is useful to inform the conceptualisation of Living Labs in the energy context in poor informal 
settlements. 
Basic 
Living 
Lab 
concepts 
in 
the 
ICT 
The Living Lab is famous around the world, and the possible reason for this could be because 
of their connection with the ICTs because most Living Labs are ICT compliance (Jere, 
Kauhonina and Gamundani, 2014). The Living Lab and ICT are seen as the centre of innovation 
in several societies and several countries, including South Africa, and these countries are 
showing innovations by the use of the Living Lab concept to attain continuous technological 
changes, and the process that has been used is the organisation of workshops to educate people 
on the Living Lab concepts (Jere et al., 2014). However, the Living Lab concept has been there 
in the ICT sector for a while now, but of course still new in the energy area, hence not much 
energy Living Labs in existence. 
The credit of the Living Lab concept is given to William J. Mitchell of the Massachusetts 
Institute of Technology (MIT) as a result of his idea for moving several kinds of research from 
the Laboratories to a wired building to monitor end-users interconnection with innovations in 
the year 2000s (Guldemond and Geenhuizen, 2012). By the use of open participation between 
several stakeholders, the Living Lab does not recommend any particular business or technology 
model; instead, it pays attention to the abduction of the value of technology depending on the 
practicality (Eriksson et al., 2016). The value abducted is done at the user's and stakeholder's 
levels. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 64 
 
Living Labs approaches can be used in several areas, like the urban and rural regions 
(geographical locations) and the ICT for energy efficiency and food security (Thematic stance) 
(Geerts, 2011). This statement is vital to this research study as it fulfils the need for the 
applicability of the energy Living Lab which could be supported by the plan of the Living Lab 
concept using gendered energy technology challenges. 
The Living Lab concept focuses on a methodology where “innovations (e.g., services and 
products or application improvements) are generated and validated in participatory multi-
contextual empirical real-world environments” (Eriksson et al., 2016:6-7). The approach or 
process of the human-centric in a Living Lab involves the citizens, humans, and the civic 
community as a means of innovation and object of activities which extends to user co-creation 
processes, leading to product creation and not just as the end-users and consumers (Eriksson et 
al., 2016). 
Basic Living Lab concept in education 
The education Living Lab concept (Callaghan and Herselman, 2015), describes the initiation 
procedure of a Living Lab in education. The explanation of this Living Lab in education was 
done by a case study, context process, and the data collected was done through a qualitative 
nature. These will become the basis for testing the energy Living Lab relating to this research 
study. 
Context of the education Living Labs 
To address the challenges facing the education system in Southern Africa, there was a need for 
a research unit or a hub that could be used to improve participatory, innovative sustainable 
research in the education sector. This hub was established as the Living Lab concepts with the 
support of the Living Lab Institute of South Africa (LLiSA) for an interdisciplinary research 
space where participants, students, and teachers can carry out research and bring about 
solutions to the challenges facing the educational environment (Callaghan and Herselman, 
2015). 
Establishment process of the education Living Lab 
The establishment of the education Living Lab was done by the process of the appreciative 
inquiry process (Callaghan and Herselman, 2015). According to Bushe (2013:1), “Appreciative 
Inquiry is a method for studying and changing social systems (groups, organisations, 
communities) that advocates collective inquiry into the best of what is to imagine what could 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 65 
 
be followed by the collective design of a desired future state that is compelling and thus, does 
not require the use of incentives, coercion or persuasion for planned change to occur.” 
The appreciative inquiry also promotes the Living Lab by relating to the central concept of 
‘open innovation,’ ‘user-driven,’ ‘co-design’ and multidisciplinary (Callaghan and Herselman, 
2015). Therefore, the process of establishing the Living Lab in education is in different phases, 
and these are briefly described below with examples (Callaghan & Herselman, 2015:6-7). 
These phases will be suitable for this research study and during the initial stages of the energy 
Living Lab to be developed. 
Phase 1 – Dream 
This is the point where the purpose and structure of the Living Labs are established and 
involves the identification of stakeholders, discussions/presentations, and workshops. In this 
phase, the participants dream about the chances of collaborative research with the users or other 
participants relating to education challenges. The focus in this phase is on innovative 
contributions, which will involve an extensive selection of stakeholders and the end-users. 
Phase 2 – Design 
In this phase of the education Living Lab, there was a finalised documentation to conclude on 
the design of the emerging Living Lab. In this phase, the purpose, mission, vision, and structure 
will be refined, and the approval of the recognised bodies or institutions (case studies) will 
commence. 
Phase 3 – Develop 
In this phase, final documentation and presentation of the proposal were done, and approvals 
were derived, which assisted in the initialisation of collaborative networking with the 
stakeholders and users. The stakeholders and users made their contributions towards the 
development of the Living Lab, and the process of establishing the Living Lab as a hub or unit 
continued all through the design and development phase. 
Phase 4 – Apply 
In this phase, the Living Lab paradigm continued as an “experimental application, and the 
Living Lab structure was developed” (Callaghan & Herselman, 2015:7). 
Phase 5 – Implement 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 66 
 
In this phase, the implementation of the Living Lab of Education started, and the completion 
was recognised, which led to the launch of the Living Lab. Then the stakeholders and end-
users collaborated to expand and generate more ideas to improve the system. 
Phase 6 – Review 
This phase is a continuous one promoted by the reflection of an idea generated from the 
participants, which led to the evaluation of the programs and adaptations of the activities or 
identified structures. 
Basic Living Lab concept in an urban area (e.g., Manchester, UK) 
The Living Lab, although in the urban region of Manchester, UK has the aim of ‘improving 
quality of life’, ‘improving comfort levels’ and ‘improving energy efficiency’. These slogans 
or aims of the Manchester, UK Living Lab is also vital to this research study as it fulfils the 
need for the applicability of the energy Living Lab which could be supported by the plan of the 
Living Lab concept using gendered energy technology challenges in the Poor informal 
settlements. 
Regarding the energy challenges, the urban areas in Manchester, UK are referred to as the main 
areas for geographic bearing and communication (Bouzarovski et al., 2017). The UK 
Government (2015), envisaged that in recent years, the rate of energy poverty has increased 
amongst most Manchester areas. According to the Manchester UK Living Lab, some of the 
urban areas are made up of old and poorly enclosed housing with “low average income” and 
the people situated in the areas are mostly “vulnerable to energy poverty”. 
The Manchester-UK Living Lab is a joint venture of the “Greater Manchester-UK Combined 
Authority (GMCA) and the University of Manchester”, which values a “local level energy” 
enhancement concept in “Greater Manchester-UK”. In recent years, the Living Lab is operating 
within a high rate of energy poverty, with an estimate of 900 households being occupied. This 
Living Lab has a target “low-income household”, “single-parent households”, “elderly 
households”, and households where disabled or long-term ill people are located. The SETI 
Living Lab also has a target on the poor informal settlement or household energy, which makes 
it related and acceptable to the Manchester Living Lab. 
2.3.3 Typology 
of 
the 
Living 
Labs 
Although Living Labs were earlier mentioned in the context of ICT, education, and health 
sectors, the term Living Lab was firstly used at the “Massachusetts Institute of Technology” 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 67 
 
(MIT), to narrate an environment of a laboratory which has all regular home facilities and built 
to be like a possible real home (Intille and Larson, 2005). 
There is much research about the Living Lab focusing on ICT (Thinyane et al., 2012), education 
(Callaghan and Herselman, 2015), and health (Platz, Herselman and Rapp, 2016). Therefore, 
it is essential to understand different typologies of the Living Lab and the processes of 
developing them from a theoretical viewpoint, relating them to the SETI Living Lab. The next 
section explains the typologies of the Living Lab. 
Table 2.4 sums up the fourfold Living Labs typologies (Intille and Larson, 2005:6). The 
following types of Living Labs and examples from the literature, a short illustration of their 
classification, and characteristics are provided.

When analysing the typology of the Living Labs in table 2.4 from literature, the “extended to 
testbed”, the people-public-private partnership (4Ps), and the “co-creation and supporting 
research context” are in line with the focus of the end-user’s participation during the design 
phases. These three types of Living Labs seem to correspond with the focus of the research 
study, and the SETI Living Lab will likely fall within these typologies, and the typologies will 
be the basis for testing the SETI Living Lab. 
The classification sections of the typologies of the Living Lab identified in table 2.4  are also 
seen as different types of the Living Labs in another approach, which is based on the actor 
driving their activities (Leminen, Westerlund and Nyström, 2012). Therefore, the 
classifications, which are: “utilizer-driven,” “enabler-driven,” “provider-driven” and “user-
driven” Living Labs will also be reviewed as four typologies of the Living Labs from another 
approach (Schuurman et al., 2015, p.5-6). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 69 
 
Utilizer-driven Living Labs 
This type of Living Lab is usually short-lived or in the form of a project-based because they 
usually contend for speedy results and supports research context and co-design with end-users 
(Schuurman et al., 2015). The focus here is to produce and test new products and services 
because they are launched to develop businesses. However, the utilizers make use of the Living 
Lab as a planned tool to investigate their product and services on the users to be in a central 
position before the products are developed (Schuurman et al., 2015). 
Therefore, this type of Living Lab seems to resemble the focus of co-designing, which requires 
end-users to take part in the decision-making activities before the development of the products, 
and this is in line with the research study. 
User-driven Living Labs 
Unlike the utilizer-driven Living Lab, the “user-driven Living Labs” are long-lived since they 
are developed in or around the end-users environments (Schuurman et al., 2015), and this kind 
of Living Labs are quite uncommon because it might be a result of the enabler-driven Living 
Lab (which is discussed below), where the end-users are required and propelled to participate 
in the innovation activities, although it could be challenging to start this type of Living Lab 
from scratch by the community end-users themselves  (Leminen et al., 2012). 
This type of Living Lab benefits the users more and benefits the stakeholders indirectly because 
the focus is co-created for the end-users mainly and established by end-users with a focus on 
solving everyday environmental life problems (Schuurman et al., 2015). This type of Living 
Lab shows promises of how the SETI Living Lab would look like. 
Enabler-driven Living Labs 
The enabler-driven Living Lab is also a long-lived one that involves the public sector actors, 
non-governmental sectors, towns, and municipalities whose aim is to carry out projects to 
produce social innovations or environmental developments (Schuurman et al., 2015). From 
table 2.4 above, the observation is that the “enabler-driven Living Labs” are similar to the 
knowledge sharing and collaboration activities which indicates that the central part here is the 
creation and dissemination of information and knowledge through the stakeholders and end-
users involved in the system (Schuurman et al., 2015). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 70 
 
Therefore, the SETI Living Lab also intends to create and disseminate knowledge gained from 
the participation of the end-users and those involved in the energy technology innovation 
processes and not mainly from the stakeholders. 
Provider-driven Living Labs 
The “provider-driven Living Labs” aims at improving the lives of the users by allowing all 
participants in the system to gain from the outcome of the innovation, which most of the 
innovation looks at producing candid knowledge and information for all involved in the system 
(Schuurman et al., 2015). These providers can be those in public and private organisations who 
participate in the “Living Lab” to co-design new products, services, and bring solutions to the 
needs of the industry. 
Some “provider-driven Living Labs” are established as long-lived platforms for innovation and 
aiming to advance research development of theory, expanding the creation of knowledge and 
bringing about specific solutions to problems (Schuurman et al., 2015). Observing table 2.4 
above, the provider-driven Living Labs falls into the Original American type of Living Lab, 
which uses a lab infrastructure for the study of end-users in an environment, like the urban poor 
environment. The testbed Living Lab can also fall into the Provider-driven Living Labs, where 
the “technical testbed infrastructure” is used to develop and test “innovative ICT” services 
(Schuurman et al., 2015). 
However, Schuurman et al. (2015, p.5-6) state that Leminen et al. (2012) argue that “Living 
Labs are usually either utilizer-driven or provider-driven because both emphasise efficiency 
with regards to the firm's investments, our observation complements this statement, as Living 
Labs can be a provider- and utilizer-driven, depending on the focus of the analysis (case-based 
or infrastructure-based).” This type of Living Lab is inversely related to the proposed SETI 
Living Lab. 
Therefore, the typologies of the Living Labs have been identified, however, most of them lack 
the aspect of gender. The argument remains that the existing Living Lab typologies are gender-
neutral, which means they focus on users without incorporating the gender aspect. They also 
do not incorporate the systems approach into the development processes. Therefore, this 
research study will contribute by developing the SETI Living Lab typology, which focuses on 
users incorporating the gender aspect with the use of systems approach. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 71 
 
2.3.4 Case 
studies 
of 
Living 
Labs 
and 
evidences

From Table 2.5, the few of the Living Labs described have areas of focus, and the focus of this 
research study has been mentioned above also (Living Lab for gendered energy technology 
innovations with systems approach). Therefore, the usefulness of Living Labs in the literature 
could motivate the development of the Living Lab concept to support the gendered energy 
technology innovation in poor informal settlements. 
Therefore, the idea is to develop similar Living Labs, with different case studies in poor rural 
environments but different cultural contexts as well, using systems approach. The question will 
be if the descriptions of the Living Lab’s above will hold to develop the SETI Living Lab? 
Furthermore, the testing and development of real-life and iterative approaches have been 
described in numerous studies utilising Living Labs (Mukumba, Makaka and Mamphweli, 
2016). Such studies also underscore how such methodologies can capture the key approaches 
to facilitating the uptake and localization of new technologies. For instance, Fortiş and Petcu 
(2022) provide a general overview of one of their studies implementing iterative development 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 75 
 
in a biogas technology solution in an informal settlement in South Africa. Incidentally, through 
this process of development and testing, followed by another round of feedback collection, and 
consequently coming back to the development phase again, the project was ultimately able to 
develop a technology that not only fits the local context within the country but is also socially 
accepted by the community. It also highlights the need to consider how the use of technology 
is received by the intended beneficiaries by incorporating feedback from the field and 
implementing changes into the exhibited technologization accordingly. 
 
Figure 2.5: Living Lab innovation cycle. Source: Evans et al. (2017:11) 
Bérubé (2022), adapted the Living Lab innovation cycle by Evans et al. (2017), which is a 
depiction of how innovation is pursued in practice starting from the conceptualization, followed 
by the development of the concept into a prototype, testing and going to the community to 
spread the innovations. Mbatha and Musango (2022) surveyed the literature on the application 
of SLL (Sustainable Living Labs) in energy and highlighted implementation experience and 
flexibility in learning and refining the approach as the two main success factors. Their research 
also found that projects employing these methodologies were more successful in their 
deliverables, and in leaving noticeable, long-lasting change on the societies to which they are 
beneficial. From this review, it becomes clear that most SLL initiatives must go through several 
cycles and be piloted in real-world environments since variables that either make or mar the 
success of such projects remain complex and vary in acceptance across community settings, 
especially in the energy-related projects. In their research on retrofitting technologies 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 76 
 
conducted in Coventry University, Ahmed, McGough, and Mateo-Garcia (2017) pointed out 
even further evidence to complement the previous arguments for the efficiency of SLLs. They 
state that the Living Lab approach was appropriate for finding out new technologies that could 
be utilised and tested on real life situations and the feedback obtained was equally useful in 
calibrating the technology. It also increased end-user acceptance from end-users, which 
indicates that Living Labs are not an isolated process, that it is, rather, cyclical. 
Furthermore, Botha et al. (2012) in their study unfolds the discussion on design science and 
Living Lab research for development within innovative environments in South Africa. They 
underscore the fact that living labs offer spaces and contexts for genuine co-design and 
simultaneity, processes that are vital when seeking to fashion creative and localised solutions. 
The system and the subsequent iterative development and use of technology within the Living 
Labs provide sustainable solutions with feedback constantly integrated into the development 
process. In writing about the prospects of Living Labs, Choi and Kwon (2021) touch upon the 
intentional positioning of living labs to boost innovation in the energy industry. According to 
them, innovation with Living Labs is done in a shared setting where stakeholders such as end 
users and local communities are engaged in it. This involvement assures that the solutions 
implemented are not only technologically feasible but acceptable to society and not detrimental 
to the environment. 
In their reflection on integrating gender considerations into energy innovations in Living Labs, 
Anditi et al. (2022) also discuss the relevance of covering gender aspects. They present a gender 
analysis framework for the informal urban settlements in Africa, and thus, this shows and 
proves that integrating looks for gender perspectives when coming up with more cycles of 
technological improvement then benefits in coming up with more equitable technological 
interventions. That way, SLLs are advanced on a broader base, thus increasing their returns on 
investment by offering those at the lower end of the population a fair quota. 
The role of stakeholders in Living Lab 
The roles that stakeholders played and their characteristics in the development of the SETILL 
will be described in the chapter 3 of this study. However, a brief review of the role of 
stakeholders in the Living Lab is represented in this section. 
Living Labs involve stakeholders who are important as they bring in the relevant knowledge 
that is crucial for the innovation processes (Mbatha & Musango, 2022). Ersoy and Van Bueren 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 77 
 
(2020) postulate that there is a need for a successful innovation outcome based on the level of 
engagement of the stakeholders. While discussing the contested area of energy innovation, their 
stakeholders are normally people within the community, governmental and non-governmental 
organisations, and private firms (Mbatha & Musango, 2022). These stakeholders involve those 
who possess fresh perspectives and tools that help in the development and the implementation 
of energy solutions as the innovations being proposed are more realistic and proven (Mbatha 
& Musango, 2022). 
Figure 2.6: Three-circle model of the stakeholder constellation in a Living Lab. Source: Korzer 
et al. (2020:7) 
Figure 2.6 above, Korzer et al. (2020), presented the three-circle model of stakeholder 
constellations in Living Lab based on Seebacher et al. (2018), showing the distribution of 
stakeholder constellations in a Living Lab. Circle 1 centres on the physical arrangement of the 
Living Lab and its organisation, Circle 2 is centred on more extensive participation and Circle 
3 is centred on a more specific way of participation. This model points to the systemic 
involvement of multiple stakeholders in business ventures for the purpose of innovation. Living 
Lab is concerned with the energy solutions that benefit community members and is used in the 
current analysis (Choi and Kwon, 2021). Their participation is useful as they offer insights into 
the nature, demands and requirements for energy uses in their facilities. By adopting a user-
driven design the authors allow for the innovations to be real and hence meet actual needs of a 
given community (Choi and Kwon, 2021). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 78 
 
Furthermore, when community members are involved in the process of innovating the 
solutions, they get empowered to accept and adapt to the new technologies hence promotes 
their acquisition (Anditi et al., 2022). For example, in the informal sector, where energy poverty 
remains a massive concern, engagement of women in the co-creation process is relevant 
because they are affected the most and they provide feedback on the most appropriate solutions 
(Musango et al., 2020). It can therefore be mentioned that government agencies are involved 
in the process of shaping the environment that will support innovation as a key factor in Living 
Lab (Ersoy and Van Bueren, 2020). They can offer legal requirements, monetary resources, 
and policies that can be used to foster progression and adhesive for the advancement of new 
energy options. The application of innovation by governments is also important as they act as 
a repository whereby successful innovations can be scaled up and harmonised with other 
energy policies and programs (Ersoy and Van Bueren, 2020). Choi and Kwon (2021) argue 
that the involvement of the private sector in Living Lab improves the energy solutions through 
effectiveness and relevance. 
These involve the involvement of other stakeholders in Living Lab and specifically, the non-
governmental organisations act as go-betweens to the community. They can provide channels 
for communication, foster the development of trust, and make sure that the less powerful 
receive a platform for speaking in relation to the importance of innovation. These organisations 
also provide important competencies such as Community Development, Advocacy, and 
capacity development that will contribute positively to the activities of the Living Lab 
(Herselman et al., 2015).  
In the area of energy innovation systems, Living Labs are used to allow the elaboration of new 
solutions in real conditions (Fortiş and Petcu, 2022). Such an iterative philosophy of working 
closely with users increases the chances of detecting pitfalls early enough and improving on 
the solutions that are developed hence one can speak of agile as being more robust from the 
user perspective. For instance, integration of solar energy systems at the Living Lab may 
require deploying various fixtures and how the community of the Living Lab uses them to 
implement the best and ideal solution for the place (Grové, 2022). Also, the idea of Living Lab 
does not only encompass the development of innovations but also their implementation and 
diffusion of the effective innovations in countries. Buy-in from government entities and private 
organisations is also necessary for supporting and investing in the needed structures with regard 
to the scaling of proven approaches. They can make use of their networks and experience in 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 79 
 
organising the targeted communities and raising awareness in support of the innovations to 
ensure that information gets delivered to the concerned masses. 
2.3.5 Living 
Lab 
in 
the 
energy 
context 
Living Labs have become an indispensable tool for advancing innovations in the energy field, 
as they can serve as an experimental model for developing and implementing energy solutions 
within real-life contexts (Mbatha and Musango, 2022). This approach ensures that the 
developed technologies are not only technologically feasible, but it is also realistically possible 
and would focus on the end-users. Thus, when addressing energy needs throughout different 
contexts including rural informal settlements, energy Living Labs result in responsible 
innovation that has been grounded on the principles of inclusivity, reflexivity and 
sustainability. 
In the energy sector, Living Labs can be defined as a facility, venues where stakeholders such 
as end-users, researchers, from the industry, policymakers and community organisations work 
in cooperation to create and with the help of experiments, test energy solutions (Tercanli and 
Jongbloed, 2022). This participatory approach is essential because the innovations would in 
real sense come with realities for the targeted users, especially individuals in the underserved 
markets. Living Labs include people directly in the innovation process, capturing vital ideas 
and input that traditional research settings may miss (Campos & Marín-González, 2023). 
The energy efficiency projects implemented in Living Labs can have many benefits, one of 
them is pre-implementation of activities in real-life conditions (von Geibler et al., 2014). This 
implies that energy technologies can be subjected to usage conditions before requiring 
complete use with the real world, making the tests more realistic, practical and revealing 
dependability, practicality and benefits. For example, new technologies in renewable energy 
like solar energy or wind energy devices can be installed in societies in order to assess the 
adaptability of the technology in offering permanent and sustainable energy. In the following 
deployments of these technologies, information collected from the real world can then be used 
to enhance them and adapt them to suit the needs of the users (Fortiş & Petcu, 2022). 
In the context of energy Living Labs, Campos and Marín-González (2023) emphasise the 
necessity of being responsible for the innovation processes that are implemented. It implies a 
situation whereby all parties can contribute their quota towards the generation, development, 
and marketing of the innovation, preferably, women. This is of particular concern whenever 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 80 
 
energy poverty also comes into the picture, such as when women are mainly responsible for 
managing energy, fuel, and lighting at home but are rarely involved in decision-making about 
energy sources (Botha et al., 2012). In this Case, Living Labs could involve more Women in 
co-creation as well as testing of energy technologies to ensure products with satisfactory 
solutions to their problems are designed. In the Energy Living Labs area, reflexivity is 
understood as the dynamic self-reflectiveness of the innovation processes (von Wirth et al., 
2018). This means that as in any innovation process the ongoing evaluation of the energy 
solutions and of the process of innovation has to be done, in order to fine tune the energy 
solutions and the process to fit the user needs. This cyclical action of thinking and modifying 
guarantees that the changes remain appropriate and efficient throughout the period (Herselman 
et al., 2010). 
Another principle is sustainability where energy Living Labs aim to develop solutions that can 
be managed in such a way as to protect the environment and preserve resources for future 
generations (Callaghan and Herselman, 2015). This relates not only to the creation but also to 
the long-term effectiveness of innovation solutions based on environmentally friendly energy 
technologies. Sustainable innovation in energy Living Labs aims at maintaining economic, 
social, and environmental aspects while seeking to meet overarching sustainability objectives 
of energy production and use like reduction of emission levels and more equality in energy 
access (Ersoy & Van Bueren, 2020). The figure 2.7 below extracted from Veeckman et al 
(2013) highlights the Living Lab triangle that sought to illustrate the proximity relations 
between the users, the technology developers and the researchers within the Living Lab 
context. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 81 
 
 
Figure 2.7: The Living Lab triangle. Source: Veeckman et al. (2013:6) 
Introducing Living Labs also enables the coordination of technical, social, and policy aspects 
of innovations in the field of energy (Campos and Marín-González, 2023). For instance, the 
adoption of the Living Labs as an environment enables the implementation of smart grids that 
can accommodate integrated energy management systems that will help in minimising energy 
wastage. These systems can also be coupled with renewable energy, thus allowing for improved 
energy efficiency and optimal use of energy resources. Through the integration of 
policymakers, these innovations can be attached to the specific guidelines and policies to reach 
all members of the society (Mbatha & Musango, 2022). With regards to South Africa, where 
the majority of the population does not have access to clean, efficient, and affordable energy 
sources, Living Labs may indeed be a more appropriate solution to the challenges posed by 
energy poverty. By adopting the spirit of participation, self-interpretation, and co-design, 
Energy Living Labs can create solutions to cater for these communities. This approach 
enhances not only the energy extended to these communities and security, but is also perceived 
as an innovation platform where the community stands to benefit directly. In addition, Energy 
Living Labs positively responded to Sustainable Development Goal 5 (SDG 5) on gender 
equality for women and girls (Giannetti et al., 2020). Since the energy idea and its practical 
application is co-designed and trialled with the target beneficiaries, Living Labs can help make 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 82 
 
the processes of energy innovation fairer and more sustainable for women. It is especially so 
within such areas where women are caught up with numerous energy-related hurdles, and 
where the attainment of better and secured energy would be most optimal for them (Mukumba, 
Makaka, & Mamphweli, 2016). 
Brief examples from South Africa and African context 
In South Africa, it becomes evident that several living labs are implementing energy efficiency 
and the desired change. Several Living Labs in South Africa testify to the success of the 
implementation. Perhaps the most prominent example is the Stellenbosch Smart City program, 
which focuses on implementing new smart energy solutions through the Living Lab approach 
(Mukama et al., 2022). The participants involved in this project include the local authorities, 
institutions of higher learning, private organisations, and the general population. The subject is 
based on the promotion of using wind power and other sources of cleanly generated electrical 
power, improving the efficiency of energy usage, and developing eco-friendly technology in 
urban planning. With the Living Lab methodology, the project has experimented with several 
sustainable technologies like solar PV power, energy-efficient lighting, and smart advanced 
metres (Molinari et al., 2023). 
Another successful example is the iShack project that has been implemented at Stellenbosch 
and aims to advance access to energy in the context of Community-Based Management 
(Bloem, Swilling and Koranteng, 2021). It employs a Living Lab project cycle incorporating 
residents in the sustainable energy solution development. The iShack Project touches on energy 
and social issues by incorporating solar power and housing improvements. Living Lab has 
helped identify and offer solutions to numerous clients by delivering cheap and dependable 
power to several inhabitants and proving that Living Lab is well equipped to solve complicated 
socio-economic problems (Sphokazi Phelokazi, 2022). 
From a regional perspective, Energy Living Lab in Kenya is among the few that have 
successfully adopted this concept in Africa (Mbatha and Musango, 2022). The following is an 
initiative to employ decentralised renewable energy systems to enhance access to electricity in 
rural areas. It focuses on the users since the local communities participate in the innovation 
process to develop optimum solutions to the problems in their daily routine activities. The 
project has introduced several technologies in the categories of renewable energy, such as solar 
microgrids and biogas systems, which have greatly enhanced energy provision and availability 
to most of the targeted communities (Valentyna Loboichenko et al., 2024). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 83 
 
Moreover, Nigeria has adopted the Living Lab concept to engage citizens in innovation 
processes, especially in the energy sector, through the Eko Innovation Centre (Lie, van Paassen 
and Witteveen, 2023). The centre aims to foster partnerships among the government, academia, 
industry, and CSOs to cycle innovative solutions for energy systems. Established through the 
Eko Innovation Centre as an innovation hub, some successful projects of the company are the 
operation of solar PV, energy-efficient home appliances, and smart grid systems. Regarding 
Living Labs, the projects implemented in Nigeria have increased energy availability and 
utilisation, indicating the concept's efficacy in enhancing innovation in the area. 
By examining these successful examples, it is possible to identify the principles of the Living 
Lab approach, which were applied: fruitful cooperation with stakeholders, co-design, and 
application to real-life context. People-centred design helps to work with stakeholders at the 
beginning of development to ensure that solutions owe more to the target users (Godinho, 
2022). ‘Co-creation, you know, makes people own the ideas and this in turn makes them 
committed to the success of such changes thus making it easier to achieve sustainability and 
scalability of such changes (Itten et al., 2021).’ This is a very important approach in the 
execution of projects because real-life testing reveals the realities on the ground regarding real-
life challenges that may be faced when implementing the solutions. 
2.3.6   Conceptualising systems Living Lab: Living Lab, energy Living and systems approach 
The systems energy technology innovation Living Lab (SETILL) will be developed in chapter 
three of this study, which will lead to the final conceptualization of the systems Living Lab. 
The SETILL is the link between the Living Lab, energy Living Lab and systems approach (SD 
and SE). Further details of the systems approach used in the SETILL will be presented in the 
Level 3 section of this literature review chapter. 
Systems Living Labs can be characterised as a new paradigm of innovation and problem 
solving, especially useful for use in modern society, where problems are often closely 
connected. Systems Living Labs differ from the previous ones as the latter are applied to single 
technologies or solutions for complex issues within complex and constantly changing 
environments (Hossain, Leminen, & Westerlund, 2019). Thus, the systems Living Lab will be 
developed in this study as a Living Lab used to solve energy challenges with the use of a 
systems approach. This approach accepts that problems that exist in real life occur within the 
context of connected webs of multiple associates that are interdependent, such that actions 
taken in one part of the web influence and affect the rest of the web (von Wirth et al., 2018). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 84 
 
Therefore, in the SETILL, a systems thinking researcher, expert or academic must be involved 
as one of the stakeholders who would understand the systemic variables or models involved 
for the development of energy innovations. 
 
Figure 2.8: The Living Lab process and systems approach: Source: Yasuoka et al. (2018:135) 
Figure 2.8 displayed from Yasuoka 2018 and his perspective on the Living Lab linking with 
the systems approach highlights some of the specifications of Living Labs as previously 
discussed, to prioritise problem identification through stakeholder dialogue and the learning 
process. This approach shares several similarities with Soft Systems Approaches (SSA). 
However, unlike SSA, which explores a problem space for solutions, Living Labs focuses on 
the concrete interactions of services and products (Yasuoka et al., 2018). Therefore, to logically 
implement the Living Labs approach, it is necessary to use models that not only capture 
stakeholders' subjectivity but also describe the concrete features and systems of the service 
(Yasuoka et al., 2018).  
Furthermore, the structural model for Living Labs closely resembles those used in Hard 
Systems Approaches (HSA) (Yasuoka et al., 2018). Consequently, it is crucial for Living Labs, 
as a method for addressing social challenges, to incorporate both SSA and HSA models and 
methodologies, as they complement each other (see Figure 2.8). 
The emphasis in Systems Living Labs is therefore to foster cooperation from different 
stakeholders, including academia, systems thinking professionals, industry, government, and 
civil society. Interdisciplinary teamwork is highlighted by Callaghan and Herselman (2015), 
indicating that this multiplicity of specialties brings a diversified perception and approach to 
probing and analysing complex innovative challenges. For positioning multiple stakeholders, 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 85 
 
systems Living Labs enhances the provision of contextually consistent and corroborative 
interventions that respond to the needs and barriers within the system (Leal Filho et al., 2022). 
In addition, Systems Living Labs are based in contexts where they offer possibilities to 
implement and adjust solutions in as close to real-life situations as possible. This approach is 
in contrast to the conventional elements used in laboratories, in distinct ways, and interventions 
from the realities and associated risks of daily life (Motloung, 2022). The Living Lab directly 
interacts with the problem environment, which leads to the creation of the empirical and 
practical data that are critical for making a proper policy decision and designing relevant 
interventions (Musango et al., 2020). Therefore, Systems Living Labs for social innovation 
play a role in transforming the system by providing fields for experimentation and learning. 
According to Van Rensburg et al. (2014), this Lab helps to support repetitive attempts at 
experimentation with new ideas that positively help cultivate on-going learning or refinement 
processes. Thus, in Living Labs, knowledge is created through experimentation, where the 
efficiency of interventions is proven and the presence of unanticipated outcomes and new 
features inherent in the system is revealed (Choi & Kwon, 2021). 
Integrative approach and applications to energy solutions 
Systems approaches with Living Labs are a full-spectrum approach to energy issues affecting 
informal settlements. Systems thinking offers a framework for approaching energy systems 
more comprehensively because it looks at the larger picture of how all the parts in the system 
interact with one another (Naumann et al., 2020). In contrast, systems engineering gives a set 
of procedures for developing and implementing systems more efficiently. These two systems 
approaches were applied to develop the SETILL model for this research study. 
As expressed by Mulugetta, Ben Hagan and Kammen (2019), integration is helpful as it 
encompasses several advantages, which include the following : 
1. To address the problem of a lack of integrated approach by ensuring that energy 
solutions cannot be developed without considering socio-economic and environmental 
implications. This makes it easier in the long run to develop more sustainable and 
stronger systems of energy that would be more suited to suit the needs of society. 
2. To lead to innovation by incorporating the end-users in the co-design activities. It 
makes these solutions more relevant and acceptable in the scope by involving the key 
stakeholders. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 86 
 
3. The aspects of Living Labs which include real-life testing where technologies can be 
tested and modified to fit the end users. 
To foster this integrated approach, growing the recognition and understanding of the multiple 
intersecting roles and responsibilities of all organisations and agencies involved, as well as 
the processes and the objectives for co-design and co-production and the mechanisms for 
assessing and evaluating the strategies and approaches, must be established. Some crucial 
processes in this approach include the identification of the stakeholders, the active 
involvement of the users who identified as the stakeholders, the design of the prototypes, the 
implementation of the prototypes in real-world settings, and the final refinement of the 
solutions (Coulentianos et al., 2022). It focuses all the possibilities of solutions, makes them 
technologically practical and sociopolitically acceptable which engenders more relevance and 
sustainability. 
Although many pointed out that Systems Living Labs is a refinement compared to the simple 
models above it is a more developed way to solve the complex energy issues of such informal 
settlements. Systems Living Labs could also construct summed up strategies for innovation 
founded on systems thinking as the means to produce an area in which stakeholders 
collectively create, collectively experience, and collectively transform innovative answers for 
energy demand. The same approach also locates relevancy and effectiveness of innovations 
as well as community enablement (Musango et al., 2020). Stakeholders within the vicinity of 
the Systems Living Labs include inhabitants, traders, civil society organisations, as well as 
relevant government authorities, who participate in the progressive creation of localised 
energy solutions (Hossain et al., 2019). Being engaging in developing such solutions assist in 
ensuring that the solutions this technology needs are accurate and plausible depending on the 
application of the technology and the ability of the people in the community to accept the 
change or willingness to embrace the change. Nonetheless, the cyclically systematic nature of 
System Living Labs means that some energy solutions can be tweaked over time depending 
on the outcome from actual real-life practice cases (Habiyaremye, 2019). This means that 
implementation risks that may exist within the local context are properly controlled and at the 
same time, the scalability and replicability of innovation are achieved (Choi and Kwon 2021). 
It also affords the demarcation of realistic concerns that may obtain with some innovations 
from the incubation phase thus ensuring nearly total optimality once the proposed innovations 
are put into practice (Herselman et al., 2015). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 87 
 
In addition, Systems Living Labs involve and welcomes all the stakeholders and concentrate 
on not only the technical system, but also the social, economic and environmental aspects of 
the energy system. This sympathetic perspective allows Living Labs to design system solutions 
that not only address energy needs but also development goals such as eradicating poverty and 
promoting sustainability (von Wirth et al., 2018). Thus, through resolving the issue of 
interdependencies, Living Labs has a vision of achieving sustainable results as far as the 
community and the environment are concerned (Herselman, Marais and Pitse-Boshoman, 
2010). There are also focuses on the fact that strategies for scaling and replicating are 
constituent parts of Systems Living Labs, meant to take and spread successful implementations 
beyond the pilot stage (Hossain, Leminen and Westerlund, 2019). Politics, private industry, 
and population help translate evidence-based solutions into new formats through partnerships 
(Van Rensburg et al., 2014). They provide a means of popularising innovations and making an 
indelible mark on access to energy and sustainability. 
Therefore, assessment and knowledge sharing are core components of Systems Living Labs, 
as they should be efficient. Stakeholder involvement in monitoring changes in energy scores 
and scrutinising lessons derived from innovations through publications as well as corrective 
and improvement-focused workshops ensures cumulative learning and enhancement 
(Callaghan & Herselman, 2015). This study envisaged that through sharing lessons learned and 
other more effective solutions, Living Labs would facilitate the exchange of knowledge and 
encourage others to design even better solutions for improving the sector of energy access. 
Therefore, the integration of Systems Living Labs best practices into existing policy 
approaches is a step that needs to be made to ensure continued and effective change after the 
end of experiments (Hossain, Leminen and Westerlund, 2019). Energy policies at the local, 
national, and regional level must therefore incorporate such approaches into governance so that 
‘lock-in’ ensures long-term adaptability and sustainable innovation (Leal Filho et al., 2022). 
This integration helps to improve the operation of energy systems in an ongoing manner and
