also helps in the scaling of effective measures at the international level. 
2.3.7    Real-world testing and iterative development of systems Living Lab 
Systems Living Labs contain a focus on testing energy innovations in real-life situations where 
the concepts are supposed to operate (Rezaee, 2022). Therefore, the SETILL will be tested in 
the case study identified in this study. This approach is essential for making sure that adopted 
or to-be-adopted technologies are feasible, sustainable, and adequate for addressing the needs 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 88 
 
of final consumers (Rezaee, 2022). Combining concepts of real-world testing and an agile 
development methodology, will allow feedback to inform ongoing improvement and 
subsequent trials, which is critical for the deployment of energy technologies within informal 
settlements. 
The assessment of proofs in Systems Living Labs contexts consists of experimenting with 
typical energy-focused innovations in real-life contexts adequately (Huang and Thomas, 2021). 
However, the importance of image processing in applications implies their differences from 
typical lab experiments, which commonly do not consider actual conditions of operation. As 
noted by Ahmed, McGough, and Mateo-Garcia (2017), real-world testing means the discovery 
of issues that have not been foreseen during the testing and the data that is gathered about users’ 
interactions with the technology. It enables the fine-tuning of the solutions in order to best fit 
the tastes of the end-users, since all organisations’ applications are designed to improve the 
end-users’ quality of life. 
The iterative development process is an important part of Systems Living Lab methodology, 
in which a few iterations of development that are based on the same logical framework are 
made (Leal Filho et al., 2022). This process encompasses a cycle of prototyping the new 
communication technology, testing it with users, gathering feedback, and improving on it. It 
fits within the plan, do, check, and act (PDCA) cycle, where each step focuses on modifying 
the design and operation of the energy technology that was reviewed in the previous step. 
Campos & Marín-González (2023) show that this approach encapsulates a culture of learning 
and innovation assurance to guarantee that the final product is well adopted by the local context 
and users. The identification of stakeholders and end-users as participants in the development 
process is indispensable to the success of energy innovations. In the case of Botha et al. (2012), 
the authors underscore that adopting the co-design and co-testing of the solutions makes it 
possible not only to arrive at technically feasible solutions but also to evaluate the social 
acceptability of the solutions. 
Benefits and challenges of real-world testing and interactive development 
Field testing in the actual informal settlements used as case study assures that the energy 
innovations are functional and applicable to the contexts that the various informal settlements 
present. As Anditi et al. (2022) pointed out, this approach is beneficial as it originates from the 
contextualization of the problems and comes up with solutions for them considering the 
hardships of the community; for instance, they have to work with limited resources and 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 89 
 
restrictions on accessible infrastructure. Proliferation methods in the development of such 
technologies involve the incorporation of continual feedback from the targeted users to 
improve the chances of acceptance and usage of the technology (Mbatha and Musango, 2022). 
From the literature, Choi and Kwon (2021) explained that when users are presented with the 
fact that their feedback is being implemented in the design, they are likely to accept the 
technology. Generally, solutions that are cultured and designed through experiences and 
subsequent feedback are much more likely to be transferable across different contexts and, 
hence, easily scalable. Ersoy and Van Bueren (2020) highlight that systems Living Lab can 
also enable the creation of a variable and more resilient infrastructure that may be expanded or 
reapplied to similar informal areas or settings. 
As highly flexible learning environments, however, systems Living Lab real-world testing and 
developmental cycles are not without difficulty. This is a reference to the fact that while 
undertaking research, Von Wirth et al. (2018) mention that the movements of managing the 
actual field trials may include dealing with the different stakeholders that can be tedious, in 
addition to making sure that one’s data is collected in a constant manner. Also, iterative 
development involves ongoing commitment and investment and can be a challenge even in 
organisations that have relatively easy access to resources when operating in contexts in which 
resources are limited (Motloung, 2022). However, such challenges are most surmountable by 
first having proper strategies for outlining relationships and expectations, as well as the overall 
flow of operations, and second, designing a proper organisational structure for handling data. 
Grové (2022) also notes that the implementation of the iteration approach needs to be 
orchestrated based on a clear methodology and accompanied by support frameworks.  
2.3.8 
 
 
Scaling 
and 
replication 
strategies 
In order to deploy effective energy solutions piloted in the SETILL, it is important to undertake 
planning and involve political and business actors. A strategic approach is essential for 
translating systems Living Labs into approaches that can be scaled beyond their respective 
communities. According to Campos and Marín-González (2023), Living Labs’ inclusive, 
reflexive, and sustainable perspective shall be crucial for establishing an energy transition 
agenda in line with stakeholders’ multifaceted demands. Such scaling strategies must be 
informed by clear planning with consideration of the local environment, the available 
resources, and the probable challenges in noticing such an account. Key players in policy are 
equally important in their duty of establishing a favourable environment through favourable 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 90 
 
policies and laws, as well as recommending incentives that will be of importance in the 
fulfilment of goals and objectives (Musango et al., 2020). 
The first implementation issue in scaling and replication entails identifying how best to apply 
solutions that have already been shown to work appropriately given a range of contexts. Other 
authors, like Mukumba, Makaka, and Mamphweli (2016), suggest that one needs to consider 
accessorial context features, including climate conditions, levels of economic development, 
and cultural patterns, when transferring biogas technologies to informal South African 
settlements. Moreover, Botha et al. (2012) underscore the need for design science and Living 
Lab research ability to work within the local context and environment of the target group. Thus, 
the advantages of Living Labs stem from the fact that they are holistic, operate in a dynamic 
cycle, and are capable of modifying technology based on feedback acquired from testing 
scenarios (Pereira et al., 2020). It allows for developing reliable and viable solutions that work 
in different situations, thus achieving sustainability.  
For instance, Ahmed, McGough, and Mateo-Garcia (2017) explained the progressive 
development of retrofitting technologies at Coventry University, where subsequent iterations 
of the specific retrofitting solutions were refined in order to achieve the best possible fit with 
user demands and the environment. The next points reveal some potential difficulties in the 
scaling and replication of innovations stemming from SLLs. Some of the challenges identified 
by Ersoy & Van Bueren (2020) include lack of funding, reduced inclusion of stakeholders and 
complications in legislation. To this end, it is critical that risk assessment and management be 
fully incorporated into the basic strategizing models and methodologies. For this reason, 
innovation as a concept can also be useful in ensuring that the barriers are removed by raising 
awareness and making the stakeholders become advocates for the cause. The next section 
explains core variables which are important to this research study. 
2.3.9 
 
 
Integrating 
gender-centric 
approach 
in 
the 
SETILL 
The core element of gender topics in energy Living Labs is essential when it comes to the 
inclusion of women’s needs in the Living Labs. When it comes to South Africa in particular, 
informal settlements are a major issue, and they are tightly connected with numerous socio-
economic problems. Meanwhile, the participation of women in energy innovations is crucial 
in order to create suitable practical strategies in the context of a challenged country. There is 
an understanding that women in informal settlements have certain energy needs that may be 
different from those of the rest of the population (Van Rensburg et al., 2014). Biomass energy 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 91 
 
sources like wood and charcoal are not only conventional but also unhealthful and unsafe due 
to their effects on indoor air pollution (Van Rensburg et al., 2014). Many women are most 
affected because meal preparation is more female than male, thus drawing them closer to the 
heart. Musango et al. (2020) have opined that integrating gender into energy services is 
essential to redressing such differences and comprehensively enhancing wellbeing. 
In addition, gender can be a barrier to the use of clean energy because it affects economic 
activity, and where women lack other sources of clean energy, they cannot competitively 
engage in economic activities that require energy and innovation (von Geibler et al., 2014). 
Small-scale businesses can be owned by women and are primarily located in informal 
settlements; they therefore require reliable and affordable sources of energy. That said, the 
systems energy technology innovation Living Lab (SETILL) can improve gender relevance for 
women’s economic empowerment and other socio-economic goals when considering gender 
perspectives in their solutions. The gender-centric observation guarantees that innovations are 
developed with the input of women as well as addressing their needs. The occurrence of Anditi 
et al. (2022) discusses the gender analysis of energy innovations, stressing that women should 
be included in the increasing and development of such projects. This can help in developing 
more suitable and effective solutions for using energy since the input is normally collected 
directly from the women. 
 
 
Figure 2.9: Gender integration continuum. Source: edna (2014) 
Figure 2.9 depicts the Gender Integration Continuum which describes the range of gender 
mainstreaming that ranges from gender neutral approach to gender sensitive one. This 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 92 
 
continuum highlights the need to address gender in the design and delivery of specialised 
programs to reduce barriers and gaps relating to gender. In South Africa, women's involvement 
in energy Living Lab would go a long way in addressing energy poverty and other impediments 
to access to modern energy in the form of electricity and other related requirements and services 
(Mbatha and Musango, 2022). 
Since the SETILL engages women as key players in energy projects, we may end up with better 
solutions in terms of appropriateness and acceptance rates. Sexualized programs in Energy 
Living Labs can enrich a set of softer approaches used to create more sustainable energy 
products. According to Ahmed, McGough, and Mateo-Garcia (2017), contemporary 
technological advancements enhance the practice of retrofitting buildings in cities and the 
achievement of sustainable construction. By incorporating gender aspects into Living Labs 
technologies, it will be possible to enable the technologies to be environmentally friendly while 
embracing society. For instance, biogas technology, which uses organic waste to produce clean 
energy for cooking, could greatly improve the lives of women who live in the slums. Mukumba, 
Makaka, and Mamphweli (2016) have also stressed the prospect of biogas in South Africa, 
acknowledging its applications concerning energy dearth and waste control. Mainstreaming 
remains a way to obtain satisfactory results in the usage of biogas systems by assessing the 
gender aspect and working on the solutions that will be most helpful for ladies (Hossain, 
Leminen and Westerlund, 2019). 
Nevertheless, there are key challenges for gendered perspectives on adoption in the SETILL, 
as illustrated below. Women’s roles and responsibilities may be restricted by the cultural and 
socially acceptable exercise of decision-making authority. In their article, Botha et al. (2012) 
also give emphasis on the role of Living Labs in innovation and development, though they 
mention that culture and social factors must contribute to making this process open to all. 
Moreover, both where gender and energy solutions are concerned, insufficient levels of 
awareness and education may prove detrimental for the accomplishment of the set aims. More 
awareness-raising programs on what needs to be done would need to be taken to people to 
create the enabling environment for them to embrace such endeavours. Herselman, Foko, 
Nungu, Ng'amba, and Mgeni (2015) describe some of the lessons learned from scientific 
collaborations in South Africa and Tanzania and argue that collaborations should be tailored to 
capacity development needs and address the lack of women's participation in research. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 93 
 
South African examples of the application of gender mainstreaming can be discussed in more 
detail to highlight its effectiveness in driving change in the energy sector. For instance, the 
GENS Living Lab project explored by Sphokazi Phelokazi (2022) described the involvement 
of stakeholders in furthering ambiguous sexual energy solutions. In this project, the women 
have the most benefits: through their participation in the conceptualization, decision-making, 
and practical execution of the energy systems, the energy systems are more efficient and 
sustainable.  
Strategies for gender integration in the SETILL 
Considering gender perspectives in energy Living labs in South Africa are critical in order to 
create sustainable solutions to energy crises in informal settlements because women bear the 
most out of them (Yvonne Chivanga, 2023). Key steps that can be used to effectively advance 
gender integration and guarantee the success of energy advancements in these settings include 
among them, one of the major antecedents is to support the advancement of women and their 
engagement in decision-making in the SETILL. This is in line with Botha et al. (2012), who 
argue that research work aims at embracing innovation and development; hence, there is a must 
to embrace varied visions. Through integrating women into the planning, design, and execution 
of energy projects, Living Labs will guarantee gender-wise satisfactory solutions. 
Training and capacity building, which are sensitive to the operations of institutions providing 
energy, promote the improvement of the position of women through their engagement in the 
development of innovative energies. Herselman, Marais, and Pitse-Boshoman (2010) indicate 
in their paper that living lab methodology can be used to improve interpersonal skills, arguing 
that learning is a continuous process. Living Labs can support women with training programs 
that focus on gender concerns and potential, which will enable them to be involved in energy 
projects. 
Another important strategy geared towards improvement is the development of energy 
solutions that address the needs of women in the informal settlement. Anditi et al. (2022) 
suggest a gender analysis framework for energy innovations to identify the gender dimensions 
and ways to consider them when designing innovations. This strategy entails the making of 
gender-responsive assessments to determine the challenges and possibilities, particularly in 
using energy among female individuals. Through the implementation of the following insights 
into Living Labs design, products to be developed for the specific clientèle can be designed in 
the best way possible. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 94 
 
The implementation of gender initiatives in Living Labs involves encouraging people from 
government and non-government organisations, community representatives, and women’s 
organisations as partners. Sphokazi Phelokazi (2022) explores stakeholders’ engagement in a 
gendered energy Living Lab, describing their centrality. Being a form of partnership between 
different stakeholders to work together to reach common goals, Living Labs can help mobilise 
the efforts of participants and enhance change for the benefit of both men and women regarding 
the issue of utilising sustainable sources of energy. 
The role played by advocates in the development of gender-integrated policies that support 
energy innovation is essential in this context. Mukumba, Makaka, and Mamphweli (2016) also 
mention the use of biogas technologies in South Africa and highlight the role of policy support 
in their promotion. Therefore, systems energy technology innovation Living Lab can act as an 
active participant in engaging policymakers, private investors, and legislators in the process of 
constructing a parity-based appeal and sustainable funding and regulatory system that will 
support women’s freedoms and rights, as well as provide all women with equal opportunity to 
benefit from energy access. 
 
2.4 
Level 3: Core variables 
In this research study, the core or important variables emanated from the research process. 
These variables are discussed in this section and they would have an underlying concept and 
how their interrelationship influences each other. These core variables were necessary for the 
progress of this research study. They are shown as the follow-up context for level 2 and level 
1 of the literature review, respectively (see figure 2.1). 
A systematic literature review was also  done on the core variables. The core variables were 
identified using a research focus on gendered energy technology and systems Living Lab. 
These core variables are the ‘Biogas technology’, ‘Informal settlements’, and ‘systems 
approaches’ used for this study to achieve the research objectives. These variables are termed 
‘core variables’ because they are very important for the development and testing of the SETILL 
model in the phenomenon being studied. 
2.4.1 The systems approaches for this research study - SsT (SD) and SE 
Researchers like Kalnins and Jarohnovich (2015); Behl and Ferreira (2014) have attempted 
definitions of systems thinking, but the term ‘systems thinking’ was initially invented by Barry 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 95 
 
Richmond (1994) who refers to the demand for interdependence of systems thinking. Systems 
thinking could involve a trans-disciplinary approach from natural sciences, social sciences, and 
philosophy. According to Giddings, Hopwood, and O’Brien (2002), the three spheres of 
sustainable development which are “economic, environmental and social” are seen as “nested” 
within each other and not seen as separate views. Therefore, the systems approach that 
acknowledges the developing properties, complexity, and relationships should be undertaken 
to combine these three spheres of sustainability (Hardi and Zdan , In Giddings et al. 2002). 
This is because the systems approach is mostly preferred to the analytical approach due to its 
relationship to connections, context, and relationships (Gallopín, 2003). 
Systems thinking could bring about a solution to the problem of this research as it approaches 
problems and practicability as concurrently dynamic and multidimensional (Blewitt, 2012). 
Therefore, systems theory or systems thinking creates an integrated explanatory framework 
that considers different interactions as systems in the world and not as a single agent (Clayton 
& Radcliffe, 1996). Furthermore, to technically build innovation in the social and economic 
discipline, it would be necessary to apply the systems thinking approach to the process of the 
energy system. This is because the energy system has different features and functions as a 
synergy with diverse systems but intending to ensure services of energy delivery to the -users 
(women in this context). Therefore, the rapid growth of technology innovation emerges as 
constant complex systems that are dependent on the existing systems. This is related to global 
growth which increases the complex world’s social network through economic feedback loops 
(Arnold and Wade, 2015). Therefore, the systems thinking approach would allow for the 
understanding of the primary causes leading to the complex behaviour and dynamism, which 
allows for the forecasting of the causes and the adaptation of the effects. 
Arnold and Wade (2015) conceptualise SsT as a collection of synergistic analytical skills 
employed to enhance the ability to identify and comprehend systems, predict their behaviours, 
and devise modifications to achieve desired outcomes. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 96 
 
Figure 2.10: A systems thinking systemigram for the definition of systems thinking concepts. 
Source: Arnold and Wade (2015:676) 
As illustrated in Figure 2.10, the primary processes of a Systems Thinking (SsT) approach start 
with the identification of elements, recognition of purpose, and comprehension of 
interconnections within a group of synergistic components known as a system. Gaining insights 
into the dynamics and behaviour patterns involves distinguishing stocks, variables, and flows, 
as well as identifying non-linear relationships within the system. Following this, deciphering 
complexity through conceptual modelling enhances the understanding of reinforcing 
behaviours and feedback mechanisms at various levels. This process, in turn, deepens 
comprehension of the system and solidifies concepts and patterns. The system's objective is 
achieved by unravelling complexity and understanding, predicting, and adjusting feedback 
mechanisms to yield effective outcomes. Within these key processes, new processes can be 
added or removed to improve the understanding of system behaviour. These processes create 
a behaviour pattern within the system that can be either 'reinforcing' or 'dynamic' in nature. 
Furthermore, the systems thinking approach that would be used for the study to answer the 
research question is the system dynamics modelling. System dynamics modelling, a particular 
approach used for system thinking, would be used in this study to achieve the desired purpose 
of the study. “The systems approach represents a broad view, taking all aspects into account 
and concentrating on interactions between different parts of the problem” (Checkland 1981, 
p.5). A system in this study is not a physical, objective entity, such as a nuclear power station 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 97 
 
would be considered a system, the understanding of a system is as a conceptual idea for viewing 
a problem situation based on Checkland’s views (Onyeagoziri, 2020). Systems thinking was 
also described by Checkland (1979, p.135) “as a method for notating the real world in a manner 
which may not or may plot onto reality; thus, it is a notation which is a choice of the viewer 
and a contribution to epistemology before the contribution of the ontology.” 
Within the range of methodological approaches for systems inquiry, system dynamics 
modelling is a technique that provides insight into the behaviour of complex systems and as 
noted by Wolstenholme (1985) encompasses the feedback or adaptive effects of such systems. 
System dynamics, according to Brent et al. (2018), is seen as a process that aims for the 
consideration of most of the possible impactful elements in a system which initiates their 
connections mainly through modelling. The theory of gender as an approach or procedure 
shows its complex identity (Nightingale, 2006), and this complex identity as a theory or an 
application could be ‘dynamic’ and ‘subjective’ in nature, which would require a systems 
dynamics modelling (Musango et al., 2020) in this study. The importance of systems approach 
in investigating the possibilities of enhancing the deployment of technology within the energy 
sector has been identified by the International Energy Agency (2012). This indicates that there 
could be an increase in the understanding of any complex system by initiating a holistic idea. 
Therefore, systems approaches could help to offer energy access sustainability which would 
help to identify the gendered energy technology access to allow for the security of energy 
services in the poor informal settlement. This would consider the lowest level of energy 
services and the supply of electricity and other clean energy options for the poor informal 
settlement (Shrestha and Acharya, 2015). Furthermore, the systems approach could be required 
for the exploration of energy usage behaviour which is related to energy cultures by involving 
different and common factors to enhance energy security or efficiency. Stephenson et al. 
(2010), indicated that user behaviour can be obtained from the interrelationships between 
components that are unique in a particular group which is expected to provide the same 
behaviour pattern while initiating a common goal, purpose, and functions. 
Additionally, Stephenson et al. (2010) and Shrestha and Acharya (2015) adapted a systems 
model using the systems approach to generated the “energy culture” concepts and “sustainable 
energy access frameworks” respectively which could be used to identify the mechanisms 
influencing the process of energy access in the poor informal settlement. This is related to this 
research study. The systems approach has been applied strategically to several research studies 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 98 
 
in various ways since it was invented by Barry Richmond in 1987 (Hester and  Adams, 2013). 
The systems dynamics modelling has been utilised for studies in strategic planning and 
management (Haines, 2016), in public health problems (Homer and Hirsch, 2006), for 
analysing the design decision parameters which affect product performance and quality 
(Hoffenson and Söderberg, 2015). 
Another systems approach used in the SETILL is systems engineering (SE). The 
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) defines Systems Engineering (SE) 
as a methodology for developing effective systems by integrating various aspects into design 
syntheses through a validation process while considering the system as a whole (INCOSE 
2011). Although systems may have unique characteristics, applying the SE framework 
ensures successful outcomes. SE addresses the entire life cycle of system development and 
operation, ensuring interoperability, functionality, and achievement of objectives (Fumagalli 
2012; Gadre, Esterman, and Thorn 2017). The system life cycle encompasses specific 
activities, including the system's duration, influenced by its nature, complexity, and function. 
This cycle begins with identifying a need in the system design, followed by operational use 
and maintenance until the system's purpose is fulfilled, and finally, it is retired and disposed 
of at the end of its life. This means that at each level of operation, the system design permits 
change towards a better result in totality of the system results (Esa 2006). This was 
particularly relevant in this research study as changes occurred at every stage of the SETILL 
development towards the biogas technology. 
Second, the processes of isolating systems and initiating interactions in the context of energy 
poverty and access require successive action. The Systems Engineering (SE) itself needs an 
understanding of the current environment and upcoming problems to apply the same effectively 
(Fujimoto et al. 2017). This entails considering different strategies in developing the system 
and employing concept analysis of SE to combine the different components (Vezzoli et al. 
2017). The reason for utilizing the SE concept in these research is to create a starting point 
solution to energy poverty as the main problem or principal cause of poverty (Sturdivant 2017). 
The development of SE application takes a structured path whereby the solution results from a 
problem identification, definition of a need, and attainment of a unique and hitherto unachieved 
function. Another important aspect of SE is the determination of interfaces in energy poverty 
and access processes in terms of functions needed and relationships to the problem. Given that 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 99 
 
new requirements are expected in the near future (Bahill et al., 2017; Dick, Hull, and Jackson, 
2017), the logical continuation of SE can provide a sustainable solution to energy poverty. 
Furthermore, because of the rise in new technology issues, the system dynamics modelling was 
utilised to systemise the issues connected to the technology transfer process and their linkages  
(Kalnins and Jarohnovich, 2015). There seem to be challenges in the sustainable energy supply 
(Brent and Kruger, 2009), however, a research study by Roychoudhury et al. (2017) indicated 
that there is a need to utilise systems approach for the learning of climate change as a system 
with interrelationship variables and dynamic patterns. In South Africa, two sustainable 
frameworks have cooperated with the practical shift of renewable technologies, but it is stated 
that the systems analysed lacked the concept of effective sustainability (Brent and Kruger, 
2009). However, this framework could help to actively address energy access sustainability by 
analysing and identifying the different components and interrelationships limiting gendered 
energy access processes. This is also in agreement with this research study. 
Therefore, this research study argues that an extensive, most cooperative method to gendered 
energy technology innovation in a poor informal settlement would: 
1. Make use of the systems approach to understand the influence of the stakeholders to 
attain a sustainable (gendered) energy technology innovation in the Living Lab. 
2. Make use of a “bottom-up approach” for data collection which would specifically 
focus on the context of identifying different typologies of the Living Lab with 
highlights to inequalities. 
3. Make use of transdisciplinary methodologies that can tackle complex and uncertain 
problems. 
4. Make use of literature review, semi-structured interviews, workshops, and other 
techniques that would help to achieve the research goals. 
Therefore, to solve the issues of energy access sustainability in the case study, a systems 
reinforcing model (SRM) by Okoye (2020) will be applied to develop the SETILL model 
leading to the biogas technology and achieve the research result of this study (see figure 2.10).  
The SRM was used to exhibit conceptual flexibility, allowing the incorporation of various 
methodologies to determine specific outcomes. These methodologies must be integrative, 
modelling outcomes for each system as well as the model as a whole (Okoye 2020). To 
illustrate its utility, the SRM applied to this study was employed to create rapid graphic 
representations and evaluations of observations and trade-offs within the study area (see figure 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 100 
 
3.5) . The model is applied iteratively to ensure the development of a high-quality model with 
behaviour and structure that reflect reality (Okoye 2020). The model was also used to determine 
the energy use behaviour in the informal settlements used as case study which were represented 
as the ‘material cultures’, ‘cognitive norm’s and ‘energy practices’. The application process of 
the SRM began with: 
1. Addressing the initial system interface by making design decisions related to material 
cultures, cognitive norms, and energy practices, using a simple stock and flow 
structure based on feedback loops to analyse interrelationships. This is the first step 
which was used to assess the level of energy poverty in this study. 
2. Tackling the second system interface by integrating system designs with design 
decisions to assess their performance and facilitate energy access, thus generating 
additional feedback loops. 
3. Engaging with the final system interface, incorporating the last two system designs, 
which either support or hinder Energy Access Systems (EAS). At this stage, 
secondary data, inferential analysis, and desktop studies are explored and 
systematically integrated. 
Figure 2.11: The systems reinforcing model leading (SRM) leading to energy access 
sustainability (EAS). Source: Okoyo (2020:164) 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 101 
 
Figure 2.11, illustrates the system modelling of interfaces and interconnections, depicted 
through arrays of feedback loops. This representation employs Systems Engineering (SE) with 
Systems Thinking (SsT) analyses, culminating in the development of a proposed new System 
Reference Model (SRM) (Okoye, 2020). 
From the SRM above, four principal system interfaces are identified in the processes of energy 
poverty and access, as adapted from the frameworks of EC (Stephenson et al., 2010) and SEA 
(Shrestha and Acharya, 2015), in which the model presented the interfaces. According to 
Okoye (2020), The first system interface (first row) encompasses systems related to energy 
poverty, including material cultures, cognitive norms, and energy practices, collectively termed 
‘design decisions’ as they evaluate energy poverty levels and influence household energy-use 
behaviour. The second system interface (second row) consists of systems facilitating energy 
access, which rely on the assessments of the ‘design decisions’ systems. The third system 
interface (third row) comprises systems that evaluate the potential benefits of available access 
options. The fourth system interface (fourth row) includes systems that aim to enhance Energy 
Access Services (EAS) when all interfaces align favourably. These systems are termed ‘system 
designs’ as they are deemed adequate to promote energy access in specific regions (Gupta et 
al., 2018; van Gevelt et al., 2018). Each system can be examined independently but within the 
comprehensive system configuration depicted in Figure 2.10. The open-ended looping permits 
the incorporation of new systems, potentially increasing the complexity of system interactions. 
As previously discussed in the introduction chapter of this study, the frameworks of Energy 
Cultures (EC) (Stephenson et al., 2010) and Sustainable Energy Access (SEA) (Shrestha and 
Acharya, 2015) were instrumental in the development of the SRM. These frameworks 
encompass a variety of systems and subsystems that delineate the processes within them. The 
systems can also be used separately which is why this research study decided to apply the row 
1 and row 2 only for the development of the SETILL leading to the biogas technology. 
The systems approach relativity to energy systems in the context of informal settlements 
Systems thinking is an approach that can be used to elaborate and solve energy issues due to 
the dependency of one element on another in energy solutions (Gómez Martín et al., 2020). 
These factors should include the socio-economic context, the physical and infrastructural 
background, and the legal settings of the project together concerning the environment. Through 
systems thinking, that is, through comprehensive or general thinking, stakeholders can form 
better strategies that can solve the present energy requirements and consider the environment 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 102 
 
shaping energy systems (Robertson Munro and Cairney, 2020). The following are some of the 
advantages of using systems engineering when remediating energy issues in informal 
settlements (Parisi et al., 2021). Integration with other community needs, such as water and 
housing, is assured after a comprehensive planning process. Sustainable design centres on the 
efficiency of designs that utilise renewable energy sources and efficient energy technologies. 
The concept of scalability brings flexibility in that solutions can be constructed in easily 
expandable forms to accommodate the growing number of community members or shifted in 
response to changes in the needs of such a community. The reliability improves the ability of 
energy systems to withstand prospective danger and have spare contingencies and backups 
(Hossain et al., 2021). 
In South Africa, some projects have effectively incorporated the systems approach principles 
when creating sustainable energy solutions for vulnerable groups in unsustainable structures 
(Alkhouli, Aly and Johannesburg, 2019). There is a case known as the “iShack Project,” where 
a retrofitting program combines solar power with housing in Stellenbosch. In that sense, the 
project's planning is done according to the systems approach that would imply meeting the 
energy requirements with social, economic, and environmental demands. This is in agreement 
with the view that the provision of clean sources of energy has had positive impacts on people’s 
lives. 
The 
systems 
approach 
application 
to 
the 
South 
African 
context 
In some areas, informal settlement populations experience several difficulties associated with 
energy availability and durability in South Africa (Smit, Musango and Brent, 2019). Many 
settlements do not have the necessary installations and equipment for energy supply and 
distribution. At the same time, the high poverty levels amongst the populace do not allow them 
to access and/or pay for modern energy services. Many challenges formally limit the provision 
of official energy services since the informal settlement in a community seldom falls within 
the recognized regulatory structures. Furthermore, using traditional biomass fuels leads to the 
cutting down of trees for energy and emitting fumes into the atmosphere, which is detrimental 
to the environment (Tomlin, 2021). 
As a result, the following challenges are posed: systems approach presents a useful tool that 
enables consideration of the overall environment in informal settlements. Such findings are 
valuable because understanding connections between energy access and development and 
social and environmental factors supports informing policies and interventions by focusing on 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 103 
 
a broader range of processes and outcomes (Jeuland et al., 2021). For instance, renewable 
energy systems can use renewable energy solutions to reduce energy poverty and create 
employment for young people. 
Systems thinking and engineering approaches in informal settlements should thus be legal and 
regulatory compliant in the countries or regions where they are being implemented. In South 
Africa, the Integrated Resource Plan and the National Development Plan, known as the ‘Vision 
for 2030,’ are government documents containing guidelines on extending access to sustainable 
energy (Angela Mae Minas et al., 2024). With such policies in place, local governments can 
effectively coordinate appropriate community-level energy projects, thus promoting and 
enabling their scalability. Therefore, it is important for governmental organisations, non-
governmental organisations, and the business community to develop a convenient environment 
to sustain such plans. 
Furthermore, some sustainable energy technologies such as solar photovoltaic, biogas 
technologies, and mini-grid systems can be applied in the informal settlements. The systems 
approach can show how solutions can be incorporated to ensure that poor households, 
especially those without functional systems, can access affordable and sustainable energy. This 
correlates well with the text in terms of its focus on discussing and utilising systems approach 
to solve the energy crisis affecting informal settlements. 
Therefore, the effects of energy poverty are widespread, impacting socio-economic conditions, 
ethical considerations (Mehlwana 1997), and the environment (Gulati and Scholtz 2017). 
Systems approach, with its applicability in science (Rousseau 2018) and architecture 
(Meadows 2009), and its established role in Information Technology (IT) and product design 
(Messenger and Ventre 2010), offers a valuable framework for addressing these impacts. 
Implementing Systems approach in energy poverty and access processes fosters innovation 
within social and economic contexts. Systems approach is a fundamental aspect of Systems 
Engineering (SE), facilitating the analysis of complex engineering designs (Bakshi and Fiksel 
2003). Addressing energy poverty through SE naturally incorporates Systems principles 
(Forlizzi, Sevaldson, and Ryan 2017). Chapter 5 and 6 explores the application of the SE with 
SsT approach in developing a new systems energy technology innovation Living Lab 
(SETILL) aimed at improving energy access. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 104 
 
2.4.2 Biogas 
technology 
Biogas technology is a relatively new way of harnessing waste to produce renewable energy 
that is referred to as biogas, which is made of methane and carbon dioxide in large proportions 
(Luostarinen & Normak, 2011). This conversion is referred to as anaerobic digestion, and it 
entails the decomposition of organic inputs like agricultural waste, manure, municipal waste, 
plant biomass, sewage, and food waste using microorganisms that do not require oxygen 
(Luostarinen & Normak, 2011). The outcome is biogas, which can be used as fuel for cooking, 
heating, and the generation of electricity, thus acting as a sustainable and renewable source of 
energy. In the study area, Uganda, biogas is an ideal technology since it solves waste disposal 
problems in most areas with poor waste management systems in the informal settlements as 
well as serves the purpose of an energy source (Sphokazi Phelokazi, 2022). Due to its 
versatility, this technology can be applied more frequently to independents and communities, 
which are often prevalent in squatter camps. Since biogas technology manages to convert waste 
into energy, we can minimise the amount of waste and the risks associated with pollution. 
Mukumba, Makaka, and Mamphweli (2016) argue that biogas technology is another promising 
technology able to help South Africa overcome the shortage of energy sources and record the 
significance of waste management. However, they stress the need to establish adequate policies 
and a conducive environment that will encourage the rapid deployment of this technology. 
Secondly, they encouraged that communities should embrace the projects and be informed, as 
this will increase the chances of the successful advancement of biogas projects. 
Biogas digesters operate based on providing a specific environment where anaerobic 
microorganisms will break down organic matter (Achinas and Euverink, 2020). The process 
involves several stages: First, the process involves hydrolysis; second, it involves acid 
oogenesis; third, it involves acetogenesis, followed by methanogenesis. Various complex 
organic compounds get resolved or split into soluble molecules in hydrolysis. The process, 
often called acidogenesis, transforms these molecules into volatile fatty acids (Ramos-Suarez 
et al., 2021). In acetogenesis, these acids are further degraded to acetic acid, hydrogen, and 
carbon dioxide. Lastly, the methanogenesis of the products creates methane and carbon-dioxide 
to form biogas. The direct utilisation of biogas is applicable as fuel for cooking and heating or 
can be upgraded to electricity. This is in line with this research study. 
These biogas plants or systems vary depending on the kind of system and circumstances 
involved. For small-scale producers, family-sized digesters include fixed-dome or floating-


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 105 
 
drum systems installed in rural houses on average (Rasimphi et al., 2022). These systems are 
cheap & appropriate for handling organic waste from homes and animal manure. Small-scale 
digesters are suitable for single farm holdings or even small communities, while larger-scale 
digesters are suitable for larger volumes of organic waste (Huber, 2019). Industrial-scale 
digesters are used in industries, municipalities, and large farming operations. Large-scale 
digesters are planned for extensive production, waste management, and farming communities 
(Budiman, 2021). These systems are usually more comprehensive and may incorporate other 
sub-systems for purification and storage of the biogas, thus making them ideal for large-scale 
energy generation. 
Building 
a 
sustainable 
energy 
tomorrow: 
Biogas 
in 
South 
Africa 
South Africa, known for its diverse landscapes and abundant biodiversity, South Africa is on 
the brink of an energy transformation. With a population exceeding 60 million and rapid 
industrial growth, the nation’s energy demands have surged significantly, and currently, South 
Africa's energy infrastructure is predominantly dependent on traditional energy sources, with 
coal playing a major role (Green Agri, 2023). Over 80% of the country's electricity is coal-
generated, positioning it among the top global carbon emitters (CSIR, 2023). As of 2022, South 
Africa's wholesale/public nominal capacity stood at 54 GW, with coal accounting for more 
than 40 GW (CSIR, 2023). The nation's reliance on fossil fuels has continued to rise, driving 
energy demand to unprecedented levels. 
Furthermore, the heavy dependence on coal and oil for electricity generation has severe 
environmental consequences. The rapid growth of South Africa's economy has led to a sharp 
rise in carbon dioxide emissions, placing the country among the top global emitters, and it is 
now imperative to transition to more sustainable energy sources (Green Agri, 2023). In this 
context, biogas emerges as a viable option that could play a crucial role in advancing South 
Africa's energy sector. Therefore, this research study has identified the biogas technology as 
the technology to be developed during the testing of the SETILL model. Thus, the biogas 
technology would be developed and tested to a pilot scale and this study will explore the largely 
untapped potential of biogas in South Africa, examine its benefits, and discuss how it can help 
lead the nation toward a cleaner, more sustainable future. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 106 
 
Figure 2.12: The Anaerobic Digestion Process. Source: Green Agri (2023) 
A report from the International Energy Agency (IEA) suggests that by 2030, biogas could meet 
up to 4% of South Africa's overall energy needs. Biogas, a renewable energy source, is 
generated from organic waste through anaerobic digestion (figure 2.12). It can be utilised for 
electricity generation, heating, and as a fuel, while also offering additional benefits like waste 
management, water purification, and fertiliser production, also renewable and sustainable 
energy option, biogas holds significant potential to tackle the challenges of climate change, 
enhance energy security, and create jobs in South Africa (Green Agri, 2023). 
Furthermore, several studies have also investigated and reviewed the adoption decisions and 
use of biogas technology as an alternative energy source in Africa, the mechanisms influencing 
the biogas technology household decisions and adoptions, the economic possibilities of the 
biogas and the effect in reducing dependency on fossil fuels by households, and the 
socioeconomic influence of the biogas and greater public participation of the biogas technology 
as an alternative energy source in Africa and globally (Mengistu et al., 2016; Etsay et al., 2017; 
Anindita et al., 2020; Iqbal et al., 2021). In view of the above, this research study adopted the 
development of the biogas digester as the technology to be used for the validation and testing 
of the SETILL in the selected case study. 
There are more than 200 ‘biogas digesters’ found around South Africa, however, the use of 
biogas technology, in which the gas is made up of ‘methane (CH4) and carbon dioxide (C02) 
is slow in adoption and growth in South Africa, regardless of its importance to mitigating 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 107 
 
energy poverty (Mukumba, Makaka and Mamphweli, 2016). Biogas technology can be seen as 
an important technology to reducing poverty in the informal settlements and bringing 
development because it is suitable for energy recovery and it can also be used for generating 
electricity during load shedding and it can most importantly be used for clean cooking. Clean 
cooking fuel has been affected more in the integration of different energy sectors and the option 
of a clean cooking fuel policy is very limited (Rosenthal et al., 2018). 
Therefore, the development of the usage of clean cooking technology, such as the biogas for 
low-and-middle-income households (LMIC) in South Africa has the potential to promote at 
least four sustainable development goals (SDGS), such as SDG 3 (good health and wellbeing), 
SDG 5 (gender equality), SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG 15 (life on land), 
(Rosenthal et al., 2018), and the substantial use of productive energy appliances has been 
highlighted by the International Energy Agency (2019) for achieving a sustainable goal on the 
efficiency of energy until the year 2040. 
Furthermore, the biogas technology innovation which can be used for the generation of 
electricity and clean cooking (see figure 2.13) lags behind and with a low success rate in Africa, 
because of the wrong cultural assumptions which would have been tackled during the execution 
or testing phases (Claire et al., 2021). Therefore, this research study would endeavour to design 
the biogas technology to a pilot scale using the bottom-up energy innovation decision-making 
approach with a focus more on women in the selected case study. This implies that the 
identified end-users (women) will be involved with the other stakeholders or the role gender 
plays in energy innovation would inform the product of knowledge to the energy innovation 
product consumption through the systems Living Lab in the selected case study. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 108 
 
 
Figure 2.13: A Lamp (electricity) and Stove (clean cooking) generated from a typical biogas 
technology. Source: Wassie and Adaramola (2020:5).   
Benefits 
and 
challenges 
of 
biogas 
technology 
in 
the 
African 
context 
There are therefore multiple advantages to the adoption of biogas technology, especially in 
poor environments. Firstly, through biogas technology, people are in a position to cut short 
their reliance on conventional bioenergy sources, including wood, charcoal, and animal dung 
(Luostarinen & Normak, 2011). Current or probable sources of biomass fuels are not only 
inefficient but also fuel deforestation, with adverse impacts on human health due to air 
pollution (Pereira et al., 2020). Compared to traditional biomass, there is a positive change in 
energy balance where biogas offers a cleaner and more efficient way of energy utilisation, 
resulting in positive indoor air quality and a reduction in respiratory illnesses (Luostarinen & 
Normak, 2011). Ahmed, McGough, and Mateo-Garcia (2017) talk about the peculiarities of 
biogas technology and, while concerned with its applicability to urban transitions, mention the 
retrofitting aspect of it. They note that biogas technology holds potential for reducing green 
gas emissions because it captures methane from organic waste products that would normally 
release green gases into the atmosphere when decomposing. 
Also, it further strengthens waste handling in the sense that, through biogas technology, waste 
products such as methane are produced and can be of great use (Schnürer, 2016). Acid 
byproducts include digestate that can be utilised as nutrient sources, ideal for supporting 
sustainable farming (Schnürer, 2016). This closed-loop mechanism is applicable for dealing 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 109 
 
with waste disposal problems, as well as enhancing soil health and agricultural yields. This has 
specifically been underscored by Botha et al. (2012) in their call as to how Living Labs could 
be used to boost the uptake of biogas technology in South Africa as a vehicle for innovation. 
They state that users of living Labs mention that the technology of the biogas should be adapted 
to local conditions, which means it can only be developed together with the users, which is 
very helpful when generalising the results achieved. 
As a result, biogas technology has the following hindering challenges despite holding 
promising benefits: Among the main challenges, high capital investment at the beginning of 
plant construction can be mentioned. Biogas digesters and related infrastructure are capital-
intensive investments to implement and can only be easily achieved or implemented, which 
presents major challenges in many communities through the raising of huge capital (Tercanli 
and Jongbloed, 2022). Other issues include the maintenance and operational needs of the 
facilities, which are often costly to acquire and maintain. It is, however, important that biogas 
plants be monitored and maintained on a regular basis to optimise their production. The 
technical skills for maintenance are complicated, which makes it difficult for organisations to 
address the needs arising from informal settlements, resulting in operational breakdowns. As 
pointed out by Ersoy and Van Bueren (2020), there is a need to ensure protection of any 
investment in biogas through culture creation that involves the local human capital, thus 
enabling them to possess technical details in supporting the projects. 
Another challenge is that of inadequate awareness and information concerning the effects 
seminal to biogas technology. There is low awareness of the likelihood and functionality of 
biogas systems among many communities hence serve as a hindrance to the advancement in 
the use of biogas systems. Nonetheless, Anditi et al. (2022) quite concurring with this opinion 
pointed out that a number of appreciation-raising universities, communities and Education 
programs should be carried out to create awareness of the possibility of biogas technology and 
how it can be incorporated into their practice. Biogas technology utilisation is also dependent 
by the gender roles and relations in the societies involved. Musango et al. (2020) have also 
highlighted the notion of gender as crucial in enhancing the implementation of energy 
innovations in developing sectors, such as informal settlements. They have come up with a 
gender analysis framework for the way biogas projects can be implemented to embrace gender 
concerns and the respective roles of women in society. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 110 
 
Successful 
implementation 
of 
the 
biogas 
technology 
In particular, several cases of biogas technology in the context of the Community Participatory 
Technology assist in the evaluation of the study (BOURDIN, RAULIN and JOSSET, 2020). A 
specific case to illustrate this is the Integrated Rural Development and Nature Conservation 
(IRDNC) undertaking in the Eastern Cape province of South Africa (Lubilo and Hebinck, 
2019). In this regard, this project was centred on a technology by which small-scale biogas 
digesters were provided to the rural poor in their homes and used cattle dung and other organic 
wastes to generate biogas for cooking and heating. In addition to a availability of energy, the 
implementation of the project meant a decrease in the utilization of timber, as well as emission 
of toxic gases within homes. From this project, one of the important successes was the 
identification of the community members’ participation with effectiveness at the initial stage 
of the project. Therefore, the biogas systems were designed based on the ideal ratio of the 
requirements of community. 
Another good example is the project in Nairobi initiated by an organisation known as Umande 
Trust, where they installed biogas toilets in the informal settlement (Juma, 2020). These toilets 
are designed to facilitate waste disposal for human use and energy production in terms of 
biogas. The project has proved most successful in areas where people live, compounded in 
urban centres, and where conventional waste disposal and management was ineffective. Some 
use the biogas for cooking and lighting. This, in turn, enhances living standards and affords the 
population a clean energy source instead of hazardous charcoal. This success story is, therefore, 
a good lesson that biogas technology has to be taken together with other services in the 
community to come up with packages. 
Closely associated with the success of these projects are the implementation strategies that 
entailed community participation and capacity enhancement, as well as monitoring and 
evaluating the project process and outcomes (Capdevila et al., 2020). Otherwise, solutions 
developed without community participation will not likely be culturally sensitive and embraced 
by people. Sensitization, skills development, and technology transfer enhance the ability of the 
residents to run and maintain the biogas systems (Tundup et al., 2021). Ongoing evaluation and 
assessment, therefore, help in the early detection of any challenges that are solved, leading to 
the permanent durability of the projects (Williams et al., 2019). 
What can be learned from these successful implementations is that there must be involvement 
of people in the community where the biogas systems are being implemented; it helps when 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 111 
 
biogas technology is adapted to the prevailing conditions in a particular community and biogas 
implementation must be done hand in hand with other services in the community. Moreover, 
there is a need for proper sustainable funding to avoid the disappearance of models that work, 
and collaboration with different stakeholders is essential to increase the successful models’ 
capacities (Wondirad, Tolkach and King, 2020). 
2.4.3 Poor 
informal 
settlements 
The SDG 11’s aim to build sustainable cities and societies is related to the understanding of 
the poor informal settlements which is also connected to the other SDG 5 and other SDGs 
(Johnson, Gerber and Muhoza, 2019). The potential growth of the poor informal settlements in 
Africa has been given just a little awareness even though more women and girls make up the 
poor informal settlements in Africa and Asia (Chant and McIlwaine, 2015). Therefore, 
Musango et al. (2020, p.7), stated that “linking SDG 11 with gender mainstreaming to support 
SDG 5 regarding gender equality is therefore indispensable”. 
The informal settlement is defined by Stats SA (HAD, 2012) and the Cape Town Municipality 
(Stats SA, 2016) as an unintended informal dwelling or community which did not go through 
the survey and which is not recognised as a place of residence. The informal settlement can 
also be called the shacks and squatter. Therefore, this poor informal settlement tends to be 
poorly developed and mostly socially, economically, and environmentally sustainable. Figure 
2.14 shows the total dwellers in South Africa from 1996 to 2016. 
 
Figure 2.14: Compiled informal dwellers in South Africa. Source: Stats SA (2016). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 112 
 
The term poor informal settlements (Emina et al., 2011), is also referred to as; poor 
neighbourhoods (Barnes, 2001; Muyeba and Seekings, 2012), poor environments (Musango et 
al., 2020), poor community (Mdluli and Vogel, 2010; Owusu and Afutu-Kotey, 2010), poor 
areas (Tumwebaze and Lüthi, 2013; Oyolola and Abuya, 2014), poor settings (Kharboush et 
al., 2005; Emetere, 2019), and poor economy (Knight and Sabot, 1982). Therefore, the issue 
of the informal settlement relating to gender and the problems faced by women residing in the 
poor informal settlements has been talked about by (Chant and McIlwaine, 2015). However, 
their explanations and definitions do not include the security of energy services or energy 
access. 
Therefore, this informal settlement is seen as poor places, and the World Bank (2018) has 
indicated that Sub-Saharan Africa is seen as one of the main places prone to poverty. Therefore, 
any kind of poverty, including energy poverty which is the focus of this research study, is 
prevalent in the informal settlements. Therefore, this research study is focused on poor rural 
informal settlements of South Africa. However, from the view of energy poverty, there are 
implications relating to ‘gender’ which are; the use of time (biomass collections), fertile and 
planned basic needs (electricity), household safety (community Lighting), planned livelihood 
(micro-business), health implications (smoking coming from cooking) (Clancy and Skutsch, 
2003). 
Therefore, relating to the focus of this research study, poverty is regarded as the lack of access 
to energy services or basic needs, and an informal settlement is seen as a place lacking access 
to energy services and energy security, thereby, being unable to meet their basic needs. Also, 
the people who suffer most in these informal settlements are the women. 
Energy needs and challenges in the informal settlements: current energy access and usage 
patterns 
Energy access and use or emerge as a factor deeply unequal in diagrams and largely 
dependent on traditional energy resources in the investigated IHs (Oswald et al., 2020). Most 
of the inhabitants living in these settlements rely on biomass energy which include firewood 
and charcoal and kerosene among others. These sources are commonly used for cooking, 
heating and lighting since these are cheaper and more easily accessible to people compared to 
today’s energy forms such as electricity and liquefied petroleum gas (LPG) (Puzzolo et al., 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 113 
 
2019). Biomass fuels, however, come with a number of difficulties; they are not efficient and 
pose health issues from indoor pollution. 
Apparently, the energy utilization profiles in the IECs tend to be associated with the 
demographics and income standards of the households and existing energy supplies. Usually, 
the energy use is low given that only a small proportion of the population has access to 
modern energy services and owing to the high energy–income ratio (Jeuland, Biggs, & 
White, 2021). Majority of households only use energy for cooking and limited lighting hence 
leaving little power for other uses which are refrigeration, communication and entertainment 
(Aliero et al., 2021). This limited energy consumption limits economic growth and 
development and) nullifies quality of lives, keeping the community stuck in poverty. 
Some disparities in energy access become very noticeable in the informal settlements 
especially because development infrastructure in such areas is usually limited compared to the 
developed urban and rural areas. The absence of legal electricity relationships and secure 
energy contributes to making many families poor (Adom et al., 2021). On this regard, this gap 
is worsened by the fact most of these settlements are informal and rarely included in the 
national energy planning and policies (Kovacic et al., 2020). Prospects remain bleak for 
residents to attain overall energy security and end-users are forced to use costly and unreliable 
means of power in their homes. 
Socioeconomic 
and 
environmental 
impacts 
A priority that is linked with energy poverty in induced informal settlements has far reaching 
socioeconomic implication. Socio economically, energy affects household usage of income 
whereby a lot of money is utilized to cater for energy thus reducing the income available to pay 
for education health and other basic facilities. Dependence on kerosene and charcoal as source 
of energy is very expensive and since they depend on market forces their prices are also subject 
to change hence the economic vulnerability of the households (Ang’u, 2023). Also, very few 
people can afford a reliable source of energy and energy prices are very high which derails 
investment in income-generating activities hence slows the economic growth and poverty 
eradication. 
Energy poverty is a major issue having crippled health implications as well as adverse effects 
on the environment. The burning of biomass fuels to cook and heat causes emissions and fumes 
that endanger people’s indoor health by giving them diseases related to the respiratory system. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 114 
 
Compared to outdoor pollution, indoor pollutants resulting from the use of traditional ways of 
cooking were severely cited for having caused the loss of millions of lives every year, adversely 
affecting women and children since they spend most of their time indoors (Dida et al., 2022). 
Firewood and charcoal are an environmental nuisance because of the provision of raw materials 
for fuel, leading to deforestation and further land degradation, supporting structures 
undermining the ecosystems, and enhancing climate change (Leal Filho et al., 2021). These 
environmental impacts also pose further risks to communities' livelihoods, specifically those 
reliant on natural products. 
Thus, the effects of energy poverty vary across genders; this is especially the case with informal 
settlements. The impact of energy scarcity has an even more severe effect on women and girls 
in society because they are mostly involved in managing energy within households (Petrova 
and Simcock, 2019). This adds stress and time to looking for firewood and other traditional 
fuels and reduces their time for education, income, and social activities. Unlike in the past, this 
time, they replicated the gender disparities and spreaded their life chances for personal and 
economic transformation (Takamäki, 2020). In addition, they noted that health risks associated 
with substantial exposure to IAP are more pronounced among women, thereby making them 
even more vulnerable. 
As for gender, it is important to understand that women living in informal settlements have 
better yet different energy access and use prospects (Pearl-Martinez, 2020). It establishes them 
as responsible for providing energy to their homes, thereby making them over-reliant on 
inefficient and dangerous fuels. This means that the time and energy spent gathering the 
firewood or buying the fuel hinders them from their opportunity to get an education or earn an 
income. Also, women lack proper access to funds, credit, and decision-making, so they have 
poor bargaining power (Guvuriro and Booysen, 2019). Most of the time, they cannot buy 
modern energy solutions. This exclusion from the energy sector influences the entrenched 
gender inequalities while at the same time retarding efforts at attaining energy security. 
However, there are opportunities for women in energy solutions in the informal settlements, 
especially since the field has numerous challenges, as mentioned. Enabling and supporting 
women with a specific focus through energy programs will likely bring about a drastic change 
in the accessibility and utilisation of energy besides the female status. For instance, it is 
conceivable that engaging women in the conception, planning, and deployment of energy 
projects increases the relevance of the solutions delivered (Chirambo, 2020). Women can be 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 115 
 
helped to become energy entrepreneurs and create awareness of sustainable energy 
technologies like solar-based lamps, clean cooking stoves, and biogas plants (Mukhopadhyay, 
2020). These initiatives also create improved prospects about the availability of energy, 
economic reality, and the overall status of women. 
2.5 
Conclusion and remarks 
This chapter has systematically reviewed the main areas and concepts of the research study 
(see figure 2.1), and identified systems approach as a powerful way of solving energy problems 
in informal settlement when adopted in the Living Labs with gender design. Consequently, the 
focus is on including multiple perspectives and user-driven approaches, thus increasing the 
energy solutions' efficacy and stability. The information on the South African and the general 
African experiences underscore the applicability of the SETILL model to advance energy 
development and promote energy access and utilisation sustainably. So, the systematic review 
should continue, and Living Lab methodologies and practices should be refined and extended 
to face the everyday challenges of the transformative discourse of the energy sector in Africa. 
Through such interactions, participants can identify viable ways through which different 
stakeholders can work together in a co-creation process to identify real solutions that can be 
implemented by all the parties to enhance the efficient use of energy by all the individuals 
within the society. 
2.5.1 Integration 
of 
the 
three-level 
literature 
review 
framework 
This review chapter has shown that by adopting systems approach in the Living Lab and 
incorporating biogas technology into the analysis, it is possible to come up with a sound 
conceptual framework to address problems in the energy sector of the African context of 
informal settlements. Combining these approaches leverages both strengths: biogas technology 
will offer an efficient and sustainable energy source (Ani, Aniokete and Agbo, 2024), and the 
ability to test the SETILL. At the same time, applications of systems approach will enable 
solutions to fit into the specific social context (York et al., 2019). Technologies such as biogas, 
where waste material is converted to biogas through a method known as anaerobic digestion, 
help solve waste elimination and energy production dilemmas (Obileke et al., 2020). While 
rational planning offers a more organised approach, systems approaches focus on hierarchy, 
networking, and interrelatedness of the socio-economic, environmental, and infrastructure put 
in place in the informal settlements (Okoye, 2020). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 116 
 
A sustainable intervention in informal settlement design addresses the multiple interactions in 
the physical context of the area (Christley et al., 2021). This means appreciating the fact that 
energy issues do not come singly but are just a slice of a number of issues touching on such 
aspects as waste disposal, health, economic advancement, and general sustainability of the 
environment. According to the community-oriented systems, representatives and decision-
makers can approach the case considering the situation from a more general point of view 
more focused on the achievement of several goals. For example, the technology used in 
biogas production is considered as a cleaner source of energy. It improves the management of 
wastes and diminishes the contact with indoor emissions besides offering an opportunity in 
generating income from other sources such as sale of excess biogas or quality fertilizers 
(Munga, 2020). 
According to Torres (2020), the steps involved in introducing the biogas technology in the 
informal settlements are: First of all, when establishing a program and setting goals and 
objectives one should consult and scan the community needs and assets along with possible 
and gaping barriers. With this assessment, the biogas systems designed to operate by fitting 
into the local environment as their goal is set at achieving the carbon offsets. Further, 
engaging in the mutually agreed upon decision making means the community is in charge of 
the interventions and ensures that the implemented solutions are socially relevant and 
optimal. The following involves pilot testing and further synchronisation of the biogas 
systems because of the feedback from the people living in the area. Last is the replication of 
successful initiatives in the extension of microfinance services through the attraction of 
funds, skills, and partnerships with the governmental and non-governmental organizations, 
businesses and industries. 
Moreover, the women play an important role in energy innovation activities because they 
increase the likelihood of positive change that is generally favorable and comprehensive to a 
majority of people. Several times, too, female voices when included translate to occupying a 
place with ideas and revealed insights that enhance the behind the scene workings of the energy 
sector (Habiyaremye, 2019). Thus, adopting gender mainstreaming could ensure more 
extensive engagement of women in Living Labs projects, apply their perspectives and expertise 
for co-creating energy innovations not only for the benefit of the participants but also for the 
improvement of the energy framework (Fortiş & Petcu, 2022). 
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 117 
 
Chapter 1 
Conceptual Framework of the SETILL model 
1.1 Introduction 
This chapter discusses the transitional theory of the processes of the phenomena being studied 
and why. It is the development of the procedures for the phenomena being investigated. The 
conceptual framework for this study is not essentially a literature review because it can be 
created in the study by the insights on the phenomena and uses several pieces of information 
to illustrate the phenomenon being studied (Maxwell, 1996). The conceptual framework was 
linked to Chapter 1 and Chapter 2 of this thesis where the background, overview, and review 
of the literature of the study and topic were analysed and discussed. This implies that this 
chapter will partially not be repeating the background, overview and review of works of 
literature on the topic, but will discuss further the topic while leading to the development of 
the SETILL theoretical model with gender design to support the energy innovation to be 
produced and tested to a pilot scale at the selected case study. 
Therefore, this chapter represents a conceptual framework for a Systemic Living Lab intending 
to promote energy technology innovation from the perspective of gender in South African 
informal settlements. The conceptual framework for this study is a synthesis of the theory of a 
Living Lab concept, which highlights the dynamic participation of stakeholders, SsT/SE and 
co-creating solutions, intending to empower women and tackle gender inequalities in the 
context of energy security and energy innovation. These theories including the characteristics 
of the informal settlements, the social, and cultural norms and economic mechanisms, and the 
SRM for EAS, EC and SEA frameworks contributing to gender equality within the South 
African informal settlements with access to energy services, women's participation and 
decision making were discussed and reviewed in chapter 1 and chapter 2 of this thesis. 
Therefore, by linking Chapter 1 and Chapter 2 to Chapter 3 of this thesis, the conceptual 
framework is developed, leading to the SETILL model. 
Furthermore, to share ideas in a connected manner and generate a substantial ‘space’ for co-
designing and co-innovation to achieve a particular goal, the end users (women in this context) 
and other stakeholders in the public and private sectors should cooperatively work together 
(Mukama et al., 2022). The space referred to in this context is called the Living Lab. This 
chapter also explores the approach of the Living Lab as reviewed in the Literature chapter for 
energy technology innovation in an informal settlement with a gendered perspective. The 
Living Lab, if properly governed, can be seen as a vital innovation environment which focuses 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 118 
 
on problem-solving and the development of technological innovations with the consideration 
of compound methods like social, environmental, usability, technical, etc (Mukama et al., 
2022). 
Therefore, the importance of involving end-users in co-designing innovations has been often 
spoken about in literature (Ballon, Pierson and Delaere, 2005; Benoît Dutilleul, 2010; Almirall, 
Lee and Wareham, 2012; Dell’Era and Landoni, 2014). This collaboration allows for the end-
users in the energy households/sectors, health sectors, etc, to participate in the designing and 
testing of technology innovations which leads to an adoption or acceptance of the innovation. 
This participation with the end-users can either be done as end-users becoming the head 
developers (Von Hippel, 2001), like the women in the communities deciding on the innovation 
to be developed or the users participating together as partners with the stakeholders to co-create 
innovations (Brown, 2008). Thus, the Living Lab can then be seen as the equal participation of 
the end-users and stakeholders to co-create and test innovations in real-world scenarios 
(Almirall, Lee and Wareham, 2012). In recent years, the Living Labs has been used in different 
fields even in the social science fields for technological improvements (Ahmadi et al., 2020), 
which can be seen as the ‘social innovation environment’ (Edwards‐Schachter, 2012). 
Therefore, this research study agrees with the statement above that Living Lab should also be 
seen as a ‘social innovation environment.’  
Furthermore, the Living Lab can be of great importance and has some aspects of distinctiveness 
to possible co-creation, making use of real-life scenarios and make use of end-users (Dell’Era 
and Landoni, 2014), to achieve technological advancement. The main peculiar area of the 
Living Lab is the involvement of end-users from the design phases down to the testing phases 
of the innovations, which indicates that the main focus of the technological innovations is the 
end-users (Almirall, Lee and Wareham, 2012). This allows for the conceptualisation of 
knowledge and opinions of the end-users (women) in the case to be studied. The Living Lab 
can also be used as a mediator between different stakeholders like the researchers, 
communities, governments, etc, to evaluate technological innovations (Almirall, Lee and 
Wareham, 2012; Dupont et al., 2017), for proper adoption of the innovations, since the Living 
Lab environment can only be conducted by the stakeholders to give value to the technological 
innovation. The Living Lab can also serve as an example to the private and public sectors 
towards the importance of participatory technological innovations and co-designing 
innovations to bring solutions (Burbridge, 2017; Dupont et al., 2017), to the energy needs of 
the informal settlements. The Living Lab as an innovation environment allows for the 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 119 
 
generation of new and more detailed ideas by the end-users on the particular technology 
innovation to be developed, allows for the adoption of the innovation due to the testing of the 
innovation and allows for the impact of the technology innovation to be easily experienced by 
all stakeholders involved (Dell’Era and Landoni, 2014).  
Therefore, according to this research study, the development of the systems Living Lab is to 
support energy technology innovation with a gendered perspective, and also give more 
understanding to the Living Lab technology. A study has advanced the understanding and best 
application of the Living Labs to support gendered energy technology innovation (Mukama et 
al., 2022), however, this study did not make use of the SsT/SE approaches in the Living Lab
