to develop the technology innovation and also did not go further to develop or test any 
particular innovation in an informal settlement in South Africa. Therefore, this research study 
will contribute to the understanding and application of the Living Lab technology to support 
energy technology innovation with a gender design and also go further to develop and test an 
energy innovation to a pilot scale (Biogas technology) using the SsT and SE approaches, hence 
the model to be developed in this research study is called the systems energy technology 
innovation Living Lab (SETILL) with gender design. The SETILL will most importantly 
contribute to ‘gendered-energy informal settlement nexus research’ with the Living Lab in 
South Africa. 
Therefore, there is a need for more contributions and understanding of the Living Lab to 
support gendered energy technology innovation in South Africa. This is because, at the 
moment, the application of the Living Lab in the energy environment and Africa is very lean 
(Mukama et al., 2022), especially in South Africa. Furthermore, this chapter attempts or gives 
a leading role to answer research question 4 and validates research question 1 of this research 
study which made use of the literature review approach in chapter 2 and linked it to this chapter 
3. The literature search was done and analysed from google scholar, library genesis, university 
databases and websites of existing Living Labs focused on the context of the study and the 
systems thinking approaches to further lead to the SETILL and the research questions of this 
study. The search words used were: ‘systems thinking’, ‘systems Living Lab’, ‘Living Labs’, 
‘gendered Living Labs’, ‘energy stakeholders and end-users, ‘energy Living Labs’, ‘poor 
informal settlements’, ‘energy security’, ‘women empowerment’, etc. 
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 120 
 
1.2 Building the Conceptual Framework 
Miles and Huberman (1984) referred to a conceptual framework as a graphical or narrative 
conceptualization of the vital phenomena under investigation: the principal factors, variables 
and their supposed interrelationships. The principal insights in the conceptual framework are 
linked to each other and give an understanding of the relationship between events, places, and 
people, allowing for predictions grounded on events. The data constructed on the conceptual 
model (figure 3.1) are introduced by the use of a ‘concept analysis’ procedure which allows for 
exploring, defining and clarifying the concept (Xyrichis & Ream, 2007). The phenomenon 
under investigation was acquired by determining the antecedents, attributes and consequences. 
The conceptual model below shows the ‘antecedents’ as the examined events about the people, 
problems or settings under study. It also shows the ‘attributes’ of literature, individual 
experiences and preparatory investigations that will be used to understand the people or 
problems being studied. Finally, it also includes the ‘consequences’ of theories, beliefs and 
preceding discoveries informing the research study. Below is the description of the conceptual 
model for the study: 
 
 
 
Figure 3.1: Conceptual framework for the theoretical model of the research study. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 121 
 
Figure 3.1 above describes the factors that were present before the SETILL and the positive 
outcomes that are expected if the SETILL and the energy innovation are achieved. The 
conceptual model does not necessarily direct the research; however, it provides a prior insight 
into the events. 
1.2.1 
The antecedents 
The antecedents in this research study are the happening events before the concept occurred. 
To identify the significance of the development of SETILL and improve energy security and 
gender equality in the energy case study, it was necessary to identify the issues faced by the 
people and settings being studied. These initially identified antecedents in the research study, 
were all connected to energy poverty and gendered energy technology used to investigate 
various editions of the concept. 
1.2.2 
The attributes 
The attributes are the repeated various occurrences of the concept with the most periodic union 
to the concept. These initially identified antecedents in the research study, were also all 
connected to energy poverty and gendered energy technology used to investigate various 
editions of the concept. 
1.2.3 
The consequences 
The consequences are events that occurred due to the presence of the concept. To identify the 
significance of the development of SETILL and improve energy security and gender equality 
in the energy case study, it was necessary to identify the theories, beliefs and preceding findings 
guiding the research study. These initially identified antecedents in the research study, were 
also all connected to energy poverty and gendered energy technology used to investigate 
various editions of the concept. 
Additionally, all the research questions are linked to the objectives of the research, conceptual 
framework, methods and validity. Answers to the research questions will assist in realising the 
research aim. Therefore, to answer the research questions, the research will be justified by a 
theoretical model of SETILL as exemplifying the behaviour of a system that can produce 
thriving, sustainable gendered energy technology innovation. Therefore, the answers to 
research question 1 of ‘What is the conceptualization of the systems Living Lab’ will also be 
attained in this chapter. 
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 122 
 
1.3 The Living Lab concepts as applied to the SETILL model 
The energy efficiency market is controlled by an innovative techno-centric model, which 
indicates that users are the logical decision-makers who will effectively accept and use the 
energy technologies once they are informed of the gain the system has to the economic and 
environmental aspects (Caird and Roy, 2008). The Living Lab concepts have been discussed 
in Chapter 2 of this research study and will be briefly discussed in this chapter, leading to the 
SETILL model.  
Therefore, it has been noticed in this research study that the definitions of the Living Labs as a 
concept are peculiarly dependent on the field it is used for. Generally, the Living Lab involves 
studying and developing innovations in a real-world environment like the communities 
(Mukama et al., 2022). This allows for the visibility and installation of the technology 
innovations in the communities, thereby impacting them (Ballon, Pierson and Delaere, 2005; 
Hossain, Leminen and Westerlund, 2019) and leading to the adoption and acceptance of the 
innovations. Furthermore, due to the participation of end-users in co-creating technology 
innovations in the Living Labs and the real-world environment, Dell’Era and Landoni (2014) 
argued that the definitions of the Living Lab research lacked the inclusion of producing novel 
innovations in the Living Lab, which is why Living Lab was defined as “a design research 
methodology aimed at co-creating innovation through the involvement of aware users in a real-
life setting” Dell’Era and Landoni (2014:139). 
Furthermore, during the study carried out by Hossain, Leminen and Westerlund (2019:2), the 
Living Lab was defined as “a physical or virtual space in which to solve societal challenges, 
especially for urban areas by bringing together various stakeholders for collaboration and 
collective ideation”. These definitions identified above are all in agreement with this research 
study, however, this study disagrees with the Living Lab being specifically for the urban 
environment, but the Living Lab should be developed anywhere (rural and urban) for the 
benefit of the communities or stakeholders involved (Almirall, Lee and Wareham, 2012). The 
Living Lab as a resilient methodology is fully focused on the ideas or insights obtained from 
the end-users (Almirall, Lee and Wareham, 2012), to achieve a specific objective (Ahmadi et 
al., 2020), which allows for various designs in the Living Lab suited for the technology 
innovations to be developed (Mukama et al., 2022). Therefore, in the case of the women in the 
informal settlement who are not empowered, are marginalised and do not have the 
opportunities to co-design the innovations, the Living Lab will allow them to contribute and 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 123 
 
co-design the energy innovations in the communities. This is referred to as the Living Lab as 
a ‘safe space’ (Ahmadi et al., 2020). 
The Living Lab as a methodology is classified into the following according to Almirall, Lee 
and Wareham (2012): User-Centric Design (UCD), design-driven, and participatory-driven. 
The UCD has been introduced in Chapter 1 of this study and this is the process where the main 
passive collaborators are the end-users. The UCD is introduced in this research study as the 
research study intends to develop a new technology innovation to be tested and to analyse how 
technologies are affected by human factors (Mukama et al., 2022). This is because, in a 
research area, the Living Lab was defined by (Schaer, Schaible and Müller, 2020) as “a user-
centric study methodology for researchers to evaluate the performance of retrieval systems 
within real-world applications”. This implies that the end-users are to be involved in the 
research as co-researchers to achieve a common goal. Therefore, the definitions above and the 
Living Lab concepts as applied to SETILL can be suitable to the phenomena being studied 
because it involves the ‘end-users’ and the ‘real-world testing environment’. These major 
theories will be applied to the SETILL with gender designs. 
Therefore, after looking at the concepts of different Living Lab in Chapter 2 and Chapter 3 
respectively, the critical elements of the Living Labs that support innovation are: “Living Lab 
methodology”, “ICT and infrastructure”, “Living Lab management”, “partners and users”, and 
also “research” (Jere et al., 2014). The interactions necessary for innovation are depicted by 
the model according to the figure below, which illustrates a basic Living Lab concept. 
 
 
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 124 
 
Figure 3.2: Basic Living Lab concept: in Co-design Process. Source: Eriksson et al., (2016:7). 
According to Figure 3.2 above, if innovation is produced with any of the two elements found 
in the diagram, there would be a lack of severe limitations. For example, society/environment 
and technology would be uneconomical, the market and technology would be low acceptance 
by the end-users, and the market and society would be old-fashioned. Therefore, to attain an 
enabled and advanced innovation, the three elements should be available. This should also be 
the basis for testing the energy living lab as no elements (especially women, as users) should 
be left behind during the development of the energy technology innovation. 
1.4 Structuring and adapting the Living Lab leading to SETILL model 
The Living Labs and the gender studies have been discussed in Chapter 2 of this study. The 
Living Labs are seen as methodologies, real-world scenarios and landscapes where various 
approaches, techniques and concepts are used by several stakeholders for a common goal 
(Hossain, Leminen and Westerlund, 2019). However, the successful development of Living 
Labs and their results are mostly affected by several challenges like the maintenance of the 
innovation, and the engagement of the end-users and stakeholders. There are several Living 
Labs in the world with a lens of systems thinking in them. Such as the Sustainable Urban 
Neighbourhood (SUN) research projects which took place in Europe, the Living Lab for Smart 
Mobility situated in Eindhoven Netherlands, the Systems Living Lab situated in Oulu Finland 
and at the University of Trento in Italy.  However, at the moment it is not confirmed if these 
Living Labs still exist, but the point is that they used the systems thinking lens to develop and 
test products in collaboration with different stakeholders, but, with no gender lens. 
Furthermore, in South Africa, although there are several Innovation hubs in the form of the 
Living Lab focused on energy technology innovation, most of them lacked the focus on gender 
and systems thinking perspectives. Generally, these innovation hubs may not focus on gender 
design in energy technology innovation in South Africa, but they work to improve gender 
equality in other sectors. There are also research studies that have used the soft systems 
methodology lens towards a Living Lab methodology for smart cities, innovation management, 
concept designs, transitioning smart campus to cities, to develop a community Living Lab, 
framework, forming future IT to show the Living Lab processes for the involvement of users, 
for user-centric co-creation, and for the evaluation of Living Labs to innovation (Mirijamdotter 
et al., 2006; Kong and Li, 2007; Ståhlbröst, 2008; Bergvall-Kåreborn, Holst and Ståhlbröst, 
2009; van der Walt et al., 2009; Cosgrave, Arbuthnot and Tryfonas, 2013; van Geenhuizen, 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 125 
 
2018; Omotayo et al., 2021). However, these studies did not involve the gender perspective. 
The closest study was the study by Mukama et al (2022), which involved the gendered 
perspective on energy technology innovation. However, this study did not include systems 
thinking approaches. Therefore, this research study will involve the systems thinking approach 
and gendered design of the Living Lab to be developed towards the development of the energy 
technology innovation in the case study. 
The selected case study of the SETILL will show how the Living Lab adapted to energy 
technology innovation using a gender design. The SETILL will carry out transdisciplinary 
energy technology research which will involve various stakeholders; however, the major focus 
will be on the needs of the women in the community which will also impact the sustainable 
development of the community. The SETILL model will also be focused on reducing energy 
poverty in the households of the informal settlement. The SETILL will also help the researchers 
and the government learn more about the processes and how to further intervene to achieve the 
security of energy services. Furthermore, the main focus of the SETILL model is the 
involvement of women as end users and the informal settlement to drive their opinions towards 
the development of the energy technology innovation to be developed and tested, which could 
be later used by the government to improve energy services. 
Therefore, considering the Living Lab concepts, the conceptualization and understanding of 
the Living Labs, the Living Lab can be defined as “a research and innovation concept for 
experimental and experiential learning in real-life environment, involving users and multiple 
private and public stakeholders, and aimed at (1) co-designing, prototyping, testing and 
observing new solutions and novel organisational structure in an iterative fashion, and (2) 
stimulating changes in the socio-technical regime to create the most favourable conditions for 
scaling-up innovations (Mukama et al., 2022:4). Therefore, considering the definition of the 
Living Lab above and the scope of this research study, the SETILL will now be defined in this 
research study as the ‘physical or virtual and mobile research and systems thinking approach 
space for design methodology focused on co-creating and testing of energy innovations through 
the involvement of end-users in a real-life scenario towards mitigating energy poverty for the 
informal settlements by collaborating with various stakeholders to (1) understand the material 
cultural, cognitive norms and energy practices or technological mechanisms influencing the 
level of adoption of energy innovations, (2) to investigate the influence of end-users 
contributions on energy technology innovation in a systems Living Lab, (3) develop and test 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 126 
 
an energy technology innovation up to a pilot scale through the involvement of end-users, (4) 
promote energy security, gender equality and women empowerment’.   
Therefore, the development of the emerging SETILL will be supported by the concept and 
structure of the prospective Living Lab towards the understanding of the end-user condition 
and innovation solutions to withstand those in the informal settlement. Below is the structure 
of the SETILL relating to the research study. The structure of the Living Lab also shows a 
process that will be used to structure the directives or processes for the SETILL model. 
Table 3.1: Example of the structure of the SETILL model 
SETILL 
Physical or virtual and mobile research and systems thinking 
approach space for design methodology focused on co-creating and 
testing energy innovations through the involvement of end-users in a 
real-life scenario towards mitigating energy poverty for the informal 
settlements by collaborating with various stakeholders. 
Research Questions 
1. What is the conceptualization of the Systems Living Lab concept, 
gender inequality, energy security, energy technology innovations, 
informal settlements and how do human factors affect technologies 
in literature? 
2. What is(are) the mechanism(s) influencing the contribution of 
knowledge and participation of women in the decision-making 
process of co-designing, co-creating, co-researching, prototyping and 
testing the energy technology innovations in the informal settlement 
to meet the expectations of end-users? 
3. How can the mechanism(s) be improved to enhance the level of 
adoption or acceptance of energy innovations and end-users (women) 
contributions and participation to address the security of energy 
services in poor informal settlements? 
4. Will the proposed Systems Living Lab focused on gender design 
serve as a suitable theoretical framework and concept to support 
energy technology innovations, improve the adoption of gender, 
reduce energy poverty, and improve access to energy services in poor 
informal settlements and the energy sector?

technology innovation and decision-making processes is 
indispensable.

1.5 Stakeholders and their roles as applied to the SETILL model 
Stakeholders in the Living Lab are very important to achieving the aim of this research study. 
The importance of the stakeholders cannot be underestimated. This is because, the 
collaboration of the stakeholders in the Living Lab will go a long way to improve the 
acceptance and adoption of innovations and increase knowledge and transparency to the 
development of the technology innovations (Breukers and Wolsink, 2007; Reed, 2008; 
Johnson, Jansujwicz and Zydlewski, 2015; Mastelic et al., 2018; Gabriel et al., 2020). 
Therefore, the stakeholders in this context can be defined as the public group of people and 
institutions that are involved in a process of development or otherwise (Reed, 2008). To build 
trust for the success of innovations and promote acceptance, it is necessary to work with various 
stakeholders. Also, people should not be left behind when making decisions on the design 
phases and processes of innovations to avoid a lack of adoption and acceptance of the 
innovation (Dachtera, Randall and Wulf, 2014), thus the involvement of various stakeholders 
from the design phases to the testing phases is very important for a successful technology 
innovation development (Mastelic et al., 2018).  
Moreover, the concept of the Living Lab technology is enhanced by the participations of many 
stakeholders and especially from the different field of discipline. These may include the people 
in the government sectors, academic sectors, communities and in the private and public sectors 
of other fields with same vision towards technology success for the Living Lab as mentioned 
in Ahmadi et al., 2020. A study on gender equality with energy studies indicated the importance 
of the collaboration of various stakeholders to be a gain or a driver in the success of gender 
equality (Oosthuizen, de Kock and Musango, 2020). The huge challenge of collaborating with 
stakeholders in this context is usually maintaining different perspectives of the stakeholders in 
a practical study (Logghe, Baccarne and Schuurman, 2014; Ley et al., 2015) because 
individuals with their interests might clash with the research study because of different goals 
relating to their interests (Dachtera, Randall and Wulf, 2014). Therefore, to overcome these 
challenges, the identification of trustworthy groups or individuals, who are ready to work and 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 129 
 
share the environment is necessary (Dupont et al., 2019; Ahmadi et al., 2020) and the 
management of time, motivating the individuals and accommodating their interest is necessary 
to avoid them from disconnecting (Mukama et al., 2022). Brouwer et al. (2013) advised that 
the stakeholders should lead together or have equal power to avoid one of the stakeholders’ 
overpowering others in the environment. However, there should be a facilitator who should 
coordinate the environment with the teams of stakeholders for a balance in the process which 
allows for more focus (Mukama et al., 2022). 
Furthermore, new stakeholders can be brought in during the process of the Living Lab to allow 
for more opinions and greater experience (Ahmadi et al., 2020). This would help to constantly 
add or subtract, and bring versatility to the roles of various stakeholders in the Living Lab 
(Ståhlbröst and Holst, 2017). Therefore, this research study collaborated with several 
stakeholders towards a successful development of the energy technology innovation where 
women (end-users) were allowed to contribute to how the energy innovation should be 
developed to benefit them and the community. The Living Lab collaboration has been ongoing 
with various stakeholders from the start of this research study, and this allows for the addition 
of experience methodological bonding between the various stakeholders involved (Ahmadi et 
al., 2020). This continued until the end of this research project for the successful adoption of 
the innovation.  
The management of the Living Lab technology is easy when the stakeholders are categorised 
accordingly, to enable successful stakeholders’ opinions into the processes (Nyström et al., 
2014; Johnson, Jansujwicz and Zydlewski, 2015; Ståhlbröst, Bergvall-Kåreborn and Eriksson, 
2015). The connection between the stakeholders and the Living Labs is usually focused more 
on the stakeholders’ control and not on the Living Lab space (Ståhlbröst, Bergvall-Kåreborn 
and Eriksson, 2015). This is because the stakeholders drive the Living Lab and also dominate 
the Living Lab. This also means that the successful continuation or longevity of the Living Lab 
depends on the various stakeholders who agree to work together to achieve a common goal 
(Mukama et al., 2022). Therefore, the SETILL will comprise the collaborations of various 
stakeholders who are willing to achieve the desired objectives of this research study. These 
stakeholders will be made up of the researchers, the institutions, the informal settlements, the 
women in the informal settlements, funders, governments, private companies or developers 
and the community supporters or civil society, see figure 3.3 below: 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 130 
 
Figure 3.3: A road map of the stakeholders in systems energy technology innovation Living 
Lab (SETILL) with a gendered perspective. 
The stakeholders identified in Figure 3.3 are to collaborate to bring about the security of energy 
services in the informal settlement. They are strongly influencing the Living Lab bi-
directionally, except for the energy innovation which is strongly influenced by the stakeholders 
as the possible outcome from the SETILL. The stakeholders can be adjusted at any time 
because the Living Lab is an open space/environment that allows for more or additional 
collaboration (Mukama et al., 2022), for example, the community supporters or civil society 
could be adjusted or removed if not needed. Also, the technology developers' stakeholders can 
also be known as the ‘energy private companies’ or ‘energy agencies’ around the case study 
and can also be seen as ‘vendors’ as they would be doing the supplies of the products needed. 
If necessary, more stakeholders could be added or removed as the research goes on. 
The Living Lab in the energy sector can be technical (Dupont et al., 2019), due to the energy 
systems. This can also discourage some stakeholders who are not in the technical field from 
fully participating (Mukama et al., 2022). This was the main reason the Living Lab involved 
researchers from different fields, relating to the focus of the research study and a workshop 
was done to properly present the focus of the research, the systems processes of the Living Lab 
and the innovation to be developed to carry all the stakeholders along. Therefore, the 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 131 
 
stakeholders informing the SETILL are explained below and illustrated briefly in a rich picture 
in Figure 3.4 for further understanding.  
1.5.1 
The informal settlement/community stakeholders 
The research study collaborated with Mater Pluviam Holdings, a science technology company 
to partner with the Tzaneen, Limpopo of South Africa, as the selected case study to test the 
gendered energy technology innovation and the Umuahia, Abia State of Nigeria informal 
settlements. Mater Pluviam Holdings PTY LTD is trusted around the community and beyond 
and allows for easy access to the community due to the scientific innovations it has brought to 
the community in the past. The Mather Pluviam Holding made it possible for the community 
supporters/civil society who could serve as co-researchers to be identified and educated on the 
process involved in carrying out their duties. Furthermore, a non-random sample of the 
informal settlement women in households in the community was identified. These women in 
the household were a representation of the variety of women in the households in the informal 
settlements. They also helped to identify the schools in the community where the innovation to 
be developed will be tested for clean cooking to feed the school children in the case study. A 
semi-structured interview and surveys were conducted by the researchers and co-researchers 
in the informal settlements to identify the material cultures, cognitive norms and energy 
practices in the community in ten areas of the communities in August and September of 2022 
which ethnographic research was also done in 30% of the households in July 2023 to discuss 
the challenges of the energy systems.  These were done in collaboration with other stakeholders 
in the SETILL.  
This survey was done to have an insight into how the households in the community are 
influenced by modern energy needs, level of energy demand, availability of energy demand 
and general energy uses and culture, using the available energy sources in the informal 
settlements. Therefore, this research study will then require the ideas and opinions of the 
informal settlements and end-users (women) and other stakeholders to carry out the design 
processes of the energy technology innovation to be developed towards a reduction in energy 
poverty and bringing about the security of energy services in the informal settlements. This 
process is referred to as open social innovation (Yun et al., 2017). These informal settlements 
have the dynamic ability to bring success to this research study because they are legitimately 
in need of solutions to energy poverty and gender inequality. This does not imply that other 
community members or community supporters who do not have the power to impact the 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 132 
 
research cannot get involved, as they can be classified into other types of stakeholders since 
they partially also require security to energy services (Mukama et al., 2022). 
The informal settlements or community stakeholders can also be seen as the energy consumers 
or end-users because they are part of the families of the target end-users (women). Therefore, 
they will directly benefit from the sustainable outcomes of this research study. They are also 
involved in the monitoring and evaluation of the energy technology innovation project and how 
it would affect the informal settlements, they contribute to the projects and demand efficient 
and sustainable products, assist in reducing any conflicts by making the environment safe, and 
they also have the ability to accomplish the decisions of the stakeholders (Ceriano, Lalk and 
Thopil, 2023). 
1.5.2 
The government/policy maker stakeholders 
The government is one of the stakeholders in the Living Lab and its connection with the 
community is very important because of its authority and power to decide the continuation of 
the energy innovation, if not carried along properly (Johnson, Jansujwicz and Zydlewski, 
2015). The government will assist to educate others in the Living Lab, about the extent to the 
sustainability of the innovation, other laws and corrections to the policies where necessary. The 
duties of the government in the Living Lab might be to bring up laws that will assist in the 
advancement and adoption of energy innovation and security to the energy services from the 
Living Lab (Almirall, Lee and Wareham, 2012).  
The government (central or local governments) will also assist with the formulation and 
enactment of policies and legislations for the energy industry, advance the sustainable and 
effective energy use, supervise closely the energy project for the benefit of the community, 
demand more participations in the design and management of the energy technology (Ceriano, 
Lalk and Thopil, 2023), and create further expansion of the energy technology innovation to 
other informal settlements. Lastly, the social system guiding the innovation laws to the social 
challenges can be strongly supported by the government to make decisions on the challenges 
faced by the communities. They can also influence the funding of the activities in the Living 
Lab, the Living Lab itself and the maintenance of energy technology. 
1.5.3 
The technology developers/energy industry partner stakeholders 
The importance of the developers or energy industry partners in this context is never in doubt. 
This is because they provide the technical support needed and assist the government with the 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 133 
 
implementation of the policies and laws (Ceriano, Lalk and Thopil, 2023). The developers who 
can also be called the project managers can be from the energy, science, and technology sectors. 
They have a huge duty to carry out in the Living Lab. They are the branch of the stakeholders 
who would assist with the development and design of the energy technology innovation to 
benefit the end-users more (Almirall, Lee and Wareham, 2012). This is so because this research 
study has proposed that the SETI Living Lab has not accomplished its aim if there is no energy 
innovation designed from it with gendered perspectives, using the systems thinking approach. 
This is part of the novelty of this research. The partners or developers who come from the 
public or private sectors around science, engineering or energy fields can assist with advertising 
the technology innovation, help to test the innovation in the community (Mukama et al., 2022), 
and can also help to generate funds for the design of the energy technology innovation. 
1.5.4 
The researcher/academia stakeholders 
The researchers are the facilitators of the activities in the Living Lab. They will manage and 
direct the activities, invent new knowledge to assist the other stakeholders and apply for 
funding for the progress of the activities to be carried out in the Living Lab, and they will also 
avoid research bias, and be true to the other stakeholders to build trust (Mukama et al., 2022). 
The researchers should also endeavour to keep to the time frame to complete the research 
objectives. The researchers are to be resilient (Ahmadi et al., 2020), and contemplative 
(Ståhlbröst and Holst, 2017) while carrying out their duties in the Living Lab. According to 
Ceriano, Lalk and Thopil (2023:7), the technical researchers “develop energy solutions that 
address country’s specific energy needs and sustainably exploit the usage of available 
resources; keep abreast of research developments regionally and internationally; adopt better 
practices customised for the local scenarios while utilising local resources; training and skilling 
of energy workforce; act as information sources and decision makers’ consultants; are 
compelled to observe national and local rules and norms”. 
The researchers or academia’s in collaboration with the developers are to also provide a 
suitable venue and required tools needed for the activities of the stakeholders and research 
(Mukama et al., 2022). The main role of the researcher is to use empirical knowledge to give 
insights into the technicalities and sciences behind the development of energy technology 
innovation. They also do not possess the dynamic ability to force the design of innovations on 
the communities (Mukama et al., 2022). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 134 
 
1.5.5 
The funders/institution stakeholders 
The research activities and the design of the energy technology innovation require financial 
resources. The funders or institutions are the group of stakeholders who can provide this 
financial capability to achieve the desired goals of the Living Lab and the study. The Graduate 
School of Technology Management and the University of Pretoria fall into this category of 
stakeholders in this research context as they have partially funded this research study. The 
funding would be needed for training purposes, workshops, data collection and for the 
community supporters as they carry out labour activities (Mukama et al., 2022), and in this 
context, the activities that would lead to the design and testing of energy technology innovation 
to achieve the research objectives.  
Therefore, it is believed that the stakeholders involved in this research study and embedded in 
the Living Lab can help to improve energy security or reduce energy poverty in the informal 
settlement if they all work together with full commitment and dedication. The improvement in 
the Lephepane village of Tzaneen informal settlement can also be used as a model or 
framework for other likely informal settlements in Africa and globally. 
1.5.6 
The end-user stakeholders 
As discussed initially, for this research study, women are the target end-users. The energy 
technology to be developed is mostly used by women in the Tzaneen and Umuahia informal 
settlements and other likely informal settlements. Although almost everyone (including men) 
with access to the energy system are users. This research study has a focus on women because 
they happen to be affected more by the disadvantages of restricted access to the modern form 
of energy and from contributing to the designs of the energy technology innovations (Cecelski, 
2012). The focus on the vulnerability of women in poor informal settlements have been used 
by Kovacic et al., (2019); Musango et al., (2020); Mukama et al., (2022). These studies, which 
are related to health, energy, Living Labs and gender respectively, also result in the view that 
there is a link between women vulnerable to poverty and the “symmetric” dissemination of 
household responsibilities. 
The women can also be in the same category as the community stakeholders, because they 
share similar responsibilities as the community stakeholders and are all beneficiaries of the 
energy technology innovation. However, women are categorised as separate stakeholders in 
the context of the SETILL because of their importance in achieving the research objectives. A 
non-random probability sample of women representing various women was used to identify 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 135 
 
the main target women for the research study. The women have the authority and power to 
progress the study and are vital in the testing of the energy technology innovation to be 
designed because they are the most in need of the interventions (Mukama et al., 2022). Their 
decisions and opinions can be a driver to the reduction of energy poverty in the informal 
settlements. This is because the unavailability of access to energy resources is affecting 
women's empowerment and gender equality (Pascale et al., 2016). 
Furthermore, women suffer more from household air pollution from cooking and from the use 
of biomass and paraffin in their households (Kovacic et al., 2019). They are also responsible 
for fetching firewood for cooking which consumes time (Choumert-Nkolo, Combes Motel and 
Le Roux, 2019), thereby hindering them from investing enough time in education and their 
careers (Winther et al., 2017). They are also meant to handle energy equipment that could harm 
them due to a lack of electrical safety in the households of the informal settlement (Mudombi 
et al., 2018; Seguin, Flax and Jagger, 2019). Women suffer greatly in the informal settlements 
and deserve justice and also to be seen as a very important economic factor. Finally, Figure 3.4 
below shows the rich picture formulation of the SETILL, the stakeholders in their sub-systems 
and how they inform the systems in the Living Lab leading to the SETILL theoretical model. 
According to Ceriano, Lalk and Thopil (2023:6) “a rich picture is a graphic illustration that 
represents all the stakeholders, their inter-connections and concerns, and it is aimed to be a 
wide, highly-grained outlook of the problem situation”.  
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 136 
 
Figure 3.4: Rich Picture formulation for the systems energy technology innovation Living Lab 
(SETILL) with a gendered perspective showing their systems and subsystems, leading to the 
SETILL theoretical model. 
The origin of rich pictures is from integrated understanding of categorised activities (Bell, Berg 
and Morse, 2019), the construction of a rich picture should be done with the other stakeholders 
to achieve general outcomes and desired concerns (Ceriano, Lalk and Thopil, 2023). The 
identified stakeholders participating in this study were carefully selected as main collaborators 
with broad skills, ideas and technical knowledge in the gender, systems thinking and energy 
sectors. Therefore, this does not imply that more stakeholders cannot successfully participate 
to improve the study, but the stakeholders selected were done due to the time frame of the 
research study and also to focus more on the stakeholders to bring the desired outcomes in 
time. Furthermore, we cannot fully prevent the occurrence of components of some abstract 
effects on the outcomes (Ceriano, Lalk and Thopil, 2023). 
 
1.6 Conceptualising the SETILL theoretical model 
The SETI Living Lab model as conceptualised will be implemented as part of any hub of 
energy technology innovation with gender design and systems thinking approach in poor 
informal settlements. The SETILL model could be a virtual place if there is no space due to 
social distancing. This means that it could be done virtually by bringing together those involved 
online, and it can also be mobile, which means that it can be moved to any convenient space 
regardless of where the energy innovation is to be developed. Also, the SETILL model is not 
complete if there is no energy technology innovation developed to benefit a particular end-
users chosen by the researcher in collaboration with other stakeholders. 
Therefore, considering the project of the proposed SETILL context, the Living Lab is defined 
as physical or virtual and mobile research and systems thinking approach space for design 
methodology focused on co-creating and testing of energy innovations through the involvement 
of end-users in a real-life scenario towards mitigating energy poverty for the informal 
settlements by collaborating with various stakeholders to (1) understand the material cultures, 
cognitive norms and energy practices or mechanisms influencing the level of adoption of 
energy innovations, (2) to investigate the influence of end-users contributions on energy 
technology innovation in a systems Living Lab, (3) develop and test an energy technology 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 137 
 
innovation up to a pilot scale through the involvement of end-users, (4) promote energy 
security, gender equality and women empowerment. 
It is envisaged that the SETILL theoretical model will look like the Living Lab that focuses on 
the involvement of users, with a focus on women in the decision-making processes and 
knowledge contribution of the innovative design. The users would be involved in the co-
creation of the energy services solutions, co-design of energy technology innovations, 
prototyping, and testing. Although men are also part of the users, for the context of this research 
study, the focus is on women. A different study that wants to use the SETILL model can decide 
to choose a different user if possible. 
The processes of developing the SETILL model from a conceptualisation point of view can be 
illustrated by borrowing from the ICT, health, gender and education sectors. Although these 
have been developed in the ICT, health, gender and education sectors, they will become the 
basis for testing the energy technology innovation to be developed. This has given the idea of 
how the proposed SETILL model will be conceptualised. See Figure 3.5 below, figure 3.3 and 
Table 3.1 above for the proposed emerging SETILL model and the structure of the SETILL 
model. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 138 
 
 
Figure 3.5: Conceptualising the emerging SETILL theoretical model 
The essence of this section is to conceptualise the ideas of SETILL model. For the Living Lab 
to function, it will depend on the innovation process applications which will be linked to the 
‘dream,’ process, ‘design’ process, ‘develop’ process, ‘apply’ process, ‘implement’ process, 
and the ‘review’ process (Callaghan and Herselman, 2015) discussed in the literature review 
chapter. The structure of the Living Lab also shows a process that could be used to structure 
the directives or processes for the SETILL model development. The SRM model by Okoye 
(2020) was adapted into the model to understand the material cultures, cognitive norms and 
energy practices of the informal settlements before the development of the gendered energy 
technology innovation (outcome). This is the first system interconnections in figure 3.5 which 
includes the material cultures, cognitive norms and energy practices as the systems of energy 
poverty or ‘design decisions’ system. They assist with evaluating the level of energy poverty, 
energy use and energy behaviours in the households of the informal settlements. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 139 
 
The model to be developed to answer research questions 2 and 3, represents ‘what is(are) the 
mechanism(s) influencing the contribution of knowledge and participation of women in the 
decision-making process of co-designing, co-creating, co-researching, prototyping and testing 
of the energy technology innovations in the informal settlement to meet the expectations of 
end-users? and How can the mechanism(s) be improved to enhance the level of adoption or 
acceptance of energy innovations and end-users contributions and participation to achieve the 
security of energy services in the informal settlements? Finally, the road map of the SETI 
Living Lab is represented. All these three sections make up the SETILL theoretical model. 
Furthermore, the System Energy Technology Innovation Living Lab (SETILL) played a vital 
role in aiding the design and development of the gender-inclusive energy technology 
innovation (the Biogas digester). The Biogas digester served as a practical test case for 
SETILL, offering users insights into the gender dynamics of energy in informal settlements 
and fostering creative idea generation for gender-inclusive energy technology innovations. The 
evaluation of this co-designed gender-inclusive energy technology innovation took place 
during a one-day, multi-stakeholder co-design workshop held in Tzaneen, South Africa.  
1.7 Conclusions 
This chapter has contributed to the theoretical framework of the Living Lab concept in the 
energy industry and shows how the Living Lab concept is used leading to the context of the 
SETILL to achieve security of energy services. The SETILL can be mobile and the movement 
can always start from the GSTM of the University of Pretoria to collaborate with various 
stakeholders to ‘co-research’, ‘co-design’, ‘co-create’, ‘prototype’ and ‘test’ any form of 
energy technology innovation to a pilot scale and most importantly with gender perspectives 
and systems thinking approaches. Any kind of systems thinking or systems engineering can be 
used for the SETILL. 
The chapter has specifically or partially addressed objective 1 and 4 by developing a roadmap 
to the SETILL (figure 3.3). This chapter also conceptualised the Living Lab technology starting 
with building the conceptual framework, showing the antecedents, attributes and consequences 
relating to the research concept. Finally, other detailed information and literature reviews 
needed on the Living Lab concepts, SsT/SE, energy technology innovation and any of the 
sections in this chapter can be found in the preceding chapters of this thesis. The chapter also 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 140 
 
indicated the processes that will be used or adapted in the SETILL model. The next chapter 
will highlight the methodological designs used to achieve the research objectives of this study.

Page | 141 
 
Chapter 2 
Research Methodology and Process 
2.1 Introduction 
This chapter provides information on the research methodology, methodological procedures 
and strategies used in conducting this research study. Additionally, there is a consideration of 
the procedures of validity threat management and ethics of the study. The Living Lab concept 
happens to be a new field in the energy sector and would need suitable methods. 
Imperatively, the choice of the methods is not dependable on the research questions only, but 
on the state of the specific research and the conditions to obtain the data needed (Maxwell, 
1996). This implies that making the choices for the methodology is often not based on the best 
form of practices used in the case but depends basically on the familiarities and habits in the 
field. Therefore, the decision of the methodologies does not rule out the fact that they can be 
mixed to answer the research questions. This can also be referred to as the multiple research 
approach (Miles and Huberman, 1994; Yin, 2009). Therefore, the methodology can go from 
the empirical to a non-empirical and desktop studies approach which will include model-
building, and with an applied qualitative grounded theory approach (creation of a theory that 
is grounded in the field of study), quantitative approach, Ethnographic approach (which 
explains the social and cultural connection between a group of individuals) (Nicholls, D., 2009) 
and the (DRM) - Design Research Methodology framework (which is a design support 
framework used in this research study for the development of the energy technology design) 
(Blessing & Chakrabarti, 2009).   
The techniques for data gathering involved primary and secondary data collection which will 
include the perusal (library search) – data mining, observations (field research) - case study 
(using the Living Lab network to the poor informal settlements as case studies), questioning 
(field research) – interviews, surveys and questionnaires, the literature review, focus groups, 
document reviews, and workshop. The data analysis involved qualitative analysis, comparative 
analysis, stakeholder mapping, system dynamics, content analysis, narrative analysis, thematic 
analysis, coding, and memos. Some of the core methods to be used will be explained in this 
chapter relating to the research questions. 
Furthermore, the research will follow a maximum variation sampling approach which can 
allow for the comparison and contrasting of different cases for theory-building (Eisenhardt, 
1989; Kathleen and Melissa, 2007). The sampling technique will be a non-random probability 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 142 
 
sampling. Also, data such as secondary data and census data relating to the survey of the 
household and others will be generated from the municipalities or the local context to 
understand energy behaviours in the case study. The components of the research study would 
involve the following: 
❖ Theory building research – 40% 
❖ Theory testing research – 20% 
❖ Theory application research – 40% 
Based on this, a mixed-method research design will be satisfactory for this study, because 
findings cannot be accurately preconceived, and it will provide an efficient inquiry framework 
to solve the problems. However, there is caution against methodology selections before 
answering more basic questions that are connected to the paradigm (Guba & Lincoln, 1998). 
This research was carried out in line with the University of Pretoria’s policy on Research Ethics 
(see appendix -). Therefore, to contribute to the suitability of the methodologies selected for 
answering the research questions; and the philosophical foundations for this research study; the 
DRM framework and the grounded theory methodology relevant to the philosophical 
foundations, the issues of ethics in the research and trustworthiness will be discussed in this 
chapter. 
 
2.2 The philosophical basis for this research study 
This section aims to give insights into the main philosophical basis that is important to the 
management of the research design. Furthermore, to adequately address the research questions 
it is critical to understand the philosophical basis of the research study. 
Easterby-Smith, Thorpe & Jackson (2012:56), indicated the three reasons in management 
research why the philosophical basis of the research is important, which are that; “it can assist 
to clarify research design by providing adequate answers to the principal questions being 
investigated in the research, it can assist the researcher to identify which designs might work 
and which might not, and it can assist the researcher to recognise and create better designs that 
might be out of his/her experience.” 
The philosophical basis for this research study can be seen through the argument of the 
ontology and epistemology basis (Ponterotto, 2005; Eriksson & Kovalainen, 2011). While 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 143 
 
ontology gives insight into the nature of existence and reality, epistemology gives insight into 
the right processes of exploring the world’s nature. This philosophical basis was considered in 
this research study to achieve the desired research methodology and techniques to answer the 
research questions. This is because as cited by Onyeagoziri (2020), the outcome of the 
philosophical basis is important to the various methods used in conducting the research and to 
conclude on the phenomenon being studied. The philosophical assumptions should be 
understood to improve the research quality and the researcher’s creativity (Easterby-Smith, 
Thorpe & Jackson, 2012). 
Figure 2.1 below explains the relationship between the ontology, epistemology, methodology 
and the methods and techniques; 
Figure 4.1: The relationships for understanding the ontology, epistemology, methodology and 
techniques. 
Figure 4.1 represents the hierarchy between the ontology, epistemology, methodology and 
methods and techniques that were applied to this study, and table 4.1 below shows their 
importance. The initial ring describes the clear elements of the methods and techniques 
obtained in the study (in-depth-interviews), which are decisive to the development of the 
perpetual, invisible methodology, epistemology and ontology assumptions. 
Table 4.1: The substance of the connection between ontology, epistemology, methodology and 
the methods and techniques 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 144 
 
(Easterby-Smith et al. 2012:60). 
Therefore, relating to this research study, the philosophical basis of epistemology, paradigm, 
methodology and methods and techniques adapted to address the research objectives are 
explained below. The ontology philosophical area (Zlatanova, 2007; Gray, 2014) is defined as 
a comprehensive precise specification of combined knowledge (Gruber, 1995), which led to 
the epistemological basis to focus on the study as a structure which gives an insight into social 
reality was not discussed further in this research study as the focus of the research is fully on 
the epistemological philosophical stance. For little understanding of the ontological 
assumptions in the social world, the ontology is distinguished by a ‘nominalist view’ (Cohen, 
Manson & Morrison, 2000), which indicates that “objects of thoughts are merely words or that 
social reality is a product of individual consciousness and the ‘realist view’ that objects have 
an existence independent of a knower” (Manson & Morrison, 2000 in Shaw, 2013:42). 
2.2.1 
The epistemological basis of the research study 
Epistemology stance is about various processes of exploring the nature of the social and real 
world. Epistemology has shaped a clear two incompatible views on how this study should be 
done, which are ‘positivism and social constructionism’ (Easterby-Smith et al. 2012). These 
two incompatible points of view are discussed in section 4.2.2 and represented in Table 4.2 
below. 
For this research study, it is required to understand the paradigms, their outcomes, and their 
aspects. The ontology which was mentioned previously was detached from the epistemology 
during this study (Fairclough, 2005), due to the argument that the epistemology obscures the 
awareness of the nature of reality, which is termed the ‘epistemic fallacy’ (Fairclough, 2005 & 
Bhaskar, 1978). Also, Shaw (2013:47) indicated that “a constructivist epistemological position 
is not incommensurate with critical realism.” 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 145 
 
Furthermore, epistemology gives an insight into how one obtains knowledge, and that a ‘critical 
realist’ can be seen as a ‘non-positivist’. The goal of epistemology is the relationship between 
the participants and researchers of the study (Ponterotto, 2005). The above concepts further 
strengthen the methodological section of this study. 
Sayer (2000, in Bhaskar, 1986:72) indicated that “…critical realism accepts ‘epistemic 
relativism’, which gives the perspective that the world can only be known in accessible 
explanations or discourses, but it refuses ‘judgmental relativism’, which gives the perspective 
that one cannot judge between various discourses and decide that some accounts are better than 
others”. To substantiate the above, it was also agreed and explained by Al-Amoudi and 
Willmott (2011:42) “…that epistemological relativism is a philosophical theory of knowledge 
that moderates our knowledge, while epistemic relativism explains that the existing knowledge 
or discourse does the moderating.” This is important to this research, although, in this section, 
the focus is not on critical realism. 
Therefore, research studies on engineering or management since the year 1980 have given a 
state-of-the-art switch from ‘positivism’ to the ‘constructionism’ standard, and some studies 
intentionally mix research methods from both to conduct research (Easterby-Smith et al. 2012); 
however, this study has focused more on the ‘constructionist’ stance to conduct this research 
study. Therefore, the chosen stance of epistemology for this study was done because of its 
relationship with the methods adopted for this study. Below are the conflicting inferences of 
‘positivism’ and ‘constructionism’. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 146 
 
Table 4.2: The incompatible “implications of positivism and social constructionism”
Source: Easterby-Smith et al (2012:59). 
2.2.2 
The research study paradigms 
The phrase paradigm was used by Kuhn (1962) in his study to interpret the development of 
scientific discoveries in observations, instead of how they are ultimately metamorphosed in 
scientific journals and academic books. Maxwell (2005) while referring to the historian 
Thomas Kuhn indicated that the use of a research paradigm is paramount and hypercritical. 
Therefore, the chosen research techniques, research samples and methodologies can be 
determined by deciding the types of paradigms used, which permits the anthologies to see the 
study the way the academicians view it (Lincoln & Denzin, 2000). 
Furthermore, researchers out of various paradigms, tend to analyse and select a precise 
paradigm depending on the research study being carried out (Ponterotto, 2005). However, as 
indicated by Lincoln and Guba (1985), there are four typical paradigms which are categorised 
as being effective, and these paradigms are ‘positivism’, ‘post-positivism’, ‘constructivism-
interpretivism’ and ‘critical ideological theory’. These four paradigms will be briefly explained 
below. 
It is suggested that methods are connected to the paradigms; thus, this study might adopt the 
paradigm crossing to attain the method and answer the research questions, because an improved 
stance may arise by the use of the paradigm crossing (Yearworth and White, 2013). This 
permits the researcher to investigate the links between the subjective approach and hard 
approach (quantitative) regarding the constructivism-interpretivism frame (White, 2006). What 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 147 
 
this means is that the research may involve a mixed methodology that might require a paradigm 
crossing. The four paradigms are discussed briefly below: 
i. Positivism: The aim of this paradigm is essentially to predict and manage the classified 
phenomena being studied (Ponterotto, 2005). This paradigm is determined by the ‘hypothetico-
deductive’ method which is related to the quantitative research method in which the hypothesis 
is analysed (Guba and Lincoln, 1994; McGrath & Johnson, 2003). Furthermore, Easterby-
Smith et al. (2012:57) indicated that “the main concept of positivism shows the existence of 
the social world and indicates the measurement of its properties through objective methods, 
instead of being theorised subjectively through reflection or institution and sensation.” 
Therefore, this paradigm can be adopted for this research study based on its components and 
functionality, and if there is need for paradigm crossing. 
ii. Post-positivism: This paradigm is moderately adaptable to the grounded theory 
methodology (Baskerville & Pries-Heje, 1999). The post-positivism paradigm indicates that 
truths are certainly not fully acknowledged, however, it can be accessed through a research 
study (Lincoln & Guba, 1985), or that the objective phenomenon is certainly not fully able to 
capture the entire reality (Lincoln & Guba, 2000). Additionally, the post-positivism paradigm 
is focused on the validation and exploration of theories. The positivism and post-positivism 
paradigms are interested in the explanation of a phenomenon which are often attributed to the 
quantitative research methodology (Lincoln and Guba., 1985). 
iv. Critical-ideological theory: This research paradigm refers to a social science system for 
the investigation of the indicated theories to address the current problems and research gaps 
(Alderson, 2016). The critical ideology paradigm influences the understanding of the world to 
allow for the development of forces to innovate and emancipate control (Ponterotto, 2005) in 
social settings. This has various significance in the management and engineering research 
study. Easterby-Smith et al (2012:74), explained that “critical theory creates a questioning eye 
on the reasons and effect of powerful individuals and groups which in an emancipatory process 
gives a concern for the notice of the least powerful individuals.” This paradigm was not 
considered for this research study. 
iii. Constructivism-interpretivism: This research paradigm accepts various and identical 
rational realities (Schwandt, 1994) which gives an insight into the theory of acceptance of the 
world by the experiences of human factors which is published through the use of languages. 
This research paradigm allows for the understanding of the phenomena by the researchers 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 148 
 
through connections with the researchers and the phenomenon being studied or examined 
(Ponterotto, 2005). This paradigm is often used in qualitative research methods, which is 
positioned to the ‘subjectivist’ of this research study. This paradigm is satisfactorily chosen for 
this research study because it is workable and acceptable for the generation of theories. The 
rationale for choosing the constructivism-interpretivism paradigm for this study will be 
explained in section 4.2.3 below. 
2.2.3 
Relevance of the constructionist research design for this study 
The primary objective of this research study is, ‘to develop a Systems Living Lab that focuses 
on gender designs for the development of energy technology innovation up to a pilot scale 
through the involvement of end-users and other stakeholders in real-life scenarios to achieve 
the security of energy services’. This indicates that this research study is not focused on 
bringing liberation and power to the individuals in the communities, which can be ascribed to 
the ‘critical theory’ paradigm, but focuses on the people’s interpretations and procedures to 
answer the research questions, which can be ascribed to the ‘constructivism-interpretivism’ 
paradigm. Therefore, in this research study, ‘constructionism’ is associated with the processes 
of building the theoretical model, while ‘interpretivism’ is associated with the evaluation of the 
processes of building the theoretical model. 
Furthermore, the early stages of the ‘constructionist research design’, show theories with the 
absence of clear truth; therefore, it is the responsibility of the researchers to verify how various 
claims for truth and reality are constantly designed into existence (Easterby-Smith et al. 2012). 
This is why there are different types of methodologies which can be used in conjunction with 
the constructionist paradigm than the other epistemologies (Easterby-Smith, et al. 2012). 
Therefore, this research study has considered the grounded theory, which is frequently 
associated with the constructionist designs as the method to be used to direct this research 
study. The constructionist design can also be seen as a design where the researchers recognize 
the world through the mental model framework (Alesandrini & Larson, 2002). Figure 4.2 
explains further the elements of the mental model framework for proper understanding. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 149 
 
 
Figure 4.2: The (the iceberg) mental model: “A tool for guiding systemic thinking” (Northwest 
Earth Institute, n.d:2). 
The mental model assists with retaining the structures to provide their objectives, and they are 
the ‘values’, ‘beliefs’ and ‘attitudes’ to make the structure exactly as it is (Kim, 1996). 
Furthermore, constructionism in this research can happen when the end-users, other 
stakeholders or individuals in the environment engage in creating real-world considerable 
objectives. This means that constructionism is connected to empirical knowledge derived from 
Piaget’s ‘epistemological constructivism’ theory (Duijnhoven & Neef, 2014). Some studies 
have attempted to explain constructionism as knowledge derived from developing methods, 
but Papert & Harel (1991) indicated that constructionism should have various elements of 
features in its evidence rather than being knowledge derived from methods. 
Also, the constructivism-interpretivism paradigm permits the researchers and participants of 
the research study to have adequate real communication and cooperation and lengthening the 
field study period which can be attributed to this research study (Lincoln & Guba, 1985). This 
was the reason the data collection procedures (interviews and observations) for the research 
study were preferred by the constructivist-interpretivist paradigm. In the gender and energy 
study, the paradigms to choose are being argued in philosophy, hence, there are no direct 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 150 
 
addresses to the paradigms to be selected, thus, selecting a paradigm can be connected to the 
subjective research beliefs and interests of the researchers and stakeholders (Christophe et al. 
2004). As stated by Christophe et al (2004:8), “an author found critical theory to provide a 
good framework for his research and another author found radical constructivism theory of 
knowledge as a useful approach for the questions that came up throughout his projects,” 
Therefore, the significance of the paradigms would not cancel each other. 
Therefore, the constructivism-interpretivism paradigm is useful and chosen as an appropriate 
paradigm for this study from the subjective beliefs and interests of the researchers and 
stakeholders in this research study (Christophe et al. 2004), and the above clarifications aims 
to characterise the evidence for choosing the constructivism-interpretivism paradigm as the 
epistemological stance or basis for this study. 
 
2.3 The research methodology stance 
The nature of this research problem is systemic; thus, this study requires a ‘systemic 
methodology’, to advance the problem-solving circumstances and assist with progressing the 
proficiency of the research participants. Several researchers when it comes to research designs 
have conducted research with the use of a logical combination of elements of various 
methodology research paradigms to address research problems or questions (Sharp, 1998; 
Mingers, 2000; Ackroyd, 2004). 
When referring to design methodologies, there is an adopted design research methodology used 
to collaborate with the methodologies and techniques used to conduct this research study. This 
design research methodology (DRM) (Blessing and Chakrabarti, 2009), was selected for this 
research study because the focus of this study was also on designing to a pilot scale an energy 
technology innovation with gender designs to advance energy security. The ‘relativist’ and 
‘subjective’ stance examined in this study gave the stance for adopting the DRM for this study. 
The DRM was selected for this research study because it gives the systematic investigation 
framework to tackle the research problems which has a link to the constructivist-interpretivist 
paradigm. The DRM has been adopted for several studies as a framework for the development 
of design tools in engineering education and for the development of a co-design toolkit for 
participant collaborations (Calderon, 2010; Petrulaityte et al., 2022). 
Therefore, according to Calderon (2010:1), “a design research methodology is an approach and 
a set of supporting methods and guidelines to be used as a framework for doing design 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 151 
 
research”. Thus, this study will be designing and testing an energy technology with gender 
designs, in collaboration with several stakeholders and has selected the DRM as the framework 
to support the various methods used, by assisting in the planning, prototyping and testing or 
implementation of the designed energy innovation. The DRM can assist in making the design 
processes of energy technology innovation and this research studies more efficient if used 
according to the principles (Calderon, 2010). 
Imperatively, the development of gender-inclusive biogas technology, a key innovation 
resulting from this research study, was accomplished through the application of Design 
Research Methodology. This approach was informed by insights gleaned from grounded theory 
techniques, literature reviews, semi-structured interviews, and ethnographic fieldwork 
conducted in two informal settlements. 
Furthermore, according to Blessing and Chakrabarti (2009) and Calderon (2010), the DRM 
framework comprises four stages, which are the ‘Research Clarification (RC)’, ‘Descriptive 
Study 1 (DS-1)’, ‘Prescriptive Study (PS)’ and ‘Descriptive Study II (DS-II). Thus, figure 4.3 
below shows the relationship between the stages and basic means and deliverables derived 
from the stages. The strong arrows showing in-between the stages or blocks represent the 
fundamental systems flow and the dotted or weak arrows represent various monotony. 
Therefore, the DRM representation below will generally illustrate the details involved during 
the design and development of the energy technology innovation and the SETILL theoretical 
model by showing the plans or application procedures down to the testing phase. This implies 
that the research methods and data collection were conducted following the Design Research 
Methodology (DRM) which happens to be a framework for design support development 
(Blessing and Chakrabarti, 2009; Petrulaityte et al., 2022). The research activities following 
the DRM are illustrated below and also represented in Figure 4.3 below. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 152 
 
 
Figure 4.3: DRM (Design Research Methodology) used to develop and assess the energy 
technology innovation with gender design. 
The DRM framework in Figure 4.3 above has given an insight into the planned deliverables 
for each stage. The details of the information used to obtain the deliverables are also presented 
in Figure 4.3 which has led to the design processes used in this study to develop the energy 
technology innovation. Thus; the stages are described below: 
2.3.1 
Research clarification (RC) 
This stage can also be referred to as the ‘literature analysis. This stage aims to understand and 
critically analyse the theory and previous research done in the area of the topic being studied 
and relating to the research problems. This stage also gives an insight into the different designs 
done in recent and previous research to support gendered energy technology innovations 
(Petrulaityte et al., 2022). The main search idea was on ‘systems thinking’, ‘systems Living 
Lab’, ‘Living Labs’, ‘gendered Living Labs’, ‘energy stakeholders and end-users, ‘energy 
Living Labs’, ‘poor informal settlements’, ‘energy security’, ‘women empowerment’, ‘energy 
and gender’, ‘equality and women’ ‘design and energy innovations’ etc. The search ideas were 
done and analysed from Google Scholar, Library Genesis, university databases and websites 
of existing Living Labs focused on the context of the study, gender and energy technology 
innovation designs, women empowerment and equality, and the systems thinking approaches 
mainly relating to the research questions of this study. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 153 
 
Furthermore, the search ideas were targeted mostly at the stakeholders involved in this study 
and in the design of the energy technology innovation and procedures on how to factor gender 
into this study (Petrulaityte et al., 2022). This stage also made it possible for us to understand 
the importance of facilitation and communication between the stakeholders and other research 
participants (Kankainen et al., 2012; Trischler et al., 2018; Greene et al., 2020). 
2.3.2 
Descriptive study 1 (DS-1) 
This stage can also be referred to as the ‘empirical data analysis’. The semi-structured 
interviews were with the informal settlements used as case study, the end-users, especially the 
women, the staff of the private companies and government officials. We also realised that the 
gender aspect in energy and Living Lab concepts is lacking in private companies, and the 
implementation of energy designs relating to gender is also limited (Ceschin et al., 2023). 
According to Raymond (1992:22), “The interview is a discussion between two people where 
one of the people tries to conduct the discussion to acquire information for a particular reason”. 
Various types of interviews could be used, which are structured interviews, unstructured 
interviews and semi-structured interviews (Gill, Stewart, Treasure and Chadwick, 2008). 
Therefore, the semi-structured process is an interview process with various substantial 
questions that characterise the sections to be considered while allowing the researchers the 
possibility to provide effective insights as feedback (Gill et al. 2008). The semi-structured 
interview process is a link between the unstructured interview process and the structured 
interview process; thus, this interview process is suitable and chosen for this research study. 
Furthermore, the ethnographic fieldwork also adopted for this study was conducted in 50 
households across informal settlements in Umuahia, Nigeria, and Tzaneen, South Africa. This 
was undertaken with the purpose of gaining a deep understanding of how residents in the 
SETILL case study areas handle the energy-related challenges identified through the literature 
review and semi-structured interviews (Petrulaityte et al., 2022). Also, the importance of the 
ethnographic study was to collect data on how human factors affect technology and also to 
identify the energy behaviours of the informal settlements. This ethnographic research 
approach was conducted by the researchers and co-researchers in the communities used as case 
studies and the researchers spent 10 hours a day per household to examine the end-users 
(women) energy cultures, cognitive norms and material cultures and also interview them on 
the types of energy sources used and their benefits, then obtain opinions and knowledge on 
how to mitigate energy poverty and design the energy technology innovation. The ethnographic 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 154 
 
approach used in this research study was instrumental in informing the design and development 
of the energy technology innovation in the case study and the involvement of the communities 
and civil societies improved the successful development of the SETILL. 
2.3.3 
Prescriptive study (PS) 
This stage can also be referred to as the ‘assumption experience synthesis’. This stage also 
involves the theory-building approach (Meredith, 1993) which can be used for the synthesis of 
the data gathered and combined to develop the energy technology innovation and identify the 
energy technology innovation testing processes (Petrulaityte et al., 2022). This implies that the 
various data collected will be used to develop different models or frameworks that will be 
combined in the SETILL to develop energy technology innovation. Therefore, this is a stage 
where the data collection from the RC and DS-1 were applied to develop the energy technology 
innovation (biogas technology) with gender design using systems thinking approaches. 
This stage and the basic means were influenced by the grounded theory methodology (GT), 
which can be defined as a research approach for the construction of theoretical interpretation 
of social phenomena, grounded in empirical observations by different data gathering conducted 
and uninterrupted comparative analysis and cycles from occurring subjects (Kennedy & 
Lingard, 2006). Furthermore, there is no hypothesis in grounded theory but there are open-
ended research questions only and a theory result that is extensively verified, and this can be 
referred to as an emergent grounded theory (Tavakol et al. 2006). The major constraint of the 
grounded theory is that the results may be exposed to ‘research bias because of the processes 
of the approaches involved (Charmaz, 2014), however, this constraint was controlled by efforts 
used to analyse research bias in the validity section below. 
Additionally, the grounded theory approach includes data gathering, coding, and data 
categorizations which can be used to develop a theory (Strauss & Corbin, 1990). A complete 
grounded theory approach includes some of the following steps; ‘formulation of questions’, 
‘theoretical sampling’, ‘transcribing’, ‘coding’, ‘development of conceptual category’, 
‘constant comparison’ and ‘analytical memo’. However, the researchers can conduct the study 
simultaneously at times without following a specific step. The figure 4.4 below explains 
further; 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 155 
 
Figure 4.4: The processes of the grounded theory for this research study. 
The figure above explains in detail the grounded theory process to be used in this study. This 
process includes; data gathering (interviews, literature, participant observation in the SETILL, 
fieldwork, documents and journals) and continues the data analysis and development of 
concepts. The features of the key elements of the grounded theory according to Charmaz (1990) 
used for this study are explained below according to the researchers: 
Collection and analysis of data: This key element is very important as it directs the 
researchers on the data and extra data to collect, and also to understand the questions and 
headings that are unravelled (Charmaz, 2015). This key element was conducted through 
participatory observations and interviews. Participatory observations in the SETILL were 
conducted over one year for once a month, as a procedure of data collection through extensive 
stakeholders’ involvement and display of routine tasks at the informal settlements. Community 
meetings were done with 25 community members (men, women, boys and girls) who were 
already aware of the challenges of lack of energy security. The interviews were done each for 
20-30 minutes. 
Systematically, non-probability or purposive sampling was adopted because it allows for the 
use of small sample sizes concerning ‘credibility’ and not ‘representativeness’ (Nastasi et al. 
2004). The sampling approach was used to identify the times, settings and participants to be 
interviewed. The ethical conflict during the information gathered was aimed at confidentiality, 
which means that all data obtained from the people were confidential. Sample size influences 
the research questions appropriately until there is no more development of new categories and 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 156 
 
analysis found in the data collected (Bonnie, 2016). The sample size used for this research 
study was predicted to be established by the methodology or data collection approach used in 
this study. The sample size rules for different research approaches are explained below: 
       Based-rules approach

                                     Rule

researchers are allowed to choose an extra case
