The size of the exceptional database was also considered, which would provide sufficient data 
of quantity and quality. This implies that the quantity of the data is also important as it 
influences the arrangement of the interview processes. For example, about 10 or 20 hours of 
database gave sufficient data to assist a trustworthy research study when the interview 
arrangements were properly developed (Bonnie, 2016). 
Therefore, during the data collection steps, there were key sampling points considered and 
which the researchers were aware of during this research study, and they are; 1) Audio virtual 
recording – this approach secured the illustrative validity of the examinations done, quickens 
remembrance and ideas as an element of the objectives with the stakeholders. 2) Fieldwork and 
testing – this approach is where the researcher visits the case study to observe and thoroughly 
investigate the activities in the case study and collect samples which will assist in raising 
reports about the research findings to add to the data collection process. 3) Bias - when 
conducting sampling in research, it's crucial to give careful consideration to research bias as it 
constitutes a fundamental component of the sampling process. If the sampling methods are 
influenced by personal judgments or if individuals with a favourable bias are involved in 
selecting the sample, it can result in a biassed sample (Bell et al. 2018). 4) Structured 
observation sampling - in the research study, the use of structured observations required careful 
decisions regarding the sampling process. This involved not only selecting multiple individuals 
for the sample but also addressing all the pertinent issues related to the sampling process, as 
emphasised by Bell et al. in their 2018 work. 5) Qualitative interview sampling - transparency 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 157 
 
gaps can occasionally be inherent in qualitative research, particularly concerning the sampling 
process. The most challenging aspects often revolve around determining the criteria for 
selecting interviewees and ascertaining the number of individuals interviewed and qualitative 
researchers acknowledge that their sampling methods are often rooted in convenience or 
opportunistic choices, as noted by Bell, Bryman and Harley (2018). 
The data ordering was also done in this research study to ensure a straightforward and 
accessible evaluation of the process and data analysis, the data was organised sequentially, a 
step undertaken during the third phase of the research. 
The data analysis encompasses the crucial task of converting gathered data into coherent and 
meaningful insights. In this study, data analysis was conducted concurrently with data 
collection, with a specific focus on ongoing interviews and observations, allowing for the 
development of emerging conclusions. Various strategies were employed, including 
categorization techniques such as coding and thematic analysis, as well as connection strategies 
like narrative analysis, individual case studies, and the use of memos and displays. 
Data coding plays a pivotal role in category generation, especially since the initial data at the 
outset of the analysis is predominantly descriptive. According to Shaw (2013:55), “…the 
analysis is a process of sense-making of the data concerning research questions and concerns, 
the sense-making occurs as a process of ascribing codes to the verbatim transcriptions and other 
data”. The various coding methods (appendix -) that assisted with facilitating the generation of 
questions and potential answers regarding their causal relationships are: 1) Open coding - open 
coding is often described as the initial conceptualization of abstract concepts or entities. The 
process of open coding commences with a constant comparison approach, wherein the 
researcher identifies categories based on the phenomena observed within the derived data. 2) 
Axial coding - This is the stage where results and interactions are thoroughly examined. During 
axial coding, the process of sample reduction takes place, involving the consolidation of 
categories by merging them into broader ones. 3) Categorization and Proposition - At this stage, 
the synthesis of relevance and impacts culminates in the formulation of a proposition regarding 
the phenomenon under study. This is achieved by taking into account the observed results and 
the ongoing developments. Hence, a category is established based on the proposition, and it 
can be employed to advance the research process. 4) Memo - These represent the insights 
gained during the categorization processes. They also empower the researcher to create a 
checklist for the categories, aiding in the ongoing comprehension of the data collection process 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 158 
 
(Charmaz, 2008). 5) Selective Coding – This serves as a method for promptly narrowing down 
categories, thereby pinpointing the primary core variables. It is also the juncture at which the 
research trajectory shifts, with the categories guiding the research process. As Douglas (2003) 
articulates, "The selective coding process aids in reducing the categories distinguished by the 
researcher to a smaller sample size, within which propositions are assigned codes for the 
categories". 6) Theoretical sampling – This is a method wherein the refined categories are 
redefined, and additional data is gathered to align with these categories. It is a process through 
which data collection is systematically conducted to formulate a theory (theory description). 
Furthermore, other coding methods can be adopted for this stage, and this is the following: 1) 
Mini literature search - This is the phase where the research process involves the creation of 
memos and mini literature reviews following the collection and analysis of data in each stage. 
The researcher maintains this ongoing process as a collection of notes throughout the research 
journey, enabling them to stay informed and generate insights by reflecting on the collected 
data and making informal notes. This pivotal aspect of the research process serves as an 
intermediary step for drafting papers and gathering additional data (Charmaz, 2006). 2) 
Documentary research - As indicated by Bailey (1994), documentary research encompasses 
both primary and secondary documents. Primary documents entail firsthand accounts and 
"eyewitness" experiences relevant to the research study, while secondary documents provide 
insights from individuals who were not present but have knowledge of the event or research 
subject. The documentary research process involves the analysis of documents containing 
information about the studied phenomenon, as highlighted by Ahmed (2010). To ensure the 
authenticity of the documentary research, it is imperative to conceptualise, evaluate, analyse, 
and implement quality control measures on the documents. 
Therefore, the summary of this research process which is the dependability was utilised with 
the grounded theory, as it has the potential to yield both empirical findings (core variables) and 
a theoretical model (causal loop diagram). This process led to the development of concepts and 
the creation of a causal loop diagram which was used to further develop the SETILL model, 
demonstrating the reliability and credibility of the research study. The appendix --- provides a 
summary of the steps undertaken and illustrates the thorough application of the grounded 
theory process leading to the SETILL model development. 
Establishment of the categories and analytic codes from data: In this procedure, the 
researcher constructs categories through ongoing comparison and analysis of the collected data. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 159 
 
What sets this process apart is the presence of a preconceived or predetermined theory that 
emerges during the research journey. 
Acknowledgement of the underlying collective process within the data: Following the 
creation of categories and analytical codes, the essential collective process is subsequently 
formulated around the core categories. 
Inductive creation of conceptual categories: Inductive reasoning is a fundamental 
characteristic of grounded theory, as outlined by Leboea (2017). It represents the qualitative 
approach employed by researchers to uncover a phenomenon by setting aside any preconceived 
notions and constructing a theory based on the phenomenon itself. In grounded theory, the 
theory is developed and often not subjected to testing. In This study, the theory will be tested. 
Enhancing categories through the process of theoretical sampling: In grounded theory, the 
primary objective for the researcher is to construct categories and subsequently transform these 
categories into a conceptual theory, representing the core purpose of grounded theory 
methodology. The method of theoretical sampling involves selecting data that align with 
theoretical considerations. This concept is reinforced by Breckenridge and Jones (2009), who 
emphasise that theoretical sampling is a fundamental hallmark of grounded theory and a critical 
component in the development of any theory grounded in data. 
Theory saturation: Saturation occurs when the researcher consistently encounters identical 
findings. It typically happens when, after multiple rounds of qualitative data collection and 
analysis, no fresh insights, inputs, directions, or data contribute to the established categories. 
Challenges in Grounded Theory: One significant concern regarding the challenges in 
grounded theory is that it often concludes with the theory itself, rather than delving into the 
elucidation of themes. Numerous studies offer comprehensive thematic illustrations without a 
substantive underlying theory (Kennedy and Lingard, 2006). This absence of theoretical 
abstractions makes it difficult to discern meaningful relationships and interconnections among 
the themes. Thus, it becomes imperative to develop and validate the theory before proceeding 
with the second phase of the research study. Another risk highlighted in this context is the 
misconception that researchers employing the grounded theory approach can initiate their study 
without any research questions or prior exploration of existing literature, potentially resulting 
in disorganised research manuscripts that impede the research process (Suddaby, 2006). In this 
particular study, this risk was mitigated by adopting a more controlled approach—beginning 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 160 
 
with research questions that evolved. The research questions played a pivotal role in guiding 
the researcher's decisions regarding sampling, data collection, and research methods. 
2.3.4 
Descriptive study II (DS-II) 
This is where the co-designed energy technology innovation (biogas technology) with gender 
design was tested during a workshop that took place in Limpopo South Africa. The testing of 
the biogas technology and other activities were done by the researchers, the University of 
Pretoria and other stakeholders involved in the SETILL. The objective of the testing was to 
assess the performance of the biogas technology in conjunction with its intended users and to 
observe the evolving dynamics of the proposed co-design process (Petrulaityte et al., 2022). 
A mixed-method approach was employed to collect feedback on the biogas technology and 
SETILL performance at the time of the workshop. This included qualitative data from 
researchers' observations, both quantitative and qualitative data from participants' assessment 
questionnaires, and quantitative and qualitative data encompassing ideas generated and 
elaborated upon at the time of the workshop (Petrulaityte et al., 2022). Therefore, the analysis 
of the qualitative data was conducted with the coding methods already explained above 
(Creswell, 2013), while the analysis of the quantitative data was done using some inferential 
statistics implementations (Creswell, 2009) in order to establish the percentage of individuals 
who provided a particular assessment. 
Therefore, regarding the Design Research Methodology used to propagate the methods used to 
conduct this research study, an assessment questionnaire was used to assess the following; 1) 
Integrity - The degree to which the content within the biogas technology innovation and 
SETILL is comprehensive and well-structured concerning gender mainstreaming, energy 
security, and poor informal settlements. 2) Practicability - The degree of ease with which the 
biogas technology innovation and SETILL can be comprehended and put into practice. 3) 
Validity - The degree to which biogas technology innovation and SETILL empower end-users 
to grasp contemporary energy-related challenges and foster gender-specific energy concepts 
for impoverished informal settlements. 
Furthermore, The Design Research Methodology (DRM) necessitates the visualisation of the 
current state of affairs to facilitate a thorough analysis of the situation and the development of 
a proposed support ‘model’ aimed at enhancing the situation (Calderon, 2010) at the informal 
settlement. Therefore, the research questions that originally ignited interest in this research 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 161 
 
study are displayed in Figure 4.5 below and linking them to the DRM stages of Figure 4.3 as 
they pertain to the issues raised by potential participants or stakeholders during the Systems 
Living Lab activities and also during the preliminary exploratory discussions conducted before 
the commencement of the research study. This can also be called the ‘consistency matrix’ for 
research studies with more than one research question (Walwyn and Chan, 2019). This implies 
that in numerous instances, the researchers may employ various methods of data collection and 
analysis for each research question, which in such scenarios, and indeed for all research studies 
comprising multiple research questions, it proves beneficial to create a ‘consistency matrix’ to 
visually outline for each question, the pertinent literature and the designated data 
collection/analysis approach to be employed (Walwyn and Chan, 2019).

question. For instance, the research questions are specifically addressed in the stages of the

2.4 Additional essential information pertaining to data collection and 
analysis in this study 
This section explains the other data collection and analysis tools used for this research study to 
address the research questions. It also includes a brief explanation of the case study used for 
this research study. 
2.4.1 
Application of system dynamics 
System dynamics, which is a part of the systems thinking approach, was used in this study. 
Systems approaches are primarily characterised as a comprehensible cause-and-effect 
framework involving interconnected variables within a system. It necessitates the independent 
assessment and refinement of mental models aligned with a particular mode of thinking 
(Cabrera, Colosi and Lobdell, 2008). Furthermore, this approach can be viewed as a crucial 
means of scrutinising intricate interrelationships, which inherently entail complexity. 
Consequently, it has been recognized as a central approach in this research study and was 
discussed in detail in Chapter 2 of this study. Moreover, System dynamics is acknowledged as 
a suitable methodology for examining complex social and economic systems within the context 
of systems Living Lag, energy studies and gender inequality in informal settlements against 
energy poverty. 
This systems approach, in particular, proves valuable in simulating intricate problems and 
policy frameworks through experimental approaches (Forrester, 1994). It centres on control 
theory (Kirkwood, 1998) and modern nonlinear dynamics theory (Amigun, Musango and 
Stafford, 2011). Often, the interrelationship diagram takes the form of stock and flow diagrams. 
Notably, systems approach equips policymakers with ready-made tools to address significant 
complex challenges (Sterman, 1991) such as energy poverty and gender inequality. 
Significantly, this approach finds application in modelling the connections and 
interdependencies among variables that influence the contribution of women as end-users in 
the informal settlements in energy-challenged areas and contribute to energy poverty reduction 
efforts in the Western Cape, South Africa, and beyond. The approach is also characterised by 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 163 
 
its utilisation of causal loop diagrams to depict causal relationships among seemingly unrelated 
elements within a system (Forrester, 1973; Forrester, 1987, 1991). Likewise, causal loop 
diagrams have proven effective in illustrating intricate relationships and feedback mechanisms 
across various sectors, including agriculture (Vennix, 1999) and the energy sector (Amigun, 
Musango and Stafford, 2011; Okoye, 2020). 
Therefore, to answer research questions 3 and 4 respectively: the “system dynamics 
(community-based system dynamics)” modelling which is a part of the “systems thinking 
approach” was used to improve understanding of the issues and behaviours of the stakeholders. 
The case study, comparative analysis and stakeholder mapping were also used as the researcher 
mapped the stakeholders in Chapter 3 to understand the level of influence in the decision-
making process and compare the case study with the Living Lab concept. Therefore, the system 
thinking gives an insight into who links to whom during the stakeholder mapping and allows 
for mapping the decision-making which can be the high-level points for gender innovation 
decision-making. It also helped to understand or determine the extent of women's decision-
making in that part.  
Community-based system dynamics modelling 
Community-based system dynamics which is a part of the system dynamics originated from 
the study of Hovmand (2014). According to Hovmand (2014), community-based system 
dynamics is the engagement of communities to participate in the creation of models that 
produce recommendations and insights to the system, empowerment of women, and encourage 
the community members to implement changes relating to the insights. According to Suzanne 
Smit (2019), “Community-based system dynamics is an adaptation of mainstream system 
dynamics and group model building, which emerged because of the need to engage with less 
sophisticated audiences to improve learning and understanding of the systems that they are part 
of”. 
The community-based system dynamics were relevant to understanding behaviours expected 
of stakeholders influencing the energy technology innovation developed through the systems 
Living Lab. It has also been used for several issues like the study of the abuse of alcohol in 
college students (Apostolopoulos et al., 2018), the issues of “mental health in Afghanistan” 
(Trani et al., 2016) and many more in the natural management like “water and forestry 
planning” (Rosenthal et al., 2017). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 164 
 
Therefore, this systems thinking method was adapted to build a model that helped map the 
interrelationships to show the stakeholder contributions, the level of gender innovations 
decision-making, and the extent of women's decision-making in the systems Living Lab 
(research questions 2 and 3). 
Causal loop diagram 
As a result of this application, an interrelationship diagram is generated, consisting of 
interconnected loops with visual representations and dynamics employed to elucidate the 
theory underpinning the system. The connections to various variables serve as the foundation 
for constructing the causal loop diagram, with feedback loops providing insights into the 
behaviour of the phenomenon. This research has led to the modelling of the SETILL with 
gender design as a systemic behaviour. 
2.4.2 
Internet research on websites 
The internet has evolved into a prevalent tool in daily life for conducting management, 
engineering and technology research. It has significantly enhanced data transmission and 
storage in research endeavours, simplifying the tracking of social processes compared to 
traditional face-to-face interactions (Easterby-Smith, Thorpe & Jackson, 2012). 
Utilising internet resources like university databases, journals, and books has been proposed as 
a modest but significant advancement in data collection for research. Additionally, the 
proliferation of individual-owned computers has introduced new opportunities for researchers 
to gather, store, and manage data (Lee, 2000). Williams (2007) suggests that researchers 
leveraging internet resources as a delivery tool have numerous possibilities to enhance and 
streamline their data collection processes. A more comprehensive examination of the 
credibility of internet-based research data will be presented in Chapter Six of this study. 
2.4.3 
Case study areas 
This study was conducted in Tzaneen, found in the Limpopo Province (situated in the far 
northern part) of South Africa and in Umuahia, found in the Abia State (situated in the northern 
part of Abia State) of Nigeria. 
According to Vacant and Particulars (2009); and Statistics South Africa (SSA), (2012), 
Tzaneen (figure 4.6) is a spacious tropical garden town located within the Mopani District 
Municipality of South Africa's Limpopo province. Nestled in a region blessed with abundant 
rainfall and fertile soil, it encompasses approximately 20,000 square kilometres (7,700 square 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 165 
 
miles) dedicated to tropical and subtropical agriculture. Tzaneen ranks as the second-largest 
town in Limpopo, trailing only Polokwane in size. The town, with a population of 14,571 
residents according to the 2011 census, serves as a focal point for the approximately 475,000 
people residing within a 30-kilometre (19-mile) radius. Below is the drawn map representing 
Tzaneen, South Africa, case study. 
 
Figure 4.6: Drawn map of the case study 1, Lephepane community of Tzaneen in Limpopo 
province, South Africa (SAV South Africa, 2023). 
Umuahia, with its local pronunciation [ʊmʊaːhiaː], serves as the administrative centre of Abia 
State, nestled in the southeastern region of Nigeria. Positioned along a railway line that links 
Port Harcourt to the south and Enugu city to the north. Umuahia (figure 4.7) boasts a population 
of 359,230 as recorded in the 2006 Nigerian census. The city is deeply rooted in Igbo culture 
and encompasses two local government areas, Umuahia North and Umuahia South, each 
comprising various clans such as Umugbalu, Umuopara, Ibeku, Olokoro, Ubakala, and Ohuhu 
communities (Asiegbu Johnson, 1985). Umuahia is celebrated as a pivotal hub for both railway 
transport and agricultural commerce, drawing traders and farmers from neighbouring towns 
who come to vend their harvests, including yams, cassava, maize, taro, citrus fruits, palm oil, 
and kernels. Below is the drawn map representing Umuahia, Nigeria, case study. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 166 
 
 
Figure 4.7: Drawn map of the case study 2, Umuahia Town in Abia State, Nigeria. Adapted 
from: (Onyekwere and Nwakanma, 2022:127). 
Therefore, as discussed above, the research was conducted within the Lephepane community 
of Tzaneen, situated in South Africa's Limpopo Province and the Umuahia community situated 
in Nigeria's Abia State. These communities face vulnerabilities to various challenges, including 
gender inequality, energy poverty, and lack of access to clean cooking sources. Therefore, to 
address the research questions, two case studies were used to provide empirical evidence. This 
is because the data were collected in the two case studies and from there showed how that can 
be promoted within the SETI Living Lab to ensure the gendered innovations in the Living Lab. 
Furthermore, it was found during the problem exploratory phase (Petrulaityte et al., 2022) that 
these two case studies were very alike and similar and possess 98% of the same energy 
behaviours and energy challenges, which implies that the same methods could work for both 
case studies. 
The Tzaneen case study was the focal point and was instrumental to the design or development 
and testing of the energy technology innovation with gender design. This means that, the 
development of gender-inclusive biogas technology, a key innovation resulting from this 
research study, was accomplished and tested at the Tzaneen case study alone. The primary 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 167 
 
rationale behind this approach lies in the financial constraints associated with implementing 
biogas technology in one of the case studies, as well as the limited time available to complete 
the project. 
Consequently, only one of the case studies was selected for comprehensive testing of the energy 
technology innovation developed. However, the data obtained from the initial case study 
indicates the feasibility of developing biogas technology in the second case study (Umuahia, 
Nigeria). This opens the door for further research and study in this location, which could be 
done as a published article. In light of these findings, it becomes evident that the SETILL model 
can be effectively applied to address issues of energy poverty and gender inequality in both 
case studies. However, it has the potential to stimulate the generation of gendered energy 
technology innovations in the regions. 
In addition, there were several grounds or rationale emerging from this research when we 
designed and implemented this research study in two case study sites and then decided to use 
only one site for nurturing the theoretical model by developing the biogas technology. Here are 
some of the common justifications: 
Resource Limitations: Carrying out research in different case studies is time-consuming and 
costly as it consumes more time and resources in terms of personnel. Choosing to only test the 
biogas technology in one case very much made sense because of concerns of resources 
available for the study (Creswell, 2014; Denscombe, 2014; Yin, 2014). 
Cost Constraints: The use of the biogas technology of this research study in different areas 
was very costly to actualize. Hence, in an effort to manage costs, we concentrated only on the 
testing phase of the SETILL theoretical model on one research case.  
Time Constraints: This research study had set timelines or time barriers from the institute of 
study. Thus, it supported the overall propositions of the theoretical model in testing the same 
where development of the biogas technology in one case study helped in timely and focused 
study (Gerring, 2007; Creswell, 2014; Yin, 2014). 
Feasibility: The case was more difficult to conduct research for this study than the other 
because of some logistical, political or environmental circumstance in Umuahia, Nigeria. It 
was more practical to direct the attention at validating the theoretical model by advancing the 
biogas technology in one, yet more reachable center (Tzaneen, South Africa) (Stake, 1995; 
George, A. L., & Bennett, 2005; Creswell, 2014; Yin, 2014). 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 168 
 
Initial Exploration: Another valid justification was that we intended to explore the potential 
of this particular Living Lab concept or technology in multiple settings to gather initial data 
and insights. Subsequent testing of the theoretical model by developing the biogas technology 
was focused on the most promising or relevant case study (Tzaneen, South Africa) (K. M. 
Eisenhardt, 1989). 
Pilot Study: Also, we intended to use one case study that will serve as a pilot or preliminary 
investigation to refine research methods and gather initial data, which informed a more 
comprehensive study in the selected case (Creswell, 2014; Denscombe, 2014; Yin, 2014). 
Comparative Analysis: Also, we intended to compare and contrast the results between the 
case studies to draw meaningful conclusions. Therefore, in these cases, we carried out a more 
extensive analysis in one case (Tzaneen, South Africa), while the other (Umuahia, Nigeria) 
served as a reference or contrast point (Gerring, 2007; Rihoux, B., & Ragin, 2009; Yin, 2014). 
Resource Allocation: We choose to allocate more resources to in-depth analysis and testing in 
one case to maximise the quality of the results and the depth of understanding (Kathleen M. 
Eisenhardt, 1989; Gerring, 2007; Baxter, P., & Jack, 2008; Creswell, 2014; Yin, 2014; Patton, 
2015). 
Research Focus: This research study aims to address specific research questions that were best 
examined in both of the case studies. However, to fully achieve research question 4, testing the 
results of the research study in one case study was most relevant at the moment of this research 
study (Kathleen M. Eisenhardt, 1989; Baxter, P., & Jack, 2008; Creswell, 2014; Miles, M.B., 
Huberman, A.M., & Saldana, 2014; Yin, 2014). 
Ethical or Regulatory Considerations: At the time of the research study, the case study in 
Umuahia, Nigeria involved ethical or regulatory challenges that needed to be addressed before 
extensive testing can occur. Therefore, focusing on a single case that was not challenging 
initially was a necessary step (Elliott, R., Fischer, C.T., & Rennie, 1999; Morrow, 2005; 
Creswell, 2014; Bryman, 2016; Norman K. Densin., 2018; Silverman, 2019). 
Ultimately, the decision to conduct this research in two case studies and test the theoretical 
model by developing a gendered energy technology innovation (biogas technology) in one case 
study alone was based on the research objectives, available resources, and the specific context 
of the study. It was important that we clearly communicated and justified this choice in the 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 169 
 
research methodology chapter and in this section. This will be reported again at the conclusion 
chapter of this thesis. 
2.4.4 
SETILL codesign workshop 
The workshops and meetings focusing on gendered energy technology, biogas technology and 
Living Lab were held in the case studies to determine the phenomenon being studied, how to 
engage the gender innovations and for the feedback. 
However, the energy technology innovation codesign workshop extended invitations to the 
community members of the Lephepane informal settlement Tzaneen, South Africa, alongside 
representatives from both private and public sectors. The purpose was to collaboratively design 
gender-inclusive energy technology innovation (biogas technology) tailored to meet the unique 
needs of the end-users or the Tzaneen residents. The participants were a true reflection of the 
energy technology innovation target audience, encompassing a diverse group of stakeholders 
deeply invested in addressing energy challenges within informal settlements. 
In total, the workshop drew 42 participants, comprising 31 women and 11 men including the 
facilitator, who were selected through the platform of the Mater Pluviam Holdings recruitment. 
Notably, over half of the attendees had previously participated in stakeholder engagement 
initiatives organised by the researcher and the technology developers, establishing a preexisting 
familiarity with both one another and the research study. Furthermore, all participants 
possessed proficiency in the English language. 
Furthermore, during the workshops, only 5 people out of the 42 participants have heard about 
the biogas technology, it uses and how it can benefit South Africa and only 2 people know how 
it works. This means that only about 5% in the case study knows about biogas technology. This 
shows that the biogas technology is lacking behind in South Africa (Mukumba, Makaka and 
Mamphweli, 2016). All the schools and majority of the women were still cooking with 
firewood, thus; biogas as a clean energy which does not stain pots like the firewood, was 
accepted by the residents and everyone was excited to have the biogas developed in the case 
study. 
At the onset of the workshop, the participants were divided into six multi-stakeholder groups, 
each comprising seven members. These groups were intentionally composed to include 
representatives from local and private companies, members of the Lephepane Tzaneen

community, the researcher, the government and other stakeholders as represented in the figure

the Living Lab concept and systems thinking, including energy (current household energy 
issues), gender (optimal approaches and foundational principles for promoting gender 
inclusivity in the energy industry), and informal rural settlements (a brief introduction to 
Tzaneen and Umuahia).

Subsequently, the gendered energy technology innovation codesign canvas (appendix - ) was 
presented and distributed among the participants. Subsequent to this, each participating group 
was tasked with choosing a specific design segment to concentrate on throughout the workshop 
(mostly the women since the design would benefit them the most as initially explained in this 
research study). 
The researcher proposed three design objectives aligned with the focus areas of participating 
stakeholders and the energy end users featured in the design process, which include cooking, 
lighting, and self and household care (Petrulaityte et al., 2022). These are: 
❖ Develop a clean cooking solution that utilises waste as a resource 
❖ Create a lighting system to enhance safety in the settlement from the technology. 
❖ Design a proper self and house care plan for the informal settlements 
After selecting the design objectives, stakeholders embarked on a structured codesign process 
guided by the facilitator/researcher. The process unfolded as follows: 
⮚ Each group had the task of pinpointing which of the three household design 
objectives, as indicated on the problem chart 
⮚ Stakeholders engaged in detailed discussions about the information presented on the 
chart and energy innovation canvas, subsequently delving deeper into these chosen 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 173 
 
issues within the context of Tzaneen informal settlement. They accomplished this by 
questioning group members and recording responses using additional data collection 
techniques. 
⮚ Following the problem exploration phase, groups initiated the generation of 
innovative ideas centred around the three selected design objectives or challenges. 
Each group generated their own ideas towards the development of the energy 
technology innovation with gender designs 
⮚ In the subsequent stage, stakeholders were required to pick the most promising ideas 
from their collective brainstorming and integrate them into a single energy innovation 
canvas for their respective groups. This enabled the development of the energy 
technology innovation with gender designs 
⮚ The workshop's culmination involved group presentations showcasing their final, 
detailed ideas and opinions. This was followed by a comprehensive group discussion 
on energy technology innovation, culminating in the collection of assessment 
questionnaires.

In general, secondary data was also collected from the second case study to show credibility, 
and further address the research questions. However, the ultimate objective of this project is to 
establish a Systemic Energy Technology Innovation Living Lab (SETILL) in the designated 
case study areas specifically in the informal settlements of Tzaneen, South Africa, and 
Umuahia, Nigeria. The outcomes of the SETILL workshop will be showcased in the fifth 
chapter of this thesis. 
2.4.5 
Literature review approach 
This approach involved a literature search and successive analysis. This approach was used to 
address research question 1, leading to the theoretical model. This is a very vital approach as 
each chapter of this study went through the review approach to understand the phenomenon 
being studied. This emerged from the intention to attain a sustainable energy technology to 
meet the energy products by featuring women in the design process always, which was always 
done through the help of the systems Living Lab. 
Therefore, conceptualizing the Living Lab concept and looking at literature that informed the 
data collection to derive a new emerging theory helped to answer the research questions. This 
was also done by conceptualizing the Living Lab and giving a critical analysis of the terms 
defined and how the research study reconceptualized it for context-specific application in the 
case studies. A systematic literature review was used to further address research question 1 (see 
Chapter 2). The approach to the literature review was based on the following objectives: to 
develop the phenomenon of the SETI Living Lab in a poor informal settlement, as well as to 
provide a relevant and informed understanding of the gendered energy Living Lab and its core 
theories. 
The approach used for the literature review was to consider the topic at three levels of recursion 
(see Figure 2.1). The literature review was also conducted to understand the topic, the 
attributes, the antecedents, and the consequences, which helped to initiate chapter 3 of this 
study and finalize the result chapter of the study (theory building). The antecedents are the 
events that must be present before the development of the topic; the attributes are the 
characteristics that repeatedly appear in the topic, and the consequences are the events that 
happened because of the development of the topic. 
Literature review search 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 175 
 
The literature review was performed in the following archives of academics: Science Direct, 
University of Pretoria database, Scopus, Springer, “IEEE (Institute of Electrical and 
Electronics Engineers)”, “ACM (Association for Computing Machinery)” and related search 
terms to ‘ISI web of knowledge. The additional search terms used are; Living Lab, Living 
Laboratory, Living Laboratories, energy, energy security, energy studies, energy poverty, 
urban and rural Living Lab, urban and rural environment, gender, gender studies, gender 
equality, gendered energy security, gender mainstreaming, systems thinking and informal 
settlement. 
In addition, the searches of the terms above were done with scholarly search engines like 
Google and Google Scholar in areas of ICT, education, health, gender, energy and other Living 
Lab research areas and methodologies. 
Literature review analysis 
The following analyses were done during this research study: 
1. Analysis of what Living Lab is and where it has been applied 
2. Analysis of the typology of the Living Labs 
3. Identification of the theoretical basis of the Living Lab 
4. Analysis of the concepts of operational Living Labs 
5. Analysis of the processes and methodologies for the Living Lab concepts 
6. Analysis of gender innovations in the Living Lab 
7. Identification of the context of energy Living Lab 
8. Investigation of Living Lab with systems thinking concepts. 
 
2.5 The research plans 
This section outlines the key phases of the research process, spanning from data collection to 
theory development. It elucidates the pivotal checkpoints employed to continuously evaluate 
and oversee the research study, allowing for adjustments when deemed necessary. 
Furthermore, this section provides an in-depth depiction of the iterative data collection cycles 
utilized. This section was mostly involved in completely addressing research questions 2 and 
3 leading to the theoretical model (research question 4). The processes can be seen in the 
appendix - 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 176 
 
2.5.1 
Research cycle one 
The initial data cycle encompasses multiple data sources subjected to rigorous analysis and 
scrutiny, enabling the researcher to extract pertinent information germane to the research study. 
These data were meticulously catalogued within the proposition log, facilitating the systematic 
tracking of each data point. This log also provided a means to describe the significance and 
influence of the study concerning the focal variable or research focus. It served as the repository 
for findings resulting from the synthesis of the concern variable's relevance and its impact, 
subsequently leading to the creation of pertinent categories. 
2.5.2 
Research cycle two 
In the second cycle, the researcher expanded upon and developed additional propositions and 
categories, building upon the foundation established in the first cycle. It is imperative during 
this phase to avoid duplicating data already gathered or represented in the initial cycle. The 
same methodology employed in the first cycle was replicated for data collection, with the newly 
generated propositions seamlessly integrated into the proposition log to advance the research 
study. In cycle two, these propositions can be assimilated into pre-existing categories or serve 
as a basis for the creation of entirely new categories to enrich the dataset. 
2.5.3 
Research cycle three 
Cycle three places a heightened emphasis on refining the sampling process while concurrently 
necessitating the generation of additional propositions. This phase is characterized by the 
construction of an interrelationship diagram aimed at uncovering the connections among 
various categories. When the categories are streamlined into more fundamental core categories 
to facilitate the development of a research study's theory, this process is referred to as reduction 
sampling. The initial interrelationship diagram is constructed by discerning whether a given 
category is a subtype, variation, or integral component of another category. 
Subsequently, a succinct literature review is conducted, serving as a valuable resource for 
enhancing the researcher's understanding of each category or concept and providing deeper 
insights into the research. 
2.5.4 
Research cycle four 
Cycle four marks the culmination of this research study, with a primary focus on achieving 
saturation. In this final phase, the ultimate interrelationship diagram is developed. Saturation 
occurs when the researcher begins encountering repetitive information regarding the studied 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 177 
 
phenomenon or when the researcher incorporates information into the categories established in 
the previous cycles to the extent that no fresh, pivotal details or data can be incorporated. 
Additionally, propositions were generated in this phase, which played a pivotal role in 
constructing the ultimate theory. 
 
2.6 The trustworthiness of this research study 
This section of the study serves a critical purpose in addressing several key questions: How 
might the results and conclusions be susceptible to inaccuracies? How can the data potentially 
acquired enhance our understanding within the context of the conceptual framework? 
Furthermore, why should the readers or stakeholders involved in the research study have 
confidence in the results? To ensure productivity and mitigate the risk of ineffectiveness, it is 
essential to remain vigilant about potential threats, understand them, and proactively seek 
strategies to address them within the research study. As emphasised by (Maxwell, 1996), the 
significance of this section lies in the researchers' ongoing efforts to minimise validity threats 
through the collection of supporting evidence throughout the research process. 
Trustworthiness holds particular relevance in this research study, as it serves as a means to 
establish credibility in the study. It emphasises the reliance on evidence rather than methods, 
ensuring that the research continually verifies the methodological processes employed." 
2.6.1 
Trustworthiness considerations in the context of a constructionist design 
While discussions surrounding the assurance and demonstration of the quality of 
constructionist design are prevalent, it is noteworthy that the term 'validity' is seldom used by 
authors (Easterby-Smith, Thorpe, & Jackson, 2012). Golden-Biddle and Locke, (1993) have 
identified three essential components for establishing trustworthiness within a constructionist 
framework, which are elaborated below: 
i. Authenticity: This pertains to convincing individuals that the researcher possesses a 
profound understanding of the insights derived from the institution. 
ii. Plausibility: This involves considering the presence of reviews or interest among fellow 
researchers. 
iii. Criticality: This encompasses persuading readers to question unexamined assumptions, 
thereby contributing genuinely innovative insights. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 178 
 
Easterby-Smith et al. (2012:96) noted that David Silverman (2000) advocates for a more 
objectivist stance due to concerns that researchers may selectively choose evidence to support 
their preconceived biases. Silverman (2000) also offers guidance to researchers on methods 
that can safeguard against anecdotal charges, as detailed below: 
i. Constant comparison: This employs grounded theory concepts to identify new contexts that 
extend the existing theory. 
ii. Comprehensive data treatment: This involves analysing all primary data before 
concluding. 
iii. Refutability: This entails actively seeking instances that could potentially challenge or 
disprove current beliefs. 
iv. Tabulations: This emphasises thorough organisation of data, which can prove useful in 
elucidating the occurrence of phenomena. 
Easterby-Smith et al. (2012:97) emphasised that constructionist research outcomes should be 
credible and reached through transparent methods. Therefore, the researcher must elucidate 
how they gained access to the organisation, describe the informant selection process, document 
data creation and recording, delineate processes for data summarization and synthesis, expound 
on how data evolved into tentative ideas and explanations, and convey their personal feelings 
about the research. Many of these aspects were duly considered and implemented in this 
research and chapter. Below, we will delve into elements of trustworthiness before addressing 
concerns related to external and internal validity, validity procedures, and validity threats." 
2.6.2 
Components of trustworthiness 
In this research study, a comprehensive evaluation of all aspects of the research study is 
challenging, and a researcher cannot provide a universal measure of the study's trustworthiness. 
Instead, the responsibility lies with the researchers to demonstrate the evidence of 
trustworthiness in the research (Maxwell, 1992). Similarly, just as quantitative research 
requires reliability for validity, qualitative research requires dependability for transferability. 
This means that judgments or inferences cannot be made directly from one perspective to 
another. 
In establishing the worth of research, the concepts of validity and trustworthiness are 
paramount (Lincoln & Guba 1985). A crucial question in assessing trustworthiness is, 'How 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 179 
 
can a researcher convince the audience, or even oneself, that the research findings merit 
attention?' This was achieved, assessed, or addressed through four key elements, briefly 
explained below, to aid in evaluating the trustworthiness of this study: 
i. Credibility: This pertains to the extent to which the researchers believe in the truthfulness 
of the research results, prompting the question of whether they appear genuine. 
ii. Confirmability: This measures the level of objectivity, determined by whether the research 
findings are shaped by the respondents rather than influenced by researcher bias, ensuring that 
the findings genuinely reflect the research focus. 
iii. Dependability: This signifies that the research results can be replicated or repeated and 
maintain consistency within the same or similar context. 
iv. Transferability: This denotes that the research findings can be applied to some extent in 
another context." 
2.6.3 
Alternatives to internal and external validity 
In the context of enhancing the credibility of research findings and addressing potential threats 
to validity, nine procedures can contribute to trustworthiness. These procedures include 
triangulation, the incorporation of disconfirming evidence, fostering reflexivity in the research 
process, employing member checking, engaging in prolonged fieldwork, fostering 
collaboration, maintaining an audit trail, providing rich and detailed descriptions, and seeking 
peer feedback and debriefing (Creswell & Miller, 2000:126). 
In this study, the term 'credibility' may be used interchangeably with 'internal validity.' This 
substitution aligns with established validity procedures, where 'internal validity' can be 
replaced with 'credibility' (Lincoln & Guba, 1985). However, when it comes to 'external 
validity,' which is beyond the researcher's control, it may be replaced with 'transferability.' As 
Shaw (2013:58) notes, this redefinition signifies a notable shift in the concept's meaning, as the 
responsibility for demonstrating transferability lies with those seeking to apply the findings in 
different contexts when the findings are context-specific. 
Moreover, regarding the considerations for constructing valid and trustworthy research within 
a constructionist framework, the selection of validity procedures should be informed by the 
paradigmatic positions of this study. Three paradigms were examined for comparison in 
selecting validity procedures. Please refer to Table 4.3 below." 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 180 
 
Table 4.3: Assessment of validity procedures across paradigm positions
(Source from Creswell and Miller, 2000:126). 
2.6.4 
Categories of validity (trustworthiness) and their associated challenges 
The categories of validity encompass descriptive, interpretative, theoretical, and 
generalizability validity. 
Descriptive validity: In the process of depicting the researcher's observations and auditory 
experiences, the primary threat to description validity pertains to potential gaps or inaccuracies 
in the data. To mitigate this concern, the researchers employed audio or video recordings during 
observations and interviews, along with subsequent verbatim transcriptions of these recordings, 
effectively averting a significant validity risk within the study. This precaution is essential 
because any inferences or conclusions drawn by the researcher based on the descriptive data 
can be called into question if the initial description of the observed events or conducted 
interviews lacks validity. Consequently, interviews are routinely recorded and transcribed, 
unless compelling reasons exist to deviate from this practice (Maxwell, 1996). 
Interpretive validity: As Maxwell (1996) suggests, a key challenge to achieving valid 
interpretation is the temptation to superimpose one's framework or meaning onto the 
perspectives of the individuals under study, rather than authentically representing their 
viewpoints and the meanings they ascribe to their words and actions. This potential pitfall can 
manifest in several ways: 
1. When the researchers fail to attentively discern the intended meanings of the 
participants. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 181 
 
2. When the researchers are unaware of the frameworks and assumptions of the 
participants. 
3. When the researchers do not allow the participants to give their views in full and often 
when the researchers use leading and brief questions. 
In order to counteract this threat to validity, it was not a random way of re-categorizing 
participant expressions based on certain prerequisites (Maxwell, 1996), but incorporated a 
systematic method of tracing how the participants interpreted the research situation, and a 
member-checking technique to conquer this threat of invalidity. 
Theory and generalisation validity: Strauss and Corbin (1990) identified another cardinal 
issue: lack of concern for other, perhaps competing or even conflicting interpretations of the 
phenomena in study or other ways of making sense of collected data. To avoid this threat the 
researchers adopted the practice of reviewing reasearch theories dealing with concepts not 
captured in this study. 
Maxwell (1996) posited that there are two forms of generalisation: internal and external 
generalizability. Internal generalizability pertains to the extent to which conclusions can be 
applied within the specific settings or groups studied, whereas external generalizability 
concerns the broader applicability of conclusions beyond the studied settings or groups. In the 
realm of this research study, internal generalizability assumes particular significance. 
2.6.5 
Threats to validity 
In this research study, two primary categories of validity threats exist, although Maxwell (1996) 
noted that it is not feasible to enumerate all potential validity threats to the conclusions drawn 
in this research. These categories encompass researcher bias and reactivity (about the 
researcher's influence on the settings or individuals being studied). 
Researcher Bias: These instances refer to situations where a researcher's theories, values, or 
preconceptions inadvertently shape the collection and analysis of data. In this research, the 
primary concern is to understand how an individual researcher's values and perspectives might 
influence the study's approach and findings, rather than erasing the diversity in researchers' 
values and viewpoints they bring to the study (Hammersley & Atkinson, 1995). 
The potential biases in this research might include researcher bias and participant bias. The 
researchers addressed these concerns by meticulously recording responses during interviews 
to mitigate researcher bias. Additionally, personal, face-to-face interviews were conducted to 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 182 
 
promote transparency, and participant identities were kept confidential to minimise participant 
bias. 
Reactivity: Reactivity refers to the impact of the researchers on the settings or individuals 
under study. In this research, the objective is not to eliminate the researcher's influence but 
rather to comprehend and harness it effectively, as entirely eradicating the researcher's impact 
is not feasible (Hammersley & Atkinson, 1995). 
Maxwell (1996) elucidated that in participant observation studies, reactivity is not considered 
a significant validity threat, contrary to common belief. However, in the case of interviews, 
reactivity poses a substantial validity threat that cannot be completely avoided. For instance, 
the information provided by participants is influenced by both the interviewer and the 
interview's context. 
It is argued that incorporating credibility and originality can enhance the resonance and utility 
of this research (Charmaz, 2006). This notion has prompted several questions, as outlined 
below: 
i. Credibility: Can readers independently evaluate the researcher's claims? Does the argument 
draw strong connections to the collected data? Are there sufficient empirical observations 
presented for each category? Has enough data been provided to support the claims? 
ii. Originality: Can novel ideas be derived from the established categories? What is the 
significance of the research study? Can the methodologies contribute to the modification of 
existing ideas and practices? 
iii. Resonance: Do participants recognize the essence of the methodologies? Does their lived 
experience align with the identified categories? Can participants see that the analysis provides 
insights into their communities and lives? 
iv. Usefulness: Can the researchers propose additional areas for research? Does the research 
contribute to expanding knowledge? Can individuals or communities apply the results in their 
daily lives and environments? 
2.6.6 
 Considerations of validity 
In the event of validity concerns, the following suggested approaches are provided. While these 
issues should be addressed within the sampling section, they are discussed here because the 
researcher also perceives them as potential threats to validity. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 183 
 
Energy poverty system selection 
The researcher examined various energy poverty systems, such as the Lephepane community 
system, Umugbalu community system and Mater Pluvian Holdings training systems, which 
had been actively responding to energy poverty and mitigating associated gender inequality at 
the time of this research. These system studies were instrumental in gaining insights into 
current activities and informed the researcher's decision on which systems to engage with 
during fieldwork. The researchers primarily focused on energy poverty and gender inequality 
systems in South Africa, except in cases where additional relevant studies were identified in 
different locations, which had not been initially considered. The unique characteristics and 
distinctions of South Africa and Nigeria were among the factors that influenced the decision to 
concentrate on energy poverty and gender inequality systems in these nations and not explore 
systems elsewhere. 
Energy poverty and gender inequality victims’ selections 
Several concerns and questions arose regarding the interviews conducted in this study, which 
include: 
1. Did the researcher interview a sufficient number of energy poverty and gender 
inequality victims? 
2. Did the researcher inadvertently introduce bias into the data through participant 
selection? 
3. Did the researcher intentionally seek out victims with diverse perspectives and 
opinions regarding the situation? 
4. Did the researcher engage with victims who were outspoken and critical of the energy 
poverty response teams? 
5. Did the researcher interview individuals who were well-known or relatively 
unknown? 
6. Did the researcher exclusively interview participants who were referred to them? 
7. Did the researcher make efforts to gather both positive and negative feedback? 
The interviews were conducted following the previously described methodologies, and the 
researcher concluded the interview process when no new information or insights were 
forthcoming, a point commonly referred to as reaching saturation." 
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 184 
 
 
How can we ascertain the accuracy of the acquired information? 
To accomplish this, the researcher took measures to create a comfortable environment for 
participants, ensuring that the researcher-participant relationship would not pose a threat to 
validity. Additionally, interviews with participants were conducted in settings separate from 
the initial location to encourage open and candid feedback. The researcher also provided 
assurances of confidentiality to maintain the trust and privacy of the participants' identities. 
2.6.7 
Ethical considerations 
Could the research pose any harm to any of the stakeholders, members of energy poverty 
systems, the communities, or energy poverty victims? 
There was no harm inflicted upon the stakeholders, members of energy poverty systems, the 
communities, energy poverty victims, or individuals who took part in this research study, as 
their identities remained confidential. However, it is worth noting that the researchers could 
not eliminate the potential risk that those who interacted with the energy poverty and gender 
inequality victims might express opinions about them based on the information obtained during 
the interviews. As a result, some victims or concerned individuals chose to abstain from 
participation. These ethical considerations are further elaborated upon below: 
Confidentiality  
The primary ethical concern in this report or research study revolved around the principle of 
confidentiality, which entailed safeguarding the confidentiality of energy poverty data gathered 
from various sources, including informal settlements, Mater Pluviam Holdings, Umuahia 
community and the Tzaneen municipality. 
Selection of participants  
The researchers conducted interviews with various stakeholders, including members of energy 
poverty and gender inequality systems, the local government, and energy poverty victims who 
were participants in this research study. During these interviews, audio and video recordings 
were made to capture the dialogues and interactions among the parties involved. These 
recordings were subsequently transcribed for analysis. Additionally, the researcher conducted 
fieldwork to collect samples, perform tests, and create analytical memos. 
Willing participants  


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 185 
 
Individuals who had reservations or concerns regarding the potential risks associated with the 
research chose not to participate. The researcher recognized that the absence of refusals to 
participate when invited could constitute a notable threat to the research's validity. 
Plagiarism  
This study is built upon prior research in engineering, technology and innovation management, 
social studies, energy studies, gender equality, etc., conducted by both members of the research 
group and external researchers. The researcher diligently and accurately referenced these 
sources. 
2.6.8 
Assessment of trustworthiness 
The crux of the matter is that any valid concept should contain an element of truth, demonstrate 
how the research study can be practically applied or utilised, and enable others to evaluate the 
consistency of the procedures employed to attain the research findings. The researcher should 
be capable of convincing readers that the results of the research study warrant attention. To 
assess validity or trustworthiness, the following aspects are also taken into consideration and 
documented in the appendix sections: 
i. Truth Value: Do the findings presented accurately reflect the underlying data? (Credibility) 
ii. Consistency: Is there evidence of completeness, comprehensiveness, and coherence in the 
process employed? (Dependability) 
iii. Neutrality: Is there a thorough and coherent audit trail leading to the final interpretations 
and conclusions? (Confirmability) 
iv. Applicability: To what extent can the results be extrapolated to another context or with 
different participants? (Transferability) 
 
2.7 Conclusion 
This chapter has laid the groundwork for this research study by outlining philosophical 
assumptions, paradigms, research design, and methodologies used to address the research 
questions. We provided explanations for the types of data collection methods and detailed 
discussions on trustworthiness and potential threats to validity/trustworthiness. These 
discussions aim to make the researcher aware of research biases and other steps integral to the 
model's explanation and conclusion. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 186 
 
Therefore, the development of gender-inclusive biogas technology, a key innovation resulting 
from this research study, was accomplished through the application of Design Research 
Methodology. This approach was informed by insights gleaned from grounded theory 
techniques, literature reviews, semi-structured interviews, and ethnographic fieldwork 
conducted in two informal settlements. The System Energy Technology Innovation Living Lab 
(SETILL) played a vital role in aiding the design and development of the gender-inclusive 
energy technology innovation (the Biogas digester). The Biogas digester served as a practical 
test case for SETILL, offering users insights into the gender dynamics of energy in informal 
settlements and fostering creative idea generation for gender-inclusive energy technology 
innovations. The evaluation of this co-designed gender-inclusive energy technology innovation 
took place during a one-day, multi-stakeholder co-design workshop held in Tzaneen, South 
Africa. 
Imperatively, the ultimate objective of this project is to establish a Systemic Energy 
Technology Innovation Living Lab in the designated case study areas, specifically in the 
informal settlements of Tzaneen, South Africa, and Umuahia, Nigeria. The subsequent chapter 
delves deeper into the research findings.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 187 
 
3.8   Expected contributions 
This research would be of significance to a broad audience and is anticipated to have 
methodological, theoretical, and practical relevance. The study is expected to contribute to a 
body of knowledge on Living Lab studies, gender inequality and energy studies, technology 
and innovation, developmental policy, and planning, and systems thinking. The study is also 
expected to contribute to the theoretical and empirical understanding of the processes of 
developing gender innovation in the Living Lab context. 
Relating to practical adequacy, the research could also encourage researchers to use Africa as 
a research context, and understand the potentials of generating new theories in energy, 
engineering, gender and management research which could be related to the phenomena being 
studied (George et al., 2016). 
Furthermore, most of the studies on energy-poverty are done in rural context because most of 
Africa is still made of rural areas (Azimoh et al., 2015; Eder, Mutsaerts & Sriwannawit, 2015; 
Pedersen, 2016; UNDP, 2011; Zavratnik, Superina & Duh, 2019). To remedy this gap, this 
study will analyse and contribute to SETI Living Lab in an Informal Settlement context, to 
reduce energy poverty. 
Furthermore, most of the typologies of the Living Labs identified, lack the aspect of gender 
or are gender-neutral, which means they focus on users without incorporating the gender 
aspect. Therefore, this research study would contribute by developing the SETI Living Lab 
typology, which focuses on users incorporating the gender aspect. 
This study is also expected to contribute to the idea of gender inequality/equality, poverty 
alleviation, urban livelihood, and well-being recognised in the UN Agenda 2030 as a 
sustainable development goal. Therefore, the study will conceptualise and contribute to the 
understanding of energy inequality, energy security, and the Living Lab, and to understand 
how the management of the energy technology in Africa is relevant to the phenomena which 
could allow for the generation of new theories. 
The research is expected to provide an increased understanding and reduction of energy 
poverty and energy distribution dynamics and a way of judging the effectiveness of various 
energy intervention strategies. The purpose will be to support the government of South Africa, 
the energy sectors, and the poor urban informal settlements and policymakers in their 
endeavours to slow down energy poverty. 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 188 
 
Finally, the research is expected to develop a systems model or framework for the 
understanding of the behaviours expected of stakeholders influencing energy technology 
innovation in the Living Lab focusing on energy poverty and how women contribute their 
knowledge in the decision-making process of the design of the technologies. The research 
will attempt to cover all these gaps. 
3.9   Limitations of the research study 
There could be few limitations for this research study during data collection and analysis and 
other issues, which few of them are mentioned below: 
I. 
Response bias could arise from the participants in the informal settlement during 
interviews, questionnaires, and focus groups. This means that the participants might 
say what they want to say and not what they mean to say. This kind of threat could be 
reduced using content analysis and coding. 
II. 
There might be difficulty in obtaining some vital information during this study, which 
could have a huge impact on the theory to be generated. 
III. 
The informal settlements might be complicated to monitor because they 
metamorphose quicker than the system which is supposed to be used to monitor them 
(HAD, 2012). 
IV. 
The cost of research resources, like Laptops, internet facilities, and funding might limit 
extensive research relating to energy poverty and gender inequality. 
V. 
The focus would be on urban and energy Living Lab around Europe and Africa. 
Therefore, it can serve as a framework that will allow for further study to be developed. 
This means that the research study might only be conducted in one context. The 
researcher might require that more than one site or context should be used to confirm 
and further develop the theory. The research result could be applied in another context, 
but the audience of the research study are to make judgments of the transferability.

5.  Ethical considerations 
Ethics is based on justifiable standards of right and wrong that specifies what people should do 
mostly in terms of rights, responsibilities, and benefits to society, fairness, or virtues, which 
could be right to life, freedom, injury, and privacy. Such standards are suitable standards of 
ethics because they support dependable and justifiable grounds. Ethics in this research would 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 190 
 
be a study and formulation of one’s ethical standards. It is essential to always check one’s 
standards to ensure that they are reasonable and justifiable. 
In this research, no harm is predicted or expected; therefore, the research will be done ethically, 
and there will be no discrimination against any group or persons. During the data collection 
process, there will be no risk of ethical violations as only available public sources will be used. 
The researcher also will participate in the fieldwork and workshops relating to Living Labs and 
energy, will have meetings with some of the members of the urban poor informal settlement 
and stakeholders in the energy sector (especially women and girls) with granted permission. 
The participants will also be informed before the interview or group discussions, and consent 
forms will be signed by the participants who would allow for the use of audio recording, group 
discussions, and they will be informed that they could stop participating anytime if they are 
uncomfortable. Also, the participants will have the right to choose a comfortable place for the 
interview. 
In summary, this research study will be conducted ethically, and it will be done with the 
University of Pretoria’s ethics committee approval.

7. 
Conclusion 
The Living Lab concept happens to be a new field in the energy or power sector and would 
need a suitable method. The qualitative then quantitative approach that could be suitable for 
this research is proposed for this study. The research work is proposed to be done in a poor 
urban informal settlement in South Africa. The proposed kind of technology is the household 
energy technologies, relating to electricity and cooking technologies. 
The people to be involved will comprise the stakeholders and the end-users (men and women) 
in and around the case study. The development of Living Lab relating to the urban informal 
settlement will continue to be one of the most critical methods of reducing gender inequality 
in the energy sector in South Africa. The SETI Living Lab will support and help to broaden 
the understanding of the gendered energy technology innovations and their interactions with 
the urban informal settlement in Africa. 
In this study, the theoretical models will be developed to answer the research questions and 


=== PAGE BREAK ===

Page | 193 
 
the theoretical model will be sufficiently abstracted that it could be applied to different 
communities, informal settlements, energy industries and energy poverty prone areas around 
South Africa and African environs, and any other situation that requires the development of 
gendered energy technology innovations. The theoretical model will also provide an 
understanding that in focusing on security of energy services, ‘gendered energy technology 
innovations will attempt to reduce energy poverty by focusing on using the model’s or the 
mechanism(s) that make up the model for insights that will contribute to understanding gender 
and energy security. 
The theoretical models to be developed will go through checklists, especially against data to 
indicate that the model explains the real-world outcome and that the model applies to the case 
study. To instill the confidence that the research result would be worth paying attention to or 
worth considering, the data confirmability, dependability, and credibility procedures will be 
addressed, as introduced by Guba et al. (1982), to replace the terms commonly used for 
quantitative research. 
Finally, management, engineering, gender, and energy researchers will have the chance to 
contribute to empirical relevance and provide the energy sector and stakeholders, institutions, 
and policymakers with the right decisions to make.
